Table 3
Recognizing and Refactoring Bad Smells in Code
1. Q: What is a code smell, and what is its purpose in deciding when to refactor?
A: Show answer
A code smell is a recognizable structure in source code that suggests a design problem may be making the software harder to understand or change. It is an indicator, not proof that the code is defective. Examples include a long function, duplicated logic, a misleading name, mutable global data, and a class that has accumulated unrelated responsibilities. The purpose of identifying a smell is to focus attention on a likely source of maintenance difficulty and select a refactoring that may improve it. The chapter deliberately avoids universal numerical thresholds because the significance of a structure depends on its context. A smell should prompt investigation: determine what is difficult, why it matters, and whether a particular refactoring would improve the design without adding unnecessary complexity.
Study instruction:
- For each smell you identify, state the maintenance difficulty it may indicate and the evidence that would justify a refactoring.
Key point:
- A smell is a clue about possible design trouble, not an automatic instruction to change code.
- Refactor when the change improves understanding or makes future modifications safer and cheaper.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
2. Q: Why do Fowler and Beck avoid setting strict numerical thresholds for most code smells?
A: Show answer
They explain that precise criteria, such as an exact maximum number of lines in a function or fields in a class, cannot reliably replace informed human judgment. A method's length is only one part of its complexity; a longer method may be easy to follow if its steps form a coherent sequence, while a short method may hide a confusing responsibility. Similarly, a parameter list may be long for a legitimate reason or may reveal a missing data object. The chapter provides indicators that help developers recognize trouble, but expects them to develop judgment through experience. Metrics can help locate candidates for review, but they cannot decide whether a refactoring is beneficial in context.
Study instruction:
- Compare two functions of different lengths and explain which one is harder to understand based on responsibility, naming, branching, and dependencies rather than line count alone.
Key point:
- Smell detection requires contextual judgment, not mechanical thresholds.
- Metrics can identify review candidates but cannot replace understanding of the code's purpose.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
3. Q: What is the Mysterious Name smell, and why is naming so important?
A: Show answer
Mysterious Name occurs when a function, variable, field, class, or module has a name that makes its purpose or use difficult to understand. Names are one of the main ways source code communicates intent to future developers. A reader should be able to infer what an element represents or does without repeatedly investigating its implementation. A misleading or overly generic name can create confusion about responsibilities and lead a developer to use the element incorrectly. Common remedies include renaming a function, variable, or field. The chapter also notes that difficulty finding a good name can signal a deeper design problem: perhaps the element has multiple responsibilities or represents an unclear concept that should be reorganized before it can be named accurately.
Study instruction:
- Review names in a module and rewrite ambiguous ones so that their purpose, domain meaning, or expected use is clear to a reader unfamiliar with the implementation.
Key point:
- Good names communicate intent and reduce the effort required to understand code.
- Naming difficulty can reveal unclear responsibilities or a deeper design problem.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
4. Q: How can difficulty naming a function or class reveal a design problem rather than merely a vocabulary problem?
A: Show answer
A name is difficult to choose when the responsibility of the element is not clear. A function may perform several unrelated operations, a class may combine multiple concepts, or a variable may represent different kinds of information at different times. In such cases, repeatedly trying synonyms will not solve the underlying issue. The developer should examine what the element actually does and consider extracting separate responsibilities, moving behavior, splitting data, or redefining the boundary. Once the design has a coherent concept, a precise name is usually easier to find. Renaming is therefore both a refactoring technique and a diagnostic activity that can expose hidden complexity.
Study instruction:
- When a name is difficult to settle, list all the responsibilities or meanings attached to the element and decide whether they belong together.
Key point:
- Unclear names may reflect unclear concepts or mixed responsibilities.
- Improve the design before forcing a name onto an incoherent element.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
5. Q: What is the Duplicated Code smell, and how does it increase maintenance risk?
A: Show answer
Duplicated Code occurs when the same or substantially similar logic appears in more than one place. Each copy must be understood when a developer reads the program and must be found and updated whenever the shared rule changes. A missed copy can cause inconsistent behavior or allow one part of the system to retain an old rule. The chapter recommends unifying genuine duplicates, often by extracting a function and invoking it from each location. Similar but non-identical fragments may first need to be rearranged so their common parts are visible. Duplication should not be removed mechanically when two similar sections represent genuinely different domain rules; the goal is to centralize repeated knowledge, not create an abstraction that obscures meaningful differences.
Study instruction:
- Locate repeated code and determine whether the copies express one shared rule or distinct rules that only happen to look similar.
Key point:
- Duplication multiplies the locations that must change together.
- Consolidate true shared behavior while keeping distinct domain responsibilities separate.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
6. Q: How should duplicated code be handled when it appears in two methods of the same class?
A: Show answer
When two methods in the same class contain the same coherent expression or block, the developer can extract that block into a method and call it from both locations. This creates one source of truth for the shared behavior. The extracted method should have a name that describes the domain purpose of the code and an interface that reflects its actual inputs. If the blocks are only similar, the developer may use statement movement or other small transformations to align the common parts before extracting. Tests should verify both original call paths because the extracted function may be used under different conditions even though the calculations are shared.
Study instruction:
- Select duplicated fragments in one class, extract their common behavior, and test each original path that now uses the shared function.
Key point:
- A shared method prevents two copies of the same rule from drifting apart.
- Preserve the distinct behavior of each caller when consolidating the common part.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog: Extract Function
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
7. Q: What is the Long Function smell, and why do short, well-named functions often improve design?
A: Show answer
A Long Function is a routine whose size or internal complexity makes its purpose and sequence difficult to understand. The chapter favors short functions because they allow developers to see the program as a set of meaningful steps. Small functions provide explanation through names, allow behavior to be shared, and give developers choices about where responsibilities should live. The issue is not a strict line-count limit; the important factor is the distance between what the function does and how its implementation expresses it. A function may be long yet understandable, but a long function often accumulates mixed responsibilities, temporary variables, conditionals, and repeated logic that make safe change difficult.
Study instruction:
- Identify a function with several distinct activities and describe each activity in a sentence before deciding where meaningful function boundaries should be placed.
Key point:
- Function length is a clue; semantic clarity is the real concern.
- Well-named functions let readers understand the workflow without inspecting every implementation detail.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog: Extract Function
8. Q: How can comments help developers identify parts of a long function that should be extracted?
A: Show answer
A comment that explains what a block of code does often indicates that the block has a meaningful purpose that is not yet represented by the code's structure. The developer can replace the commented block with a function whose name states the intention, then remove the comment if the name makes the explanation unnecessary. This works even when the block is only one line, provided a function name adds meaningful context. The aim is not to eliminate all comments; it is to avoid using comments to compensate for unclear implementation. Comments that explain why a decision was made or record a constraint may remain valuable after the code has been clarified.
Study instruction:
- Review comments inside long routines and determine whether each describes an extractable responsibility, a rationale, a constraint, or an unresolved question.
Key point:
- Comments describing what code does may reveal an opportunity for Extract Function.
- Retain comments that explain why a non-obvious decision exists or record important context.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog: Extract Function
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
9. Q: What should a developer do when a long function contains many temporary variables and parameters that make extraction difficult?
A: Show answer
The developer should first reduce unnecessary local state and clarify the data flow before attempting a large extraction. Temporaries that can be derived from existing values may be replaced by queries. Long parameter lists may be reduced by introducing a parameter object, passing a whole existing data structure, or moving related behavior and data into a class. If the function still has too many interacting variables and parameters, a command object may provide a new home for the operation's state and behavior. The chapter recommends escalating to more substantial transformations only after simpler opportunities have been considered. Each change should be performed in small steps and supported by tests so that the extraction does not merely move complexity into a new location.
Study instruction:
- Before extracting a difficult block, list its inputs, outputs, and temporary variables, then decide which can be queried, grouped, or encapsulated.
Key point:
- Excessive parameters and temporaries can make extraction unhelpful.
- Simplify dependencies and state before choosing a larger structural transformation.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog: Extract Function
- Refactoring Catalog
10. Q: How should conditionals and loops be treated when they contribute to a Long Function smell?
A: Show answer
Conditional logic can often be decomposed into named predicates or functions so that the caller expresses the decision in domain terms. Large switch statements may have each branch extracted into a function, and repeated switches on the same type condition may indicate a need for polymorphism. Loops can also be extracted as a meaningful operation; if the loop performs more than one distinct task, splitting it into separate loops may create clearer responsibilities. The goal is not to remove every conditional or loop, but to make the purpose of each decision and iteration visible. A function becomes easier to understand when the high-level flow reads as a sequence of named activities rather than a dense block of implementation details.
Study instruction:
- Examine a long function's branches and loops and identify which express distinct business decisions or independent tasks that could be named or separated.
Key point:
- Extract meaningful decisions and iteration responsibilities rather than merely shortening the code.
- Repeated switches on the same condition may indicate a broader design opportunity.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
11. Q: What is the Long Parameter List smell?
A: Show answer
A Long Parameter List occurs when a function requires many separate arguments, making the call difficult to read, construct, and maintain. Callers must remember the meaning and ordering of multiple values, and changes to the operation may require updating many signatures and call sites. The problem can indicate that some arguments are derivable from another parameter, that several values form one domain concept, or that multiple functions share the same contextual data. The chapter suggests obtaining related values through a query, passing a whole object rather than extracting many individual fields, introducing a parameter object for values that travel together, removing flag arguments, or collecting shared data in a class. The correct solution depends on the semantics of the parameters, not only their count.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a function signature and group its arguments by source, meaning, and usage; identify whether the list reveals a missing object or unnecessary dependencies.
Key point:
- Long parameter lists increase cognitive load at every call site.
- Group related data or remove derivable arguments when that clarifies the interface.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
12. Q: When should a developer use Introduce Parameter Object to address a Long Parameter List?
A: Show answer
Introduce Parameter Object is appropriate when several parameters repeatedly travel together and represent a coherent group of information. Instead of passing each value separately, the caller passes one object that expresses the shared concept. This simplifies the function signature and can centralize validation or behavior associated with the grouped data. The parameters should have a meaningful relationship rather than merely being grouped because there are too many of them. If only one function uses a set of values and they do not form a domain concept, introducing an object may create unnecessary structure. The developer should examine repeated co-occurrence across function signatures and the likely behavior that belongs with the resulting object.
Study instruction:
- Find parameter groups repeated across multiple functions and determine whether they represent a domain concept that deserves its own object.
Key point:
- Parameter objects clarify groups of related values and simplify function calls.
- Do not bundle unrelated values simply to shorten a signature.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
13. Q: What is the Global Data smell, and why is it especially dangerous when the data is mutable?
A: Show answer
Global Data is state that can be accessed or changed from many parts of a program without a clear record of who modified it. It may take the form of global variables, class variables, or singletons. When a defect appears, developers may struggle to determine which component changed the value and why. Mutable global data is particularly risky because changes can affect distant code through hidden dependencies. The chapter recommends encapsulating the variable so that reads and updates pass through a controlled interface, then reducing its scope by placing it inside an appropriate class or module. Global data that is immutable after initialization is less dangerous, provided the language and design reliably enforce that guarantee.
Study instruction:
- Trace a global variable's readers and writers, identify hidden dependencies, and propose a controlled interface that narrows who can modify it.
Key point:
- Unrestricted global state makes data changes difficult to trace.
- Encapsulation and narrower scope reduce the number of places that can affect the value.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
14. Q: Is all global data equally harmful?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter distinguishes mutable global data from values that can be guaranteed never to change after program initialization. Immutable global configuration or constants are generally safer because distant code cannot alter their values and create hidden interactions. Mutable state is much more difficult to reason about because every write can affect behavior elsewhere. However, even immutable global data can create coupling if it represents a poorly defined dependency or makes tests difficult to isolate. Encapsulation remains useful because it makes access explicit and provides a place to enforce invariants. The risk increases with the amount of global state and the number of places that can modify it; the relevant question is how much hidden influence the data has on the rest of the program.
Study instruction:
- Classify global values as immutable constants, initialization-only configuration, or mutable runtime state and document how each category is protected.
Key point:
- Mutable global state poses the greatest risk of uncontrolled side effects.
- Enforced immutability reduces that risk but does not eliminate coupling concerns.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
15. Q: What is the Mutable Data smell?
A: Show answer
Mutable Data occurs when values or structures can be changed in ways that create unexpected effects elsewhere in the program. A component may update a record without realizing that another component depends on its previous state. Such failures can be difficult to reproduce when they occur only under particular sequences or conditions. The chapter draws on functional programming's preference for immutable data, where an update produces a new value rather than modifying the original. Even in languages that permit mutation, developers can reduce risk by encapsulating updates, limiting scope, separating queries from modifiers, and using immutable or replacement-style transformations where practical. The problem is especially serious when data is shared widely or changed for several unrelated purposes.
Study instruction:
- Trace the life cycle of a shared mutable object and identify every location where it can be changed and every consumer that depends on its state.
Key point:
- Mutation creates temporal dependencies between parts of a program.
- Limit who can change shared data and make updates explicit.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
16. Q: How can a developer reduce the risk of mutable data without adopting a purely functional language?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends controlling mutation rather than assuming every application can or should prohibit it completely. Encapsulate variables so that updates occur through narrow functions; split a variable when it is reused to hold different kinds of information; move side-effect-free calculations away from code that performs updates; separate queries from modifiers; and remove setters when callers should not be able to change a value. A derived value that can be calculated from other data should often be represented by a query instead of stored mutable state. The goal is to make updates explicit, local, and easier to reason about. Mutation within a small scope may be harmless, but risk grows when many components can change the same data.
Study instruction:
- Choose a mutable field and design a sequence of controls that limits writes, separates calculations from updates, and removes unnecessary setters.
Key point:
- Mutation can be made safer by narrowing its access and separating side effects.
- Replace stored derived state with a query when no independent value needs to be maintained.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
17. Q: What is the difference between Divergent Change and Shotgun Surgery?
A: Show answer
Divergent Change occurs when one module is changed for several unrelated reasons. For example, the same class may need edits whenever database access changes and also whenever financial-product rules change. The module contains separate contexts that should likely be separated. Shotgun Surgery is the opposite pattern: one particular change requires many small edits across many different modules. The logic for a single responsibility has been scattered rather than localized. Divergent Change asks why one module has several reasons to change; Shotgun Surgery asks why one reason to change requires touching so many modules. Both indicate that the current module boundaries do not align well with the system's patterns of change, but they call for different reorganizations.
Study instruction:
- For recent changes, record whether each change affected many responsibilities within one module or one responsibility across many modules; classify the pattern accordingly.
Key point:
- Divergent Change scatters unrelated reasons within one module.
- Shotgun Surgery scatters one reason for change across many modules.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
18. Q: What is the Divergent Change smell, and how can it be corrected?
A: Show answer
Divergent Change occurs when a module is repeatedly modified for different and unrelated concerns. A class may contain database access functions and financial calculations, so a change to either area requires working inside the same module. The design should separate the different contexts so that each type of change has a clearer home. If the two processes form a sequence, Split Phase can create a data structure between them. If they interact through several calls, Move Function and related module or class extractions can separate responsibilities. Where a function mixes the contexts, Extract Function may be needed before moving behavior. The objective is to align module boundaries with distinct reasons for change.
Study instruction:
- Review a module's change history and group edits by business reason; identify responsibilities that should be isolated into separate modules or classes.
Key point:
- One module should not accumulate several unrelated change responsibilities.
- Split sequential or interacting concerns according to their data and dependency relationships.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog: Split Phase
- Refactoring Catalog
19. Q: What is Shotgun Surgery, and what design problem does it reveal?
A: Show answer
Shotgun Surgery occurs when a single change requires many small edits across multiple classes or modules. Because the relevant logic is scattered, developers must find every affected location and risk missing one. The chapter recommends bringing related behavior and data closer together through moving functions or fields, combining functions that operate on the same data into a class or transformation, or separating phases so common calculations occur in one place. In some cases, temporarily inlining poorly separated functions or classes can bring related logic together before extracting a better structure. The design problem is not simply that many files are touched; a broad change may legitimately affect several components. The smell is that one responsibility has been fragmented in a way that makes every change difficult to complete consistently.
Study instruction:
- Trace one recent change through all the files it touched and determine whether the edits reflect legitimate component boundaries or dispersed fragments of one responsibility.
Key point:
- Shotgun Surgery makes a single change require scattered edits.
- Reorganize behavior around the concepts that change together.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
20. Q: What is Feature Envy, and how can a developer recognize it?
A: Show answer
Feature Envy occurs when a function in one module spends more time using the data or operations of another module than it does using the features of its own module. A common sign is a function that calls many getters on another object to calculate a value that logically belongs with that object's data. The developer should examine which module owns most of the information required by the behavior and whether the behavior changes with that data. Often, moving the function to the data-owning module improves cohesion and reduces cross-module communication. Sometimes only a portion of the function is envious; that portion can be extracted and moved independently. The decision should account for behavior that legitimately coordinates multiple objects or represents a separate policy.
Study instruction:
- Inspect functions that repeatedly access another object's fields or getters and assess where the calculation's data and responsibility naturally belong.
Key point:
- Behavior often belongs near the data it uses most heavily.
- Extract and move only the envious portion when the full function has multiple responsibilities.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
21. Q: Should every function that accesses another object's data be moved into that object's class?
A: Show answer
No. Feature Envy is a clue, not an absolute rule. Some functions coordinate behavior across several modules, implement a policy that properly belongs elsewhere, or intentionally use patterns such as Strategy or Visitor to vary a small part of behavior. The chapter's general heuristic is to place behavior with the module that owns most of the data it uses, but it recognizes that there are legitimate exceptions. Developers should identify the reasons for the current placement, assess which module is expected to change with the behavior, and consider whether moving it would reduce or increase coupling. Moving a function simply to eliminate getter calls may create an inappropriate dependency or violate a useful architectural boundary.
Study instruction:
- For a suspected Feature Envy case, compare the data used by the function, the responsibility it implements, and the module most likely to change when that responsibility evolves.
Key point:
- Proximity to data is a useful heuristic, not an inflexible placement rule.
- Preserve deliberate patterns and boundaries that isolate legitimate variation.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
22. Q: What is Data Clumps, and how can repeated groups of values indicate a missing object?
A: Show answer
Data Clumps occur when the same small group of values repeatedly appears together, such as the same fields in multiple classes or the same arguments in several function signatures. Their repeated association suggests they represent a meaningful concept that lacks an explicit home. The chapter recommends extracting a class from a group of related fields, then using a parameter object or preserving the whole object to simplify function signatures. A useful diagnostic question is whether the values still make sense independently: if removing one value would make the others meaningless or incomplete, they may belong to a single domain object. The new class may later acquire behavior that currently sits in the functions manipulating those fields.
Study instruction:
- Identify recurring clusters of fields and parameters, then test whether they represent one coherent domain concept with a natural lifecycle or behavior.
Key point:
- Repeatedly co-occurring data may indicate a missing domain object.
- Group values by meaning, not merely because they appear together in one call.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
23. Q: How should a developer decide whether a data clump deserves a class?
A: Show answer
The developer should look for repeated co-occurrence, shared meaning, and a natural set of invariants or behaviors. The chapter proposes asking whether the remaining values would still make sense if one of the values were removed. If the group becomes incoherent, this suggests the values form a single concept. Common prefixes or suffixes in field names can also reveal a cluster. A class can then own the fields and may eventually acquire functions that currently operate on them from other modules. However, not every group of values needs a class: a temporary collection used once or a simple result record may be better represented as a plain data structure. The object should improve domain meaning and cohesion rather than introduce indirection without purpose.
Study instruction:
- For a repeated field cluster, test the values for semantic interdependence and identify the responsibilities or validations that naturally belong with them.
Key point:
- A class is justified when the data group represents a coherent concept.
- Avoid turning every temporary parameter bundle into a permanent domain object.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
24. Q: What is Primitive Obsession?
A: Show answer
Primitive Obsession occurs when a program represents domain concepts using generic primitive types even though those concepts have their own rules, operations, or constraints. Examples include representing money as an ordinary number, coordinates as unrelated numeric values, ranges as repeated comparisons, or telephone numbers as arbitrary strings. Such representations spread validation and formatting logic throughout the code and make invalid combinations easier to create. Replacing a primitive with a domain object can centralize the relevant invariants and operations. If a primitive value is acting as a type code that selects conditional behavior, replacing the type code with subclasses and polymorphism may be appropriate. The objective is to model meaningful domain distinctions explicitly rather than create a class for every primitive value.
Study instruction:
- Identify primitive values that carry domain rules and decide whether a dedicated type would centralize validation, behavior, or formatting.
Key point:
- Generic types can hide meaningful domain concepts and their constraints.
- Introduce domain types where they make invalid states harder to represent and behavior easier to locate.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
25. Q: Why are money, measurements, ranges, and telephone numbers examples of primitive obsession?
A: Show answer
These concepts look like ordinary numbers or strings but carry rules that generic primitives do not express. Money has currency, precision, rounding, and arithmetic constraints. Measurements have units, so adding inches to millimeters without conversion is invalid. A range has boundaries and operations such as containment or emptiness. A telephone number may require normalization, validation, and consistent display rather than arbitrary character manipulation. When the application uses primitives directly, every caller must remember and correctly implement these rules. A domain type can centralize behavior and make the meaning of a value explicit. The type should be introduced where it prevents meaningful errors or simplifies repeated operations, not just for theoretical purity.
Study instruction:
- Select a domain value currently stored as a number or string and list the validation, arithmetic, and display rules that a dedicated type could own.
Key point:
- Domain-specific types encode rules that primitives cannot express.
- Explicit units and concepts reduce invalid operations and scattered validation.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
26. Q: What does “stringly typed” mean, and why can it be a problem?
A: Show answer
“Stringly typed” describes a design in which strings are used to represent values that have richer domain meaning, such as telephone numbers, identifiers, status categories, or codes. Strings permit many values that may not be valid for the concept and encourage repeated parsing, comparison, validation, and formatting logic. A dedicated type can make valid operations explicit and centralize rules. For example, a telephone number type can normalize input and provide consistent display behavior. A string may still be appropriate for free-form text or an external identifier whose semantics are intentionally opaque. The smell appears when code repeatedly treats an ordinary string as though it were a structured domain object.
Study instruction:
- Locate string fields that trigger repeated validation or formatting logic and assess whether a dedicated value type would provide a clearer contract.
Key point:
- Strings are weak representations for concepts with structured rules.
- Use a domain type when its validation and behavior are meaningful and repeated.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
27. Q: Why does Chapter 3 focus on Repeated Switches rather than treating every switch statement as a smell?
A: Show answer
A single switch statement is not automatically a design problem. Modern languages provide expressive conditional forms, and a switch may be the clearest way to handle a small number of alternatives. The stronger smell occurs when the same conditional logic on the same type appears in multiple places. Every time a new type is added, developers must find and update all the repeated switches, increasing the risk that one will be missed. Polymorphism can centralize the variant behavior behind a common operation so that each type supplies its own implementation. The important signal is duplicated type-dependent behavior, not the mere presence of conditional syntax.
Study instruction:
- Search for repeated switch or if/else chains on the same type and assess whether they represent duplicated variant behavior.
Key point:
- Conditional logic is not inherently bad; repeated switching on the same type is the stronger smell.
- Polymorphism can centralize behavior that varies systematically by category.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
28. Q: How can repeated switches create defects when a new category is introduced?
A: Show answer
If the same category decision is repeated in several functions, adding a new category requires every relevant conditional to be updated. A developer may update the pricing switch but forget the credit calculation, report formatting, or validation switch. The application can then behave inconsistently for the new category. Polymorphism or another centralized dispatch strategy can reduce the number of locations that contain category-specific logic. The factory or dispatch point still needs to recognize the new category, and tests must verify all affected behavior. The aim is to localize variation so a new category has a predictable implementation point rather than requiring a scattered search through the code base.
Study instruction:
- Simulate adding a new category to a system with repeated switches and count every location that must be changed and tested.
Key point:
- Repeated switches multiply the risk of incomplete category support.
- Centralize type-dependent behavior and test the new category across the full workflow.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
29. Q: Why does Chapter 3 question the need for traditional loops in modern programming languages?
A: Show answer
Loops remain valid programming constructs, but many languages now provide first-class functions and collection operations that express data processing more declaratively. Operations such as map and filter can show which elements are transformed or selected without requiring a reader to trace loop counters and accumulator updates. Replace Loop with Pipeline can therefore make the intention of a transformation easier to see. The chapter is not claiming that all loops should be eliminated. A loop may be clearer when control flow is complex, when early exits or side effects are central, or when the language's pipeline abstractions make the code less readable. The decision should favor clarity and suitability to the operation, not style conformity.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a simple filtering or transformation loop as a pipeline and compare which version communicates the operation more clearly to your team.
Key point:
- Collection pipelines can make transformation intent more visible.
- Retain loops when they express complex control flow more clearly than a pipeline.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
30. Q: What is the Lazy Element smell?
A: Show answer
A Lazy Element is a program element whose structural cost is greater than the value it provides. It may be a function whose name merely repeats what its body already says, a class that contains only one trivial operation, or an inheritance layer that no longer contributes useful behavior. Such elements may have been created in anticipation of future growth or may have lost their original responsibilities after earlier refactoring. The chapter suggests inlining an unnecessary function or class, or collapsing an inheritance hierarchy when the extra structure no longer serves a purpose. The important distinction is between useful indirection that supports clarity, variation, or reuse and unnecessary indirection that makes a reader navigate more code without gaining meaning.
Study instruction:
- Review small functions, wrapper classes, and inheritance layers and identify whether each provides a meaningful name, boundary, variation point, or independent responsibility.
Key point:
- Structure is useful only when it contributes clarity, reuse, or flexibility that is actually needed.
- Remove indirection that adds navigation cost without a corresponding benefit.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
31. Q: What is Speculative Generality, and why can it make software harder to maintain?
A: Show answer
Speculative Generality occurs when developers add abstractions, hooks, special cases, parameters, or inheritance structures to support possible future needs that have not materialized. The machinery increases the number of concepts that maintainers must understand, even though current callers may not use it. It can also encode incorrect assumptions about how future variation will occur. If a feature is not needed, the complexity is a cost without a corresponding benefit. The chapter suggests removing unused abstractions, unnecessary delegation, unused parameters, and dead code that exists only for anticipated reuse. This is related to YAGNI: generalization should be justified by actual requirements rather than hypothetical possibilities.
Study instruction:
- Examine abstractions and parameters used only by tests or not used by real callers, and determine whether they support a current requirement or only a speculative one.
Key point:
- Unused flexibility adds complexity without delivering current value.
- Generalize when real variation demonstrates the need, not merely because it might occur someday.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
32. Q: How can tests reveal Speculative Generality in a code base?
A: Show answer
A function or class whose only callers are test cases may indicate that the production design has been generalized for a use that does not exist. The tests may be exercising an imagined extension point rather than protecting actual application behavior. Developers should examine why the abstraction was created, whether a real consumer exists, and whether the test itself encodes an obsolete design assumption. If the element is genuinely unused, it may be appropriate to remove the dead code and revise tests that only preserve the unnecessary structure. This does not mean that test-only helper classes or test fixtures are inherently speculative; the concern is production machinery that has no meaningful production use and exists only because a future requirement was imagined.
Study instruction:
- Search for production classes or functions whose only references are in tests and verify whether they represent test support or unused production generality.
Key point:
- Test-only usage can be a clue that production abstractions have no real consumer.
- Preserve useful test fixtures while removing unused production machinery.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
33. Q: What is the Temporary Field smell?
A: Show answer
Temporary Field occurs when an object has a field that is populated or used only under particular circumstances. A developer reading the class may reasonably expect every field to be relevant to the object's normal state, but a temporary field may be meaningful only during one algorithm or special case. This makes it difficult to understand when the value is valid and which methods depend on it. The chapter suggests extracting a class to give the field and its related behavior a more appropriate home, moving the functions that use it, or introducing a special-case class for objects in which the field is not valid. The key concern is unclear object state and scattered conditional logic around whether a field has been initialized.
Study instruction:
- Identify fields that are set only by particular workflows and document the valid states, consumers, and conditions under which the field exists.
Key point:
- Temporary fields make object state difficult to understand.
- Group the special-purpose data with the behavior or state that legitimately uses it.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
34. Q: What is the Message Chains smell, and why can chained navigation create coupling?
A: Show answer
Message Chains occur when a client asks one object for another object, then asks that object for a further object, and continues navigating through several intermediate relationships. The client becomes dependent on the internal structure of the object graph. If an intermediate relationship changes, the client may need to be modified even though the final task it performs has not changed. The chapter suggests hiding delegates at an appropriate point or moving the behavior that uses the resulting object closer to the data. It warns against mechanically hiding every link, because that can turn every intermediate object into a middle man. The goal is to reduce unnecessary knowledge of internal navigation while keeping responsibilities coherent.
Study instruction:
- Find long getter or property chains and identify which relationship changes would force the calling code to change.
Key point:
- Long navigation chains expose callers to internal object structure.
- Hide or move behavior where doing so reduces coupling without creating excessive delegation.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
35. Q: How is Middle Man different from Message Chains?
A: Show answer
Message Chains occur when a caller navigates through several objects to reach information or behavior, exposing internal relationships. Middle Man occurs when an object provides many methods that simply delegate requests to another object, adding an unnecessary layer between the caller and the object that performs the work. Hiding a delegate can reduce a chain, but doing so excessively can create a middle man whose only purpose is forwarding calls. The chapter recommends removing unnecessary delegation when the intermediary no longer provides meaningful encapsulation or behavior. The design balance is to hide details that should remain private while avoiding a class that merely repeats another object's interface.
Study instruction:
- Inspect an object interface and classify its methods as meaningful behavior, intentional encapsulation, or simple forwarding; separately identify clients that navigate long chains.
Key point:
- Message Chains expose too much navigation; Middle Man hides too much through empty forwarding.
- Good encapsulation balances access to needed behavior with minimal indirection.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
36. Q: When is delegation useful, and when does it become the Middle Man smell?
A: Show answer
Delegation is useful when an object hides its implementation details and provides a stable interface to the rest of the system. A client may ask a scheduling object whether a manager is available without needing to know whether availability is stored in a diary, database, or another service. Delegation becomes a smell when a class's interface mainly forwards calls to another object and contributes little encapsulation, policy, or coordination. In that case, callers may be better served by accessing the object that actually owns the behavior. If only a few methods are trivial, they may be inlined; if the whole intermediary is unnecessary, the class relationship may be reorganized. The decision should be based on whether the intermediary protects a meaningful boundary.
Study instruction:
- For each delegating method, write down the implementation detail it hides and decide whether that protection remains valuable to callers.
Key point:
- Delegation supports encapsulation when it hides a useful implementation boundary.
- Remove forwarding that adds no meaningful behavior or protection.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
37. Q: What is the Insider Trading smell between modules?
A: Show answer
Insider Trading occurs when modules share too much internal information or depend on one another's implementation details. Excessive data exchange and close knowledge of internal structures increase coupling, making a change to one module more likely to require changes elsewhere. The chapter suggests moving functions and fields to reduce unnecessary communication, creating a third module for genuinely shared concerns, or using a delegate to mediate interaction. Inheritance can also lead to excessive knowledge between subclasses and superclasses. The objective is not to eliminate all communication—modules must collaborate—but to keep dependencies explicit and limited to what the interface requires. Modules should share purposeful abstractions rather than continually exposing private data and structure to one another.
Study instruction:
- Map the data and calls exchanged between two highly connected modules and identify information that could be encapsulated or moved into a shared abstraction.
Key point:
- Excessive internal knowledge creates coupling and spreads the impact of changes.
- Use clear boundaries and purposeful shared abstractions to regulate module interaction.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
38. Q: What is the Large Class smell, and what clues indicate that a class is doing too much?
A: Show answer
A Large Class accumulates too many fields, methods, or responsibilities. It may contain several groups of fields that belong together, repeated blocks of code, or multiple sets of operations that clients use independently. The class becomes difficult to understand because it represents too many concepts and gives developers many unrelated reasons to modify it. Clues include clusters of field names with shared prefixes or suffixes, methods that use only particular subsets of state, and clients that call only distinct subsets of the class's interface. The chapter recommends extracting coherent groups into separate classes or using inheritance where a genuine common structure and variation exist. The goal is to create cohesive components with clear responsibilities, not simply to reduce the number of lines or fields in one class.
Study instruction:
- Group a large class's fields and methods by the data they use and the clients that call them, then identify candidate responsibilities for extraction.
Key point:
- Large classes often contain several responsibilities that change independently.
- Use field clusters, method usage, and client behavior to find meaningful new boundaries.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
39. Q: How can a developer identify which responsibilities should be extracted from a Large Class?
A: Show answer
The developer should look for groups of fields that are used together, common prefixes or suffixes that indicate a domain concept, and methods that operate on only one subset of the class's data. Clients provide another important clue: if different clients use distinct subsets of the class's interface, those subsets may represent separate responsibilities. Similar methods may also share substantial logic that can be reorganized into smaller cohesive operations. The new class should own a coherent concept and, where appropriate, the behavior that manipulates its data. Inheritance may be appropriate when the structure represents a meaningful specialization, but extraction into a separate class is often simpler when the responsibilities are independent rather than variants of one abstraction.
Study instruction:
- Create a responsibility map for a large class, showing field clusters, methods that use them, and client groups that depend on each capability.
Key point:
- Data usage and client behavior reveal natural boundaries.
- Extract cohesive responsibilities rather than splitting a class arbitrarily by size.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
40. Q: What is Alternative Classes with Different Interfaces?
A: Show answer
This smell occurs when two classes are intended to be interchangeable for a similar role but expose different interfaces, preventing callers from using them through a consistent protocol. The chapter treats substitution as an important benefit of classes: one implementation should be able to replace another when it provides the same expected operations. The remedy is to align the functions and move behavior until the interfaces match. If the resulting classes contain common behavior, a shared superclass or another abstraction may be useful. The goal is not to make unrelated classes identical; it is to create a common interface where the system genuinely needs the alternatives to be interchangeable. Consistent interfaces simplify client code and reduce special-case handling.
Study instruction:
- Compare two classes that serve the same role and list the operations, parameter meanings, return values, and error behavior that must be aligned for safe substitution.
Key point:
- Interchangeable implementations need compatible interfaces and behavior.
- Interface alignment should reflect a real substitution requirement, not superficial similarity.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
41. Q: What is the Data Class smell?
A: Show answer
A Data Class is a class that mainly contains fields and accessors, while other classes perform the behavior associated with that data. The class may be a passive structure whose state is manipulated in detail by many clients. This can indicate that behavior has been placed in the wrong module and that the data class should own some of the operations that use its fields. The chapter suggests encapsulating public records, removing setters for fields that should not be changed, and moving behavior from clients into the data class where appropriate. It also recognizes an important exception: an immutable result record produced by a distinct calculation phase can be a good design, as in the intermediate statement data structure from Chapter 1. The smell is therefore about behavior misplaced around a mutable data holder, not the mere existence of a data-transfer structure.
Study instruction:
- Examine a data-only class and trace which clients manipulate its fields; distinguish misplaced business behavior from a legitimate immutable result record.
Key point:
- Data-only classes can indicate that behavior belongs closer to the data.
- Immutable result records are a legitimate exception when they represent output from a distinct operation.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
42. Q: Why are immutable result records an exception to the Data Class smell?
A: Show answer
An immutable result record can serve as a deliberate boundary between one operation and the code that consumes its results. In the refactoring example from Chapter 1, statement-data preparation returns a structure containing calculated values for a renderer. The record is intended to carry data from a distinct calculation phase to a presentation phase, not to own a set of mutable business behaviors. Because its fields are effectively immutable, callers do not need setter methods to protect against uncontrolled updates, and derived information can be represented directly in the result. This is different from a mutable data holder whose state is manipulated in detail by many clients. The decision depends on the record's purpose, mutability, and role in the architecture.
Study instruction:
- Compare a mutable entity object with an immutable report or query result and explain why behavior may belong in different places in each design.
Key point:
- A data-only result object can be appropriate when it forms a clear, immutable boundary.
- Evaluate the role of the data structure before treating it as a design smell.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
43. Q: What is Refused Bequest, and why does the chapter treat it as a relatively weak smell in many cases?
A: Show answer
Refused Bequest occurs when a subclass inherits methods or fields from a parent but uses only a small portion of what it receives. The traditional response is to move unused behavior into a sibling class or push methods and fields down to the subclasses that need them. Fowler and Beck caution that code reuse through inheritance is not always harmful, even when a subclass does not use every inherited implementation. The smell becomes stronger when the subclass refuses to support the superclass's interface, because callers may reasonably expect it to honor the inherited contract. If the mismatch causes confusion or violates substitutability, delegation may be preferable to inheritance. The design should be changed when the inheritance relationship creates a real problem, not merely because some inherited code is unused.
Study instruction:
- Review a subclass that uses only part of its inherited behavior and determine whether the unused implementation is harmless reuse or an interface-contract problem.
Key point:
- Unused inherited implementation is not automatically a serious design defect.
- Refusing the superclass interface is a stronger warning and may justify delegation.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
44. Q: What is the difference between refusing inherited implementation and refusing an inherited interface?
A: Show answer
Refusing implementation means a subclass does not need or use some of the code it inherits, but it may still support the interface and behave correctly when used as the parent type. This can be a tolerable consequence of reusing a useful part of a superclass. Refusing the interface means the subclass cannot meaningfully honor some operations or expectations promised by the parent. This is more serious because callers may depend on the parent contract and receive surprising or invalid behavior from the subclass. In that situation, the inheritance relationship may be wrong, and replacing inheritance with delegation can provide a clearer model. The key test is behavioral substitutability, not simply how many inherited methods the subclass calls internally.
Study instruction:
- For a subclass, list inherited operations and test whether each can be honored according to the superclass contract, even when its implementation is not reused.
Key point:
- Not using inherited code is less serious than being unable to honor an inherited contract.
- Delegation may be appropriate when the subclass is not a valid behavioral substitute.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
45. Q: Are comments a code smell in themselves?
A: Show answer
No. Chapter 3 explicitly says comments are not inherently a bad smell and can be valuable. They are discussed because comments are sometimes used as a deodorant for confusing code: a lengthy explanation may be compensating for unclear names, an oversized function, or complicated logic. When a comment explains what a block does, the developer should first consider making the code express that intention through a named function or clearer declaration. After the structure is improved, the comment may become unnecessary. Comments that explain why a decision was made, record a constraint, describe an unresolved issue, or identify an area of uncertainty can remain useful to future maintainers.
Study instruction:
- Classify comments in a module as explanation of what, rationale for why, constraint, uncertainty, or outdated commentary; refactor only those that replace clarity in the code.
Key point:
- Comments can be helpful documentation and are not automatically a smell.
- Use clear code to explain what happens and comments to preserve context that code alone cannot express.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
46. Q: When should a developer retain a comment rather than remove it through refactoring?
A: Show answer
A comment should be retained when it communicates information that is not obvious from the code itself, especially the reason for a non-obvious decision, a constraint imposed by the environment, or uncertainty that future developers should investigate. A comment can also record why an apparently unusual solution is necessary, such as a workaround for a known external limitation. If a comment only describes the steps that a block of code performs, a meaningful function or variable name may explain that more reliably. Comments should be maintained as the code changes; stale comments are particularly harmful because they direct readers toward incorrect assumptions. The objective is not fewer comments, but more accurate and useful communication.
Study instruction:
- For each retained comment, ask whether it explains why, a constraint, or uncertainty that cannot be made clear through the code structure alone.
Key point:
- Preserve rationale and constraints that the implementation does not reveal.
- Remove or update comments that merely repeat or contradict the code.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
47. Q: How should a developer respond when a comment explains a complex conditional or a block of business logic?
A: Show answer
The developer should first determine whether the comment describes the intent of the code and whether the code can be restructured to communicate that intent directly. A complex conditional may be decomposed into named predicate functions, and a block of business logic may be extracted into a function named after the responsibility. If a function already exists but its name does not explain its purpose, renaming it may remove the need for explanatory commentary. If the comment describes a business rule or constraint that is not obvious from the expression, it may still be valuable as supporting rationale. Tests should protect the behavior while the code is reorganized so that removing the comment does not conceal an accidental change.
Study instruction:
- Select a comment-heavy conditional and express its intent through named predicates or extracted functions before deciding whether the comment still adds useful context.
Key point:
- Let structure and names explain what the code does.
- Retain commentary that explains important rationale or constraints not represented in the implementation.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog: Extract Function
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
48. Q: How should a developer prioritize several code smells found in the same module?
A: Show answer
Prioritize the smells according to the maintenance difficulty and risk they create, rather than trying to eliminate every smell at once. Consider how frequently the code changes, the cost of understanding it, the number of modules affected by a typical change, the probability of introducing inconsistent behavior, and the importance of the affected functionality. A duplicated business rule that repeatedly causes inconsistent results may deserve priority over a mildly long function that is rarely changed. Some smells reinforce one another: a Large Class may contain Data Clumps and Feature Envy, while a Long Function may contain repeated switches and duplicated code. Addressing a root structural problem can remove several symptoms, but changes should remain small enough to test and review.
Study instruction:
- Rank identified smells by observed change frequency, defect history, risk, and expected maintenance savings, then choose one bounded improvement to perform first.
Key point:
- Prioritize by real cost and change impact, not by the number of smells.
- One structural refactoring may address several related symptoms when it targets their common cause.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
49. Q: Why should developers avoid treating the smell list as a mandatory checklist for every class or function?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents smells as indications to investigate, not requirements that every code element must satisfy. Some structures are appropriate in their context even if they resemble a smell. A small class may legitimately hold immutable result data; a subclass may safely reuse only part of its inherited implementation; a conditional may be clearer than a polymorphic hierarchy; and a comment may preserve important rationale. Applying a refactoring without a clear benefit can add indirection, new abstractions, or unnecessary maintenance work. Developers should use the list as a diagnostic aid, then decide whether a particular change makes the code easier to understand or modify. The best design is not the one with the fewest visible smells but the one that best supports the system's real needs.
Study instruction:
- Choose one apparent smell and write both the case for refactoring and the case for leaving the code as it is; justify the decision using actual maintenance needs.
Key point:
- A smell is evidence to examine, not a rule that must always trigger a refactoring.
- Avoid design changes that add complexity without solving a real problem.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
50. Q: How can code smells help developers choose an appropriate refactoring from the catalog?
A: Show answer
A smell can be used as an initial diagnosis that points toward a family of possible refactorings. A Mysterious Name suggests renaming or clarifying the underlying responsibility; Duplicated Code suggests extraction or consolidation; a Long Function suggests extracting coherent responsibilities; a Long Parameter List suggests grouping or removing unnecessary arguments; and Repeated Switches may suggest polymorphism. Other smells point to moving behavior, encapsulating state, extracting classes, or simplifying unnecessary indirection. The catalog provides mechanics and guidance for individual transformations, but the developer still needs to understand the underlying design problem and select a suitable remedy. A smell may have more than one possible fix, and the best solution depends on the program's context.
Study instruction:
- For five detected smells, consult the catalog and compare the suggested refactorings before selecting the one that addresses the root cause most directly.
Key point:
- Smells help identify the problem; refactoring techniques describe ways to change the structure.
- Select a technique based on context rather than applying the first listed operation automatically.
Source links:
- Refactoring Catalog
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
51. Q: How can one underlying design problem produce several different code smells?
A: Show answer
A class with too many responsibilities may become Large Class, exhibit Divergent Change because unrelated requirements modify it, and create Shotgun Surgery because clients and other modules also contain fragments of its logic. A long function may contain duplicated code, repeated switches, and excessive parameters. Mutable global state can contribute to Feature Envy and Insider Trading because modules communicate through uncontrolled shared data. These smells are not always independent, and treating each symptom in isolation can lead to repeated or conflicting refactorings. Developers should look for the underlying reason that several symptoms exist, then choose a structural change that improves the relevant boundaries. Tests and incremental steps help confirm that solving one smell does not introduce another problem.
Study instruction:
- Select a module with multiple smells and draw the relationships between them, identifying whether one structural cause explains several symptoms.
Key point:
- Smells can be symptoms of a common responsibility or dependency problem.
- Treat root causes rather than mechanically removing each symptom in isolation.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
52. Q: What should a developer do when a suspected smell is difficult to classify?
A: Show answer
The developer should describe the observed maintenance problem in concrete terms instead of forcing it into a named category. For example, state that a feature change requires editing six unrelated modules, or that one function retrieves data from another object through a long chain of getters. Then identify the likely causes: scattered responsibility, unclear ownership, duplicated rules, or excessive coupling. The smell list can suggest relevant concepts and refactoring options, but an exact label is not required to improve the code. If the problem remains unclear, use comprehension refactoring, code review, or a small experiment to see whether a proposed boundary improves understanding. The outcome matters more than assigning the perfect smell name.
Study instruction:
- Document a design concern without using smell terminology, then map it to one or more possible smells and evaluate whether the mapping suggests a useful refactoring.
Key point:
- Smell names are vocabulary for discussion, not a required classification exercise.
- Describe the practical cost and choose a change that directly addresses it.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
53. Q: How should code smells be used during code review?
A: Show answer
Reviewers can use smells as prompts for questions about maintainability and change impact. Instead of saying that a class is simply too large, ask whether it contains unrelated responsibilities or whether distinct clients use separate subsets of its interface. Instead of objecting to every switch, ask whether the same type decision is repeated elsewhere and whether adding a category would require scattered edits. The reviewer should connect a proposed refactoring to a concrete benefit and consider the risk of unnecessary abstraction. A small demonstration can help the original author understand the suggestion. The review should also preserve behavior through tests and normal approval procedures. Smell terminology supports shared reasoning when it explains an actual maintenance concern rather than becoming a checklist for stylistic compliance.
Study instruction:
- In a code review, identify one smell and phrase the feedback as a concrete maintenance concern with a proposed, testable improvement.
Key point:
- Use smell language to explain change risk and design concerns constructively.
- Review the benefit and trade-off of the proposed refactoring, not just whether a pattern is present.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
54. Q: How should a team use code-smell metrics or automated scanners?
A: Show answer
Automated tools can help locate candidates such as long functions, large classes, duplicated fragments, or repeated dependencies, but their reports should be treated as prompts for human investigation. A tool can measure line count or duplication frequency, but it cannot reliably decide whether an abstraction is meaningful, whether an inherited method is harmless reuse, or whether an immutable result record is appropriate. Teams should calibrate thresholds to their code base and avoid using raw smell counts as a proxy for engineering quality or individual performance. A better process is to combine automated signals with change history, defect patterns, review feedback, and knowledge of the domain. The final decision remains whether a refactoring would improve the actual maintenance situation.
Study instruction:
- Review a static-analysis or code-quality report and validate a sample of findings manually before prioritizing any refactoring work.
Key point:
- Automated tools can find candidates but cannot determine design quality in context.
- Do not turn smell counts into simplistic performance targets.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
55. Q: What is a practical process for responding to a code smell without making an unnecessary redesign?
A: Show answer
Begin by describing what is difficult about the current code: understanding it, finding a change location, avoiding inconsistent copies, controlling state, or extending a category. Identify the likely smell and examine the surrounding responsibilities, callers, data dependencies, and history of changes. Consult the relevant refactoring techniques and choose the smallest transformation that directly improves the problem. Establish or run tests that protect observable behavior, perform the refactoring in small steps, and review whether the resulting structure is actually easier to understand. Stop when the design supports the relevant change adequately; do not continue adding abstractions merely to eliminate every possible smell. The chapter's emphasis is informed judgment supported by concrete signs of trouble.
Study instruction:
- Apply this diagnostic sequence to one real module and record the observed problem, candidate smell, proposed refactoring, tests, and evidence that the result is better.
Key point:
- Diagnose the maintenance problem before selecting the refactoring.
- Make the smallest useful improvement and stop when the design meets the actual need.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog
56. Q: How do the code smells in Chapter 3 relate to cohesion and coupling?
A: Show answer
Many smells can be understood as signs that responsibilities or dependencies are distributed poorly. Large Class and Divergent Change often indicate weak cohesion because unrelated concerns have been grouped together. Shotgun Surgery, Feature Envy, Message Chains, and Insider Trading can indicate excessive coupling because a change in one area requires knowledge of or edits to several others. Data Clumps and Primitive Obsession may indicate that domain concepts are not represented explicitly, while Global Data and Mutable Data make state dependencies harder to control. These are diagnostic interpretations rather than rigid one-to-one mappings. The developer should ask whether related behavior and data are located together, whether dependencies are intentional, and whether changes can be made within a clear boundary.
Study instruction:
- Group the chapter's smells under cohesion, coupling, state management, naming, and abstraction, and explain how each group affects maintainability.
Key point:
- Many smells reveal misplaced responsibilities or uncontrolled dependencies.
- Improving cohesion and controlling coupling often address several smells together.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
57. Q: How can developers distinguish a useful abstraction from a Lazy Element or Speculative Generality smell?
A: Show answer
A useful abstraction provides a meaningful name, encapsulates a coherent responsibility, supports real reuse, or isolates a variation that the system actually needs. A Lazy Element adds an extra function, class, or inheritance layer without enough corresponding value. Speculative Generality adds machinery for hypothetical future use that has no current consumer. The distinction depends on what the abstraction does for the design today and whether its future benefits are credible. Developers should ask whether the element is used by production code, whether it hides meaningful complexity, whether its interface expresses a real concept, and whether removing it would make the code easier to understand. It may be better to inline an unnecessary wrapper and reintroduce a better abstraction when real variation appears.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a little-used abstraction by listing its current callers, responsibility, extension points, and the concrete problem that would arise if it were removed.
Key point:
- Abstraction is valuable when it provides real meaning or isolates demonstrated variation.
- Unused or trivial structure can increase navigation and maintenance cost.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
58. Q: How should developers handle a code smell that is caused by an intentional architectural boundary?
A: Show answer
The developer should determine whether the apparent smell is the result of a deliberate boundary that protects a separate concern. For example, a function may use data from several modules because it coordinates a workflow, or a delegating class may hide a replaceable external service. Moving behavior merely to reduce getter calls or remove a forwarding method could weaken the architecture. The team should consider the reason for the boundary, the direction of dependencies, the expected pattern of change, and the cost of violating the abstraction. If the boundary has become obsolete or no longer protects a meaningful concern, refactoring may be justified. If it remains valuable, the smell may be a harmless trade-off rather than a defect.
Study instruction:
- For a suspected smell near a module boundary, document the architectural purpose of that boundary and compare the risks of keeping or moving the behavior.
Key point:
- Context and intent matter when interpreting a smell.
- Do not weaken a useful architectural boundary to satisfy a superficial rule.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
59. Q: What should developers remember about the relative strength of different code smells?
A: Show answer
Not every smell has the same urgency, and the same smell can have different significance in different systems. A subclass that does not use some inherited implementation may be acceptable if it still honors the superclass interface. A small immutable result record may be an intentional design boundary rather than a problematic Data Class. A single switch may be clearer than a class hierarchy, while repeated switches on the same type may be dangerous. A long function that is stable and rarely touched may be a lower priority than a modest amount of duplication that repeatedly causes defects. Developers should use the strength of the maintenance problem, the frequency of change, and the impact of errors to determine where effort is warranted.
Study instruction:
- Rank several smells from a real code base by their likely impact and explain why some should be refactored now while others can remain.
Key point:
- Smell severity depends on context, frequency, and consequence.
- Prioritization is a matter of engineering judgment rather than a fixed smell hierarchy.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
60. Q: What is the overall lesson of Chapter 3 about recognizing when to refactor?
A: Show answer
Chapter 3 provides a vocabulary for recognizing structures that may obstruct understanding and future change. Mysterious names, duplicated code, long functions, long parameter lists, uncontrolled global or mutable data, scattered responsibilities, excessive coupling, data clumps, primitive representations, repeated switches, and unnecessary abstractions all suggest opportunities for closer inspection. The chapter also emphasizes exceptions: conditionals are not universally bad, comments can be valuable, some inheritance reuse is acceptable, and immutable result records can legitimately contain data without behavior. There is no metric that can determine every decision. The developer's responsibility is to recognize the maintenance problem, choose an appropriate refactoring, and judge whether the result genuinely improves the system rather than simply satisfying a stylistic preference.
Study instruction:
- Use the chapter as a diagnostic guide on a real code base, select one significant smell, and justify the chosen refactoring in terms of improved comprehension or reduced change cost.
Key point:
- Code smells are prompts to investigate design, not rules to enforce mechanically.
- The right refactoring is the one that addresses a real maintenance problem with an appropriate trade-off.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition: Chapter 3
- Refactoring Catalog

Table 4
Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 4: Building Tests
1. Q: What is self-testing code, and what distinguishes it from manually checked testing?
A: Show answer
Self-testing code is software accompanied by automated tests that execute the program and check their own expected results. A person does not need to inspect console output, compare screens, or decide manually whether each run succeeded. Each test establishes a known condition, exercises the relevant behavior, and reports whether the observed result matches the expectation. The chapter describes the transition from tests that merely printed information to tests that compared actual output with expected output. This turns testing into a repeatable operation that can be run as routinely as compilation. Self-testing does not mean the program proves its own correctness; it means the test suite provides automatic, consistent feedback about specified behaviors.

Study instruction: Take one manual verification task from an application. Write down the initial data, action, expected result, and a machine-checkable assertion. Run it twice, including once after deliberately changing the expected behavior, and confirm that the test reports the discrepancy.

Key point: Automate both execution and result checking. A test that requires someone to interpret output is not fully self-testing.
2. Q: Why can writing tests increase programming productivity even though it requires additional code?
A: Show answer
The chapter's productivity argument is based on the difference between fixing a defect and finding it. The correction may be small, but locating the cause can consume hours, especially when the defect was introduced long before it became visible. A frequently executed regression suite narrows the search window: if a previously passing test fails immediately after a small change, the likely cause is among the recent edits. The developer is still familiar with that code and has fewer possible changes to investigate. The initial cost of writing a test is therefore offset by reduced debugging, faster feedback, and fewer long investigations. Tests also clarify intended behavior before implementation begins.

Study instruction: Track one week of development work. For each defect, note when it was introduced, when it was detected, and how long diagnosis took. Then identify which tests could have exposed it closer to the time of introduction.

Key point: The main productivity benefit is faster defect localization, not merely faster defect correction.
