The catalog is the book's practical reference collection of named refactorings. The chapters before it establish the ideas and skills needed to understand refactoring, while the catalog records specific transformations in a form that can be consulted during real work. Its purpose is not merely to define terminology. It gives a developer a repeatable description of why a transformation may be useful, when it may be inappropriate, and how to carry it out in controlled steps. The catalog is intended to support both learning and recall: a reader can study an unfamiliar technique in depth or quickly revisit a technique that has not been used recently. It is therefore best treated as a working reference rather than as a sequence that must always be read from beginning to end.
Study instruction: Choose a recurring code-maintenance problem and identify what information you would need from a refactoring reference before changing the code. Separate the information needed to recognize a technique from the information needed to perform it safely.
Key point: The catalog turns refactoring knowledge into a reusable, searchable working reference.
2. Q: How did the refactoring catalog originate, and why does that origin matter?
A: Show answer
The catalog began as the author's personal notes for remembering how to perform refactorings safely and efficiently, especially techniques that were not used every day. Those notes were subsequently refined, and the collection was expanded through deliberate exploration of additional refactoring moves. This origin explains the practical character of the catalog. Its mechanics are not presented as abstract algorithms detached from programming work; they are distilled reminders based on carrying out transformations and learning how to reduce risk. It also explains why a developer may find the catalog useful even after learning a technique: the notes preserve procedural details and special cases that are easy to forget between occasions of use. The catalog should therefore be approached as accumulated practical knowledge that can still evolve, not as an immutable list of every possible transformation.
Study instruction: After completing a safe refactoring, write a short personal note recording the sequence of changes, the tests used, and any special case that was easy to overlook. Compare that note with the structure of a catalog entry.
Key point: The catalog grew from practical notes, then gained breadth through refinement and exploration.
3. Q: What five parts make up the standard format of a catalog entry?
A: Show answer
Each entry has five parts: a name, a short sketch, a motivation, mechanics, and examples. The name supplies a stable label for discussing the transformation. The sketch is a compact recognition aid that helps a reader locate the right entry. The motivation explains why the refactoring may be worthwhile and identifies circumstances in which it should not be used. The mechanics provide a concise sequence of steps for carrying it out. The examples expand on those steps and demonstrate the transformation in a simple setting. These parts answer different questions and should not be treated as interchangeable. A sketch is not a substitute for mechanics, and mechanics alone may not provide enough explanation when a reader is encountering a technique for the first time. Together, the five parts support recognition, judgment, execution, and learning.
Study instruction: Select one catalog entry elsewhere in the book and annotate each of its five parts with the question it answers: what is it called, what does it look like, why or why not use it, how is it done, and what does it look like in an example?
Key point: The five-part structure separates identification, rationale, procedure, and illustration.
4. Q: Why does a refactoring need a consistent name, and why are aliases useful?
A: Show answer
A consistent name creates vocabulary for discussing a transformation precisely. Without shared names, developers may describe the same operation in different ways, making design discussions, code reviews, documentation, and knowledge sharing less efficient. A named refactoring allows a developer to say what change is intended without having to explain the entire procedure each time. Aliases are useful because refactorings may already be known by more than one common name. Recording aliases helps readers recognize an entry even when their team, tool, or previous reading uses different terminology. The name is therefore more than a heading: it is an aid to communication and retrieval. It should be used consistently, while aliases should be treated as alternate labels for locating the same underlying transformation rather than as evidence that the mechanics are necessarily different.
Study instruction: Ask two developers to describe a familiar code transformation independently. Compare their terminology, agree on one preferred name, and record any alternate names that would help another developer find the technique.
Key point: Names create a shared vocabulary; aliases make that vocabulary easier to recognize across different usage.
5. Q: What is the sketch of a refactoring intended to do, and what should a reader not expect from it?
A: Show answer
The sketch is a short visual or code-oriented reminder of the transformation. Its primary purpose is rapid recognition: a reader who has encountered the refactoring before can scan the sketch and identify the relevant entry quickly. The book also uses a small graphic as a memory aid. Neither device is intended to provide a complete explanation. A sketch is not a full definition, a substitute for understanding the motivation, or a safe procedure for changing code. A reader who has not seen the refactoring before will probably need to study the example and the mechanics to understand it. Treating a compact sketch as a complete tutorial risks applying a transformation without understanding its preconditions, consequences, or special cases.
Study instruction: Find a sketch for a technique you have not used. Before reading the rest of the entry, write down what you think changes and what you still need to know before attempting it. Then read the motivation, mechanics, and example to identify the gaps.
Key point: A sketch is a memory jogger and navigation aid, not a complete explanation or execution plan.
6. Q: Why does each refactoring entry include a motivation section?
A: Show answer
The motivation explains the design or maintenance reason for performing a transformation and describes circumstances in which it should not be performed. This matters because a refactoring is not automatically beneficial merely because it is available in the catalog. A developer must first recognize a problem or opportunity and then decide whether the proposed transformation addresses it. The motivation provides that decision context. It helps distinguish a useful improvement from a mechanical change that adds complexity, creates unnecessary churn, or fails to solve the actual problem. It also discourages treating refactorings as a checklist to apply indiscriminately. The mechanics answer how to perform a change; the motivation helps answer whether the change is appropriate in the current situation.
Study instruction: For a candidate refactoring, write down the code smell or design difficulty you are trying to address. Then identify at least one condition that would make the transformation unnecessary, premature, or unsuitable.
Key point: Decide whether a refactoring is justified before following its steps.
7. Q: What role do the mechanics play in a refactoring entry?
A: Show answer
The mechanics are a concise, step-by-step description of how to perform the transformation. They are designed as practical reminders for someone who already understands the technique but needs to recall its sequence. Their brevity is deliberate: the mechanics are quick to consult while working, rather than a long explanation of every reason behind every step. The examples provide the more expansive explanation that a first-time reader may need. Mechanics should therefore be read as an operational sequence, including any special-case checks, not as a complete discussion of the design rationale. When using them, a developer should preserve the order and safety intent of the steps, while consulting the example when the reason for a step or the handling of a particular situation is unclear.
Study instruction: Take a mechanics list and convert it into a working checklist. Mark each step as a code edit, a verification, or a special-case check, and identify where you would run the relevant tests.
Key point: Mechanics are concise operational notes; examples supply fuller explanation.
8. Q: Why does the catalog emphasize very small steps when describing refactoring mechanics?
A: Show answer
Small steps make it easier to preserve behavior and identify the source of a defect. If a developer makes several changes at once and a test fails, it may be difficult to determine which change caused the failure. When each change is small and followed by verification, the likely cause is much easier to isolate. Small steps also make it practical to reverse only the most recent change when something goes wrong. The author notes that experienced work may sometimes proceed in larger increments, but the safe baseline is to make small changes and reduce the step size further when the situation is tricky. The point is not to maximize the number of edits or to follow a ritual mechanically. It is to keep each transformation understandable and its effects observable.
Study instruction: Take a refactoring you would normally perform in one large edit and divide it into the smallest meaningful transformations. Identify what observable result or test would confirm each transformation before proceeding.
Key point: Smaller steps reduce uncertainty, simplify diagnosis, and make recovery easier.
9. Q: Why should a developer test after every small refactoring step?
A: Show answer
Testing after each step provides frequent evidence that the program still behaves as expected. Refactoring is intended to change the structure of code without changing its externally observable behavior. If a test fails immediately after a small transformation, the developer has a narrow search area for the cause and can back out or correct the latest change before proceeding. If testing is postponed until many edits have accumulated, a failure may be harder to localize and the developer may have to inspect or reverse several changes. The chapter emphasizes testing after every small step as part of the safe method. The exact test suite or verification technique depends on the system, but the discipline is consistent: make a small change, verify, and continue only when the result is acceptable.
Study instruction: Choose a small refactoring in a codebase with automated tests. Record the test result before the change, after each intermediate change, and at the end. Note how the timing of a failure affects the effort needed to diagnose it.
Key point: Frequent verification keeps behavior under control and makes failures easier to localize.
10. Q: How do special cases in the mechanics turn a refactoring procedure into a checklist?
A: Show answer
A refactoring may have edge conditions that are easy to forget when a developer remembers only the main transformation. The catalog includes references to special cases in its mechanics so that the procedure can prompt the developer to check them at the appropriate moment. In this way, the steps do more than describe a sequence of edits: they also act as a checklist of details that may affect correctness. Skipping a special-case check can leave a transformation incomplete or introduce a defect even when the main sequence appears correct. A reader should therefore treat qualifications and conditional steps as meaningful parts of the procedure, not as optional commentary. The chapter notes that these checks are useful even to the author, who may forget them when returning to a technique after a period of time.
Study instruction: Review a mechanics list and extract every conditional phrase, exception, or special-case reference. Turn each into an explicit question you can answer before marking the refactoring complete.
Key point: Special-case notes protect against omissions and make mechanics function as a practical checklist.
11. Q: Are the mechanics in the catalog the only valid way to perform a refactoring?
A: Show answer
No. The book generally presents one set of mechanics, but it explicitly recognizes that other sequences can also work. The selected steps are intended to be effective in most situations, not to establish an exclusive or universally optimal procedure. Developers may adapt the sequence as they gain experience and encounter different code structures, tools, or constraints. That flexibility does not remove the central safety principle: keep changes small, and make them smaller when the situation becomes more difficult. An alternative sequence should preserve the purpose of the refactoring and provide adequate opportunities to verify behavior. Changing the order or combining steps simply for speed is not a sound adaptation if it makes the result harder to understand or diagnose.
Study instruction: Compare the catalog mechanics with a procedure you have used for a similar transformation. Identify which differences are harmless adaptations and which would remove an important verification or safety step.
Key point: The published mechanics are a reliable starting point, not the only possible sequence.
12. Q: How should the difficulty of a refactoring influence the size of each step?
A: Show answer
The more difficult or delicate the situation, the smaller the steps should become. A straightforward transformation in familiar code may permit an experienced developer to combine a few operations while still maintaining confidence. A tricky transformation, unfamiliar code, or a change with many special cases increases uncertainty. In those circumstances, reducing the step size makes it easier to see exactly what changed and to detect an unintended effect early. The chapter describes the author's practice of sometimes taking larger steps at work, then backing out the last step and using smaller increments if a bug appears. This is not a reason to begin with large, poorly controlled edits. It illustrates that step size can be adjusted while preserving a small-step recovery strategy.
Study instruction: Rate a planned refactoring as low, moderate, or high difficulty. Explain the factors behind the rating and define how small each change should be, including the verification point after it.
Key point: Step size should shrink as uncertainty and complexity increase.
13. Q: How should a reader use the examples when encountering a refactoring for the first time?
A: Show answer
The examples provide a more expansive explanation than the terse mechanics. They use simple code to isolate the transformation and show how the steps work without introducing unnecessary distractions. A first-time reader should study the example alongside the mechanics to understand not only the order of the steps but also how the transformation changes the code. The examples are intentionally modest, so they may not resemble a full business application or a complicated production codebase. Their role is to teach the essential move in a controlled setting. Once the underlying idea is clear, the reader can apply the same reasoning to a more complex context, while still checking the motivation, special cases, and safety requirements of the real code.
Study instruction: Work through an example without copying it mechanically. Explain each change in your own words, then identify what additional concerns would need investigation before applying the technique to production code.
Key point: Examples are learning aids that explain the mechanics in a low-distraction setting.
14. Q: Why are the catalog's examples deliberately simple, and what are their limitations?
A: Show answer
The examples are deliberately simple because their purpose is to illustrate one refactoring with as few distractions as possible. They are not intended to be realistic models of good business design or complete representations of production systems. Complex domain rules, infrastructure, frameworks, and organizational constraints could obscure the particular transformation being taught. Simplicity makes the essential code movement easier to see. The limitation is that a reader cannot assume the example covers every concern present in a real codebase. A production application may contain dependencies, side effects, tests, data constraints, or special cases that are absent from the illustration. The example should be used to understand the basic move, while the motivation and mechanics guide the judgment and verification needed for actual work.
Study instruction: After studying a simple example, list three characteristics of your own codebase that are absent from it. For each characteristic, decide what inspection or test would be needed before applying the refactoring.
Key point: Simplicity clarifies the transformation, but production application requires additional context and checks.
15. Q: Why might code still have problems after the example demonstrates a successful refactoring?
A: Show answer
Most examples focus on the single refactoring being discussed. They are not intended to turn the sample code into a fully polished design. As a result, the code may still contain problems after the illustrated transformation has been completed. Resolving those remaining issues may require other refactorings, but adding them to the same example could make it harder to see the effect of the original technique. A reader should distinguish between the success of the specific transformation and the overall quality of the resulting design. The example can be correct as an illustration even when the code is not finished. This boundary keeps each entry focused and makes it easier to study one change at a time.
Study instruction: Inspect an example after the target refactoring and identify any remaining design concerns. Separate issues addressed by the demonstrated transformation from issues that would require a different refactoring or a separate design decision.
Key point: Completing one refactoring does not imply that all design problems have been solved.
16. Q: Why does the catalog usually keep each example self-contained instead of carrying all improvements forward?
A: Show answer
The primary role of the catalog is to serve as a reference, so each entry is generally kept self-contained and focused on one refactoring. Carrying every earlier improvement into later examples could make the code increasingly complicated and obscure which transformation is being illustrated. The author sometimes carries examples forward when refactorings naturally go together, but usually leaves the code at the point reached by the single refactoring under discussion. This makes entries easier to consult independently. A reader can open one entry to understand one technique without needing to reconstruct a long chain of changes from previous examples. The trade-off is that the reader may see unfinished code, but that is deliberate and supports the catalog's reference purpose.
Study instruction: When studying two related refactorings, compare their examples separately. Then create your own short sequence showing how they might be combined, marking where the first transformation ends and the next begins.
Key point: Self-contained examples preserve focus and make individual entries independently useful.
17. Q: Why does the book highlight changed code selectively rather than marking every changed line?
A: Show answer
Color is used to make changed code easier to spot when the difference might otherwise be difficult to see among unchanged code. However, the author does not highlight every change because excessive highlighting would defeat its purpose. If every line is emphasized, the reader loses the visual contrast that makes important changes stand out. Selective highlighting is therefore a visual navigation aid, not a complete change record or a substitute for reading the code. A reader should use it to locate noteworthy differences, then inspect the surrounding code to understand the full transformation. In other contexts, the same principle applies to review tools and documentation: visual emphasis is most useful when reserved for changes that genuinely benefit from it.
Study instruction: Review a code diff with many changed lines. Identify which changes need visual emphasis to explain the transformation and which should remain unmarked to preserve contrast.
Key point: Selective highlighting improves visibility; over-highlighting weakens the signal.
18. Q: Is the book's refactoring catalog intended to be complete?
A: Show answer
No. The author explicitly describes the catalog as a useful collection rather than a complete inventory of every refactoring that could exist. The selection reflects techniques considered both commonly used and worthwhile to name and describe. Some entries are included because their mechanics teach useful general skills; others are included because they have a strong effect on improving code design. This means the catalog is selective by intent. A missing transformation should not automatically be interpreted as invalid or unimportant, and the presence of an entry should not be taken as a requirement to use it. The collection is designed to record techniques that benefit from a written explanation, not to enumerate every possible code edit.
Study instruction: Identify a code transformation you use that is not represented in a reference catalog. Decide whether it is too small to need a formal entry, too uncommon, or sufficiently useful and subtle that documenting it would help your team.
Key point: The catalog is a curated practical collection, not an exhaustive taxonomy.
19. Q: What criteria influence whether a refactoring is worth documenting in the catalog?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives two central considerations: how commonly a refactoring is used and whether it is worthwhile to name and describe. A technique may deserve an entry because its mechanics are interesting and help develop general refactoring skills. Another may deserve an entry because it has a strong effect on improving code design. These criteria explain why the catalog does not simply list every possible operation. Documentation has value when it gives developers a reusable name, preserves a non-obvious procedure, explains an important design benefit, or helps avoid mistakes. Very trivial transformations may not gain much from a detailed write-up. The selection therefore balances practical frequency with the educational or design value of explaining the move.
Study instruction: Evaluate a candidate refactoring using three questions: how often is it needed, how much judgment or procedural care does it require, and how much design improvement can it enable? Record why a catalog entry would or would not be useful.
Key point: Frequency and the value of naming, teaching, or preserving the mechanics shape catalog inclusion.
20. Q: Why might a frequently used but very small refactoring be omitted from a catalog?
A: Show answer
Frequency alone does not guarantee that a transformation needs a full catalog entry. Some refactorings are so small and straightforward that the author does not initially consider them worth writing up, even when they are used often. The chapter gives Slide Statements as an example: it was used frequently but was omitted from the first edition because it was not recognized at the time as deserving a catalog entry. The author changed that decision for the second edition. This example shows that the boundary between an informal coding move and a named refactoring can change as experience accumulates. A technique may become worth documenting when its usefulness becomes clearer or when a concise explanation helps others apply it consistently.
Study instruction: Choose a small transformation used often in your team. Discuss whether naming and documenting it would improve consistency or whether a brief team convention is sufficient. Note what new evidence might change that decision later.
Key point: A common technique may still be too straightforward to warrant a full entry, and that judgment can evolve.
21. Q: Why does the catalog not document every inverse of every refactoring?
A: Show answer
Every refactoring has a logical inverse, but the author does not consider every inverse interesting or useful enough to describe. Some inverses are rarely performed, and some are so easy that a separate entry adds little value. The chapter uses the inverse of Encapsulate Variable as an example: although the inverse logically exists, the author hardly ever performs it and regards it as easy to carry out. This illustrates that the catalog is selected for practical usefulness rather than mathematical symmetry. The existence of an inverse does not mean both directions require equal explanation, have equal frequency, or provide equal design value. A reader should understand the relationship while still judging each direction on its own practical merits.
Study instruction: Select a refactoring and describe its logical inverse. Compare the frequency, difficulty, risk, and design motivation of the two directions, then decide whether both would benefit from documented mechanics.
Key point: Logical symmetry does not require equal catalog coverage; practical value determines what merits an entry.
22. Q: How can a developer use a catalog entry safely as a practical workflow?
A: Show answer
A useful workflow begins by identifying the code problem and reading the motivation to determine whether the refactoring fits the situation. The name and sketch help confirm that the correct technique has been located, but they are not enough to begin changing code. A developer who is unfamiliar with the technique should study the example to understand the transformation, then use the mechanics as a concise procedure. Before editing, identify relevant tests and any special cases listed in the steps. Perform the change in small increments, verify after each increment, and reduce the step size if the code is difficult or a defect appears. If a test fails, isolate the most recent change and correct or reverse it before continuing. Finally, assess whether the target problem was addressed without assuming that unrelated design issues have also been resolved.
Study instruction: Apply this workflow to a small, low-risk refactoring in a practice repository. Keep a log of the motivation check, example reviewed, mechanics followed, special cases checked, verification after each step, and any remaining design concerns.
Key point: Combine motivation, example, mechanics, small steps, and frequent verification into one disciplined workflow.
Rows: 22
Table 6
Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 6: A First Set of Refactorings
1. Q: What is the organizing idea behind Chapter 6, and how do its refactorings fit together?
A: Show answer
Chapter 6 presents a practical starter catalog for changing code structure without changing its externally observable behavior. It begins with small-scale naming and decomposition operations: Extract Function, Inline Function, Extract Variable, Inline Variable, Change Function Declaration, Encapsulate Variable, and Rename Variable. It then shows how repeated data and arguments can reveal larger abstractions through Introduce Parameter Object, Combine Functions into Class, and Combine Functions into Transform. Finally, Split Phase separates processing stages that have different responsibilities. The sequence moves from clarifying a local expression or function toward organizing data, behavior, and whole processing workflows. These techniques are complementary building blocks, not a rigid recipe that every change must follow.
Study instruction: - Draw a map linking each refactoring to the problem it addresses: unclear intent, needless indirection, awkward signatures, exposed data, data clumps, scattered calculations, or mixed processing stages.
- For a real code fragment, identify the smallest useful refactoring before proposing a larger redesign.
Key point: - Start with local clarity, then improve interfaces and module boundaries.
- Choose a refactoring because it solves a specific structural problem, not because it appears in the catalog.
2. Q: What is Extract Function, and what problem is it intended to solve?
A: Show answer
Extract Function takes a coherent fragment of code from an existing function, places it in a separately named function, and replaces the original fragment with a call. Its main purpose is to separate intention from implementation. The caller can then express what needs to happen through a meaningful name, while the extracted function contains the detail of how it happens. This is useful even when the fragment is used only once; reuse is not the principal test. A good extraction reduces the amount of implementation detail a reader must hold in mind at the current level. It can also establish a natural place for later reuse, testing, or movement into another module.
Study instruction: - Find a multi-line fragment whose purpose takes effort to infer, then propose a name describing its outcome rather than its mechanics.
- Compare the caller before and after extraction and assess whether the high-level sequence is easier to understand.
Key point: - Extract for meaningful intent, not merely for line count or reuse.
- A well-named function makes the caller read like a description of the work.
3. Q: How should a developer decide whether a fragment deserves its own function?
A: Show answer
The strongest reason to extract is that the fragment represents a distinct intention that deserves a name. Screen length and the number of times code is reused are weak rules when used alone. A single-line expression may deserve extraction if a domain-specific name communicates its meaning better than the expression. Conversely, a longer fragment may not benefit if no clearer purpose can be named. Comments that introduce a block often reveal an intention that could become a function name. Naming is part of the decision: if extraction produces a name that simply repeats the mechanics, the new function may add indirection without adding understanding. It is acceptable to experiment and inline the function again if the abstraction proves unhelpful.
Study instruction: - Review five blocks in an existing function. For each, write a candidate purpose-oriented name and explain whether that name adds information beyond the code itself.
- Treat comments such as “calculate tax” or “record due date” as clues, then test whether the proposed function name is precise.
Key point: - Prefer semantic value over arbitrary size thresholds.
- If no useful name emerges, reconsider the extraction.
4. Q: Why can very small functions improve readability rather than fragmenting the code?
A: Show answer
A short function can create a useful conceptual boundary even when its body contains only one statement. The function name communicates the intention, while its implementation remains available when a reader needs detail. This can make code self-documenting and reduce the need for explanatory comments. The benefit depends on the quality of the name and on whether the function establishes a meaningful concept. A chain of tiny functions that merely delegate without clarifying intent can instead create irritating indirection. The goal is not to maximize the number of functions or minimize their line count; it is to expose useful concepts at the level where a reader needs them.
Study instruction: - Compare a short named wrapper with its inlined expression. Ask whether the name adds domain meaning, hides distracting detail, or merely forces another navigation step.
- Identify one case where a short function improves a caller and one where it would be needless.
Key point: - Small functions are valuable when names carry meaning.
- Indirection is justified by clarity, not by size alone.
5. Q: What is the recommended sequence for performing Extract Function safely?
A: Show answer
First create a new function and name it after its intent. Copy the selected code into it, then inspect every reference to variables that were local to the original function and are not in scope in the new location. Pass required values as parameters, move declarations inward when they are used only by the extracted code, and decide how assignments will be communicated back. Compile or run static checks when the language provides useful checks. Replace the original fragment with a call, run tests, and then search for similar inline logic that may be able to call the new function. The process should be incremental enough that a failure can be traced to a small change.
Study instruction: - Practice the sequence on a function with at least one local input and one locally computed result.
- Record the test or static-check result after the scope changes and again after replacing the original block.
Key point: - Resolve scope and data flow before deleting the original code.
- Test after the call-site replacement and inspect possible duplication afterward.
6. Q: How do nested functions help when extracting code that uses local variables?
A: Show answer
When a language supports nested functions, placing the extracted function inside its original function allows it to retain access to the original lexical scope. This can avoid introducing a large parameter list merely to pass values that are already available nearby. It is often a convenient first step while the new abstraction is still closely related to its source. However, nesting can conceal the dependencies that will become relevant if the function is later moved elsewhere. If the eventual destination is a sibling function, module, or class, extracting at that level can expose missing dependencies earlier. The choice should make the next safe step easier rather than postpone a difficult scope problem.
Study instruction: - Extract a fragment both as a nested function and as a sibling function. Compare the required parameters and the dependencies each form makes visible.
- If the function is likely to move, test whether its interface is understandable without relying on hidden outer-scope variables.
Key point: - Nesting can reduce immediate scope friction.
- A destination-level extraction can reveal dependencies that nesting hides.
7. Q: How should read-only local variables be handled during Extract Function?
A: Show answer
A local variable that the extracted code reads but does not assign is usually straightforward: pass it as an argument to the new function. This makes the dependency explicit and preserves the value supplied by the caller. The same approach applies to parameters of the original function and to temporary values that are computed before the extracted block. If a value is a structured object and the extracted code mutates that object, passing the reference can preserve the intended mutation, but the developer must understand the language's reference semantics and the surrounding contract. The important distinction is whether the extracted code only observes a value or changes state that other code expects to observe.
Study instruction: - Inspect a candidate fragment and classify every referenced local as read-only, reassigned, or mutated through an object reference.
- Add parameters for read-only dependencies and write a test showing that the extracted function receives the same values as the original fragment.
Key point: - Pass read-only locals explicitly as parameters.
- Distinguish reassignment of a local binding from mutation of an object it references.
8. Q: What is the difference between reassigning a local variable and mutating an object during extraction?
A: Show answer
Reassigning a local binding changes which value the local variable refers to. If the extracted function receives that value by ordinary value semantics, the caller's local binding will not automatically receive the new value. Mutating an object through a reference is different: both caller and callee may refer to the same object, so changes to its properties can be visible to the caller. This distinction explains why a structured object can sometimes be passed into an extracted function and updated there, while a reassigned numeric temporary may need to be returned. The correct treatment depends on the language's parameter and reference semantics, not merely on the visual form of the variable.
Study instruction: - Create two small examples: one where a number is reassigned in a helper and one where an object's property is changed.
- Observe which changes are visible to the caller and document the language semantics that explain the result.
Key point: - Reassignment changes a local binding; mutation changes referenced state.
- Do not assume that passing a value automatically communicates a reassigned local back to the caller.
9. Q: How should an assigned temporary be handled when its new value is needed outside the extracted function?
A: Show answer
If a temporary is assigned within the extracted fragment and used afterward by the original function, the extracted function should normally compute and return the new value. The caller then assigns the result to its local variable. The declaration can often move into the extracted function, because the temporary's calculation belongs there. This is clearer than passing an initial value into the helper only to overwrite it. After extraction, the caller's result variable may become immutable if it is assigned only once. If the temporary is used only inside the extracted code, keep its declaration and assignments inside the new function and do not expose it through the interface.
Study instruction: - Extract a calculation that accumulates a total used later by the caller.
- Move the accumulator into the new function, return the result, and test that the caller receives the same total as before.
Key point: - Return a reassigned temporary when the caller needs its final value.
- Keep purely internal temporaries inside the extracted function.
10. Q: What should be done when Extract Function appears to require returning several local variables?
A: Show answer
Several output variables are often a signal that the chosen fragment is not a cohesive unit or that its temporary-variable structure needs improvement. The preferred response is usually to reconsider the extraction boundary and create separate functions that each return one meaningful value. Refactorings such as Replace Temp with Query or Split Variable can simplify the data flow before extraction is attempted again. If multiple values genuinely form one result, a record or object can be returned, but that should represent a coherent result rather than a way to export every local variable. A large collection of output parameters makes the new function harder to understand and can indicate that the extraction is premature.
Study instruction: - Choose a fragment that currently assigns three local variables. Try two alternative extraction boundaries and identify whether each can return one cohesive result.
- If a record is still appropriate, define what the record means and which fields belong together.
Key point: - Multiple outputs often indicate an unsuitable extraction boundary.
- Prefer one meaningful result, using a result object only when the values form a real unit.
11. Q: Why should statements sometimes be moved closer to the variables they initialize before extraction?
A: Show answer
A temporary may be declared well before the code that uses it, making it difficult to determine which part of the function owns the calculation. Moving the declaration and its related statements closer to their use clarifies the data flow and can make the intended extraction boundary obvious. Slide Statements is useful for this preparation. Once the initialization, updates, and consumption of a temporary are grouped, it becomes easier to decide whether the variable belongs inside the extracted function or should be returned to the caller. This is especially helpful when a temporary is reassigned and its final value is consumed outside the selected block.
Study instruction: - Find a temporary whose declaration is separated from its main use by unrelated statements.
- Move the declaration and related updates next to the use, run tests, and then reconsider the extraction boundary.
Key point: - Locality makes data flow easier to see.
- Group related initialization and use before extracting complex temporary-variable logic.
12. Q: What is Inline Function, and when is it preferable to Extract Function?
A: Show answer
Inline Function replaces calls to a function with the function's body and then removes the function definition when no longer needed. It is useful when the body is as clear as the function name, when the abstraction no longer contributes meaning, or when excessive delegation makes the flow difficult to follow. It can also be used to flatten a poorly factored group of functions before extracting a better set of abstractions. Inline Function is not a declaration that short functions are bad; it is a way to remove indirection that has ceased to help. The decision should be based on the role the function plays in the current design.
Study instruction: - Identify a helper whose name adds little information beyond its one-line body.
- Inline it in a controlled branch and compare readability, call flow, and test results with the original version.
Key point: - Inline when indirection no longer clarifies intent.
- Preserve short functions when their names express a useful concept.
13. Q: What safety check is essential before inlining a method in an inheritance hierarchy?
A: Show answer
Before inlining a method, determine whether it is polymorphic. If subclasses override the method, callers may receive different behavior depending on the runtime type of the receiver. Replacing a call with one implementation's body can bypass dynamic dispatch and silently change behavior. In that situation, ordinary inlining is not safe unless the polymorphic design is first changed through an appropriate sequence of refactorings. The same caution applies when a method is part of a contract implemented by several classes. A method that looks trivial in one class may represent a variation point for the hierarchy as a whole.
Study instruction: - Inspect the class hierarchy for overrides, interface implementations, and dynamic dispatch before attempting to inline a method.
- Add or review tests that exercise each relevant concrete implementation.
Key point: - Check polymorphism before inlining methods.
- Do not replace dynamic dispatch with one implementation's body accidentally.
14. Q: How should Inline Function be performed when a helper contains several statements?
A: Show answer
Find all callers and replace each call with the helper's body, adapting parameter names and local names to the destination context. Test after each replacement, then remove the function once all callers have been handled. If the body is awkward to fit into the caller, move statements in smaller increments rather than performing a large cut-and-paste change. The sequence can use Move Statements to Callers to transfer one statement at a time. Small steps make it easier to preserve ordering, variable scope, and side effects. If a quick attempt causes failures, revert to the last known-good state and repeat with smaller increments.
Study instruction: - Inline a helper that appends two values to a caller-owned collection.
- Move one statement at a time, checking statement order and running focused tests after each move.
Key point: - Fit the body to its new context carefully.
- Incremental inlining reduces the risk of changing statement order or data flow.
15. Q: Why does Extract Variable improve the readability of a complex expression?
A: Show answer
Extract Variable assigns a meaningful local name to a subexpression and replaces the original expression with that name. The variable gives a reader a concise explanation of what a calculation represents, such as base price, quantity discount, or shipping cost. It can also provide a convenient point for a debugger or diagnostic output. The strongest result is not simply fewer characters; it is a decomposition of a complex expression into named concepts that can be understood independently. Once the expression is broken into meaningful parts, the final calculation often reads as a short statement of the business rule.
Study instruction: - Take a dense arithmetic or Boolean expression and divide it into named conceptual parts.
- Read the final expression aloud using the new names and check whether each name accurately describes its value.
Key point: - Name meaningful subexpressions to expose the domain logic.
- A variable should clarify what a value means, not merely abbreviate the calculation.
16. Q: What condition must be checked before applying Extract Variable?
A: Show answer
The expression being extracted should be free of side effects. If evaluating the expression changes state, performs I/O, advances an iterator, reads an unstable external value, or invokes behavior whose timing matters, evaluating it earlier or only once can change the program. Extract Variable may also change how often an expression is evaluated when repeated occurrences are replaced with one stored value. Before extracting, establish whether the expression is pure or otherwise safe to evaluate once at the chosen point. If it is not, first isolate or redesign the side effect so that the variable represents a stable value.
Study instruction: - Inspect each operation in a candidate expression and mark reads, writes, external calls, and time-dependent behavior.
- Add a test that would detect an accidental change in evaluation count or order.
Key point: - Extract only when evaluation timing and frequency remain safe.
- Side effects must be understood before naming an expression.
17. Q: When should a name introduced by Extract Variable become a function or a class property instead?
A: Show answer
A local variable is appropriate when the name is meaningful only within the current function. If the concept is meaningful across several operations or belongs to an object as part of its domain vocabulary, a broader abstraction may be more useful. In a class, a derived value such as base price or shipping can become a getter or method, allowing several methods and clients to share the same named concept. A function can also centralize a calculation used in multiple places. The wider abstraction takes more effort, so it should be introduced when the concept genuinely has a broader role rather than merely to avoid a local variable.
Study instruction: - For each extracted name in a function, ask whether another method or client would reasonably need the same concept.
- If so, propose a method or function interface and identify the data it would depend on.
Key point: - Keep local concepts local.
- Promote a name when it represents reusable domain behavior or shared knowledge.
18. Q: What is Inline Variable, and what makes it safe?
A: Show answer
Inline Variable replaces references to a temporary variable with the expression used to initialize it, then removes the declaration. It is useful when the variable name contributes no useful meaning or gets in the way of a neighboring refactoring. Safety begins by confirming that the right-hand expression has no side effects. If the variable is assigned more than once, first make it immutable or otherwise establish that its value is stable. Replace references one at a time, testing as you go, and remove the declaration only after every reference has been replaced. Inlining should simplify the code without changing the number or order of meaningful evaluations.
Study instruction: - Select a temporary initialized by a pure expression and used at several sites.
- Make it immutable, replace each use in sequence, and verify that the final expression is still understandable.
Key point: - Inline only stable, side-effect-free values.
- Remove the declaration after all references have been safely replaced.
19. Q: Why is immutability useful before inlining a variable?
A: Show answer
An immutable declaration demonstrates that the variable is assigned only once, so every reference represents the same value. This removes uncertainty about whether different references could observe different assignments. If a variable is mutable, replacing every use with its initializer may be incorrect because the variable may have changed between assignments and uses. Making the variable immutable is therefore both a design improvement and a safety check before inlining. If it cannot be made immutable, the developer should understand every assignment and may need to split the variable into distinct values before proceeding.
Study instruction: - Search for all writes to a candidate temporary, including compound assignments and updates inside loops.
- Convert it to an immutable declaration only if it is assigned once, then run tests before inlining.
Key point: - Immutability establishes a single stable value.
- A mutable variable cannot be replaced blindly by its original initializer.
20. Q: What does Change Function Declaration encompass beyond renaming a function?
A: Show answer
Change Function Declaration changes the name or parameter interface of a function. It includes adding or removing arguments and changing the information represented by an argument, such as replacing a whole customer object with the customer's state code. Function declarations are the joints through which parts of a system connect, so a good name and a suitable parameter list influence how easily the function can be understood and reused. A more general parameter can reduce coupling to a particular data structure, while a more specific parameter can preserve useful encapsulation. There is no universally correct signature independent of the function's role and likely evolution.
Study instruction: - Choose an existing function and evaluate its name, each parameter, and the callers that supply those parameters.
- Propose one signature change and explain which dependency it removes or which useful context it preserves.
Key point: - Function names and parameters define important module boundaries.
- Change the declaration as understanding of the function's role improves.
21. Q: How should a developer choose between passing an entire domain object and one of its properties?
A: Show answer
Passing a property such as a state code can make a function more general because it no longer depends on the larger object's interface. It can also reduce coupling between modules. Passing the whole domain object may be better when the function's responsibility genuinely concerns that object or is likely to need several of its properties as the logic evolves. The choice is therefore a trade-off between narrow dependency and useful encapsulation. A function that accepts too much context may be harder to reuse, while a function that accepts an overly narrow value may force repeated signature changes when related information becomes necessary.
Study instruction: - For one function, list the properties it actually uses and the domain concept it represents.
- Compare an interface that accepts the whole object with one that accepts only the used property, then assess likely future changes.
Key point: - Narrow parameters can reduce coupling and improve reuse.
- Whole-object parameters can preserve useful domain context and encapsulation.
22. Q: When are the simple mechanics for Change Function Declaration appropriate?
A: Show answer
The simple mechanics work well when the declaration and all its callers can be changed safely in one coordinated step. For a removed parameter, first confirm that the function body no longer references it. Change the declaration, update every reference to the old signature, and run tests. It is usually safer to separate independent changes, such as renaming the function first and adding a parameter afterward. Static typing and IDE-supported refactoring can make reference discovery more reliable, while dynamic code or ambiguous names may require careful searches and additional tests. If all callers cannot be updated together, use a migration approach instead.
Study instruction: - Rename a small function with a limited number of callers, then separately add or remove one parameter.
- Search for all references and verify that no old call signature remains.
Key point: - Use simple mechanics when all affected callers are known and manageable.
- Separate distinct signature changes and test each one.
23. Q: What are the migration mechanics for a difficult function-signature change?
A: Show answer
Migration mechanics create a new function that represents the desired interface while preserving a temporary forwarding path from the old function. First refactor the body if necessary, then extract it into a new function with a temporary searchable name. Add or change parameters on the new function and test it. Next inline the old forwarding function into its callers, allowing callers to migrate one at a time. Once every caller uses the new interface, rename the new function to the intended public name and test again. This staged approach avoids requiring every call site to change simultaneously and is useful when references are numerous, difficult to locate, or polymorphic.
Study instruction: - Simulate a signature change for a function with many callers by introducing a temporary function and migrating callers individually.
- Keep a checklist of migrated callers and verify the old entry point is removed only when no longer needed.
Key point: - Migration mechanics create a safe bridge between old and new interfaces.
- Move callers gradually, then retire the temporary compatibility layer.
24. Q: How can a published API be changed without immediately breaking existing clients?
A: Show answer
For a published API, the developer may need to keep the old function available after introducing the new declaration. The old entry point can be retained as a forwarding function or marked deprecated while clients migrate. Documentation should explain the replacement and the expected migration path. The old function is removed only when the maintainer has sufficient confidence that supported clients have moved or when the compatibility policy permits removal. This is different from an internal refactoring where all callers are under the same team's control. Public compatibility is part of the API contract and must be managed deliberately.
Study instruction: - Draft a migration plan for a public function rename, identifying deprecation, forwarding behavior, release communication, and removal criteria.
- Add a compatibility test showing that the old entry point continues to behave correctly during the transition.
Key point: - Public APIs require a compatibility period when clients cannot be changed together.
- Deprecation and removal should follow an explicit migration policy.
25. Q: How should a parameter be added when many callers cannot be updated at once?
A: Show answer
Introduce the new function through migration mechanics. Extract the existing body into a temporary function, add the new parameter there, and have the old function call it with a suitable default for legacy behavior. Test the new function and, where practical, assert that the parameter has a valid value. Then inline the old forwarding function into callers one at a time, supplying the correct new argument at each call site. Once all callers have migrated, restore the intended function name and remove the old compatibility layer. The default used during transition must preserve the old behavior and must not conceal callers that still require a deliberate choice.
Study instruction: - Add a Boolean or policy parameter to a function with several call sites.
- Use a temporary implementation and a validity assertion, then migrate each caller and verify that it supplies the intended value.
Key point: - A forwarding function can preserve old behavior while callers migrate.
- Assertions can expose incomplete call-site updates during the transition.
26. Q: How can a parameter change from a whole object to one of its properties be performed incrementally?
A: Show answer
First isolate the property used by the existing function, often by extracting it into a local variable. Extract a new function that accepts that property and delegates from the old function to the new one. Update callers gradually so they pass the property directly rather than the whole object. After all callers have migrated, rename the new function to the original public name and remove the wrapper. This sequence changes the dependency without forcing a simultaneous update across the codebase. It is especially useful when the desired parameter represents a more general value than the original domain object.
Study instruction: - Refactor a function that receives a customer but uses only the customer's region code.
- Introduce a property-based implementation, migrate callers one at a time, and test both old and new paths during the transition.
Key point: - Extract the narrower dependency before changing every caller.
- Migrate call sites gradually and restore the stable name after completion.
27. Q: What is Encapsulate Variable, and why is it important for widely shared mutable data?
A: Show answer
Encapsulate Variable routes reads and updates of a variable through functions, then restricts direct access to the underlying data. Widely shared mutable data is difficult to reorganize because every direct reference may need to change in the same coordinated step. Accessor functions create a stable boundary that can be renamed, moved, or extended while callers continue to use the functions. They also provide a central place for validation, logging, change notifications, or other consequences of updates. The broader the scope of mutable data, the more valuable this boundary becomes. Immutable data is less urgent because clients cannot create inconsistent updates through shared mutation.
Study instruction: - Find a mutable global or widely visible field and enumerate every read and write.
- Introduce getter and setter functions, migrate each reference with tests, and then restrict direct visibility.
Key point: - Encapsulation turns difficult data movement into manageable function-interface changes.
- Shared mutable data deserves a controlled access boundary.
28. Q: What is the recommended sequence for Encapsulate Variable?
A: Show answer
Create functions that read and update the variable. Run static checks, then replace each read with the getter and each assignment with the setter, testing after each replacement. Once references have been migrated, restrict the variable's visibility so future code cannot bypass the access functions. If the language or module system cannot enforce the restriction, renaming the underlying variable to an unmistakable internal name can help detect missed references during a search and test cycle. If the value is a record, consider whether the record itself needs deeper encapsulation rather than merely encapsulating the variable that points to it.
Study instruction: - Perform the sequence on a shared setting or configuration value.
- Keep separate counts of reads and writes, verify that both reach the accessors, and check that direct access is no longer possible.
Key point: - Introduce accessors before moving references.
- Restrict visibility after migration so the boundary remains effective.
29. Q: Does Encapsulate Variable automatically prevent clients from mutating an object returned by its getter?
A: Show answer
No. Basic variable encapsulation controls access to the reference and its reassignment, but it does not necessarily control mutations to the referenced object. A getter may return the same object, allowing a caller to change one of its properties and thereby alter the shared state. If that behavior is undesirable, the boundary must be strengthened. Possible strategies include returning a defensive copy, returning a wrapper that exposes read-only properties, or providing explicit mutation operations. The appropriate choice depends on whether clients are expected to share updates and on the depth and complexity of the data structure.
Study instruction: - Write a test that obtains the value twice, mutates a property through the first result, and checks whether the second result changes.
- Decide whether shared mutation is part of the contract or should be prevented.
Key point: - Encapsulating a reference is not the same as encapsulating the object's contents.
- Test the mutation behavior clients can observe.
30. Q: When should a getter return a defensive copy rather than the shared object?
A: Show answer
A getter should return a defensive copy when callers may inspect or manipulate the returned value but should not be allowed to change the shared stored state. This is particularly useful for lists and record-like values exposed to many clients. A shallow copy protects only the outer object; nested objects may still be shared and mutable. A deep copy is needed when nested state must also be isolated, although it can be more expensive and may not work for every object type. Defensive copying can reveal hidden assumptions in existing clients, so tests should identify code that intentionally relied on mutating shared state before the boundary is tightened.
Study instruction: - Create tests for both top-level and nested property mutation through a getter result.
- Specify whether the contract requires a shallow copy, deep copy, or read-only wrapper, and test that contract.
Key point: - Copies can prevent accidental shared-state mutation.
- Understand copy depth and compatibility before changing getter behavior.
31. Q: Why might a wrapper object be preferable to returning a copy?
A: Show answer
A wrapper can expose selected properties through read-only accessors while keeping the underlying mutable record private. This can make attempts to assign protected properties fail or become observable, depending on the language and wrapper design. A copy instead gives clients a separate value that they can freely change without affecting the original. A wrapper is useful when clients need a domain-oriented interface and the system wants to control which operations are available. Neither technique automatically solves deep immutability unless nested values are also protected. The design should express whether clients receive a snapshot, a read-only view, or a controlled participant in shared state.
Study instruction: - Design a read-only view for a record with several properties and compare it with returning a copy.
- Identify what happens when a client attempts to update both a top-level field and a nested field.
Key point: - Copies provide isolation; wrappers provide controlled access.
- Make the intended mutability contract explicit at the boundary.
32. Q: What is Rename Variable, and why is naming especially important for long-lived state?
A: Show answer
Rename Variable changes a variable's declaration and all of its references to a name that better expresses its role. Good names are central to readable code because variables carry the concepts that connect expressions and functions. The importance of a name grows with its scope and lifetime: a terse name may be acceptable in a tiny lambda where context is obvious, but a persistent field or shared variable needs a more informative name. A name may become wrong as the developer's understanding or the program's purpose changes. Renaming promptly prevents later readers from repeatedly reconstructing the intended meaning.
Study instruction: - Select variables at three scopes: a short lambda, a function parameter, and a persistent field.
- Propose names for each and explain why the wider-scope variable needs more context in its name.
Key point: - Name quality should reflect scope and lifetime.
- Rename when understanding improves rather than preserving misleading historical names.
33. Q: Why should a widely used variable often be encapsulated before it is renamed?
A: Show answer
A widely used mutable variable may have references scattered across many modules. Renaming all of them in one step can be risky, especially when the language or tooling cannot distinguish references reliably. Encapsulate Variable creates getter and setter functions first, so clients depend on a stable function interface rather than the storage name. The underlying variable can then be renamed behind that boundary without changing every caller. The accessors can remain in place afterward, preserving the reduced coupling for future changes. This is particularly valuable for mutable shared state; a local temporary usually needs only a direct rename and test.
Study instruction: - Trace a shared variable's read and write references and decide whether a direct rename is manageable.
- If not, encapsulate first, rename the private storage, and verify that clients use only the accessor functions.
Key point: - Use encapsulation to reduce the blast radius of a broad rename.
- Keep the boundary if it continues to protect shared state.
34. Q: How can a constant or read-only exported value be renamed gradually?
A: Show answer
When a value does not change, it can often be renamed by introducing a declaration with the new name and making the old name refer to it. Clients can then migrate from the old name to the new one over several steps. After all references have moved, remove the compatibility declaration. This avoids the need to encapsulate immutable data solely to support a rename. The technique also applies to values that are effectively read-only to clients even if the underlying language does not enforce full immutability. The copy should represent the same value, and tests should confirm that both names behave consistently during the transition.
Study instruction: - Create a new constant name while retaining the old name as a compatibility alias.
- Migrate a subset of references, test, then remove the alias only after a search confirms that it is unused.
Key point: - Stable values can often be renamed through a temporary alias.
- Remove the old name only after every reference has migrated.
35. Q: What is a data clump, and how does Introduce Parameter Object address it?
A: Show answer
A data clump is a recurring group of values that travel together through several function calls. For example, a minimum and maximum temperature may repeatedly appear as separate parameters even though they represent one range. Introduce Parameter Object creates a data structure that groups those related values and replaces the repeated parameter group with that object. This makes the relationship explicit, shortens parameter lists, and gives callers and callees a consistent vocabulary. More importantly, the new structure can become the foundation for domain behavior, such as checking whether a value falls within a range. The refactoring therefore creates an opportunity for a stronger abstraction.
Study instruction: - Search for functions that repeatedly accept the same pair or group of values.
- Explain what real-world concept the group represents and design a named object for that concept.
Key point: - Repeated parameter groups often reveal a missing domain concept.
- Group related values to make their relationship explicit and reusable.
36. Q: Why does Introduce Parameter Object usually use a class rather than an unstructured record?
A: Show answer
A class provides a natural place to add behavior related to the grouped values. A range object, for example, can expose its minimum and maximum and also answer whether a candidate value lies within the range. A class can also enforce construction rules and value-object semantics, such as treating two ranges with the same bounds as equivalent. A plain record may be adequate when no behavior is needed, but a class makes the next design step easier when the data represents a meaningful concept. The choice should not be ceremonial: the new type should express a relationship that matters to the program.
Study instruction: - Turn a recurring pair of dates into a date-range type and list the operations that naturally belong to it.
- Decide whether construction should validate ordering and whether instances should be mutable or value-like.
Key point: - Prefer a class when the grouped data has meaningful behavior or invariants.
- The new type should represent a concept, not merely hide a long parameter list.
37. Q: What is the safe migration sequence for replacing several parameters with a parameter object?
A: Show answer
Create the new structure if one does not already exist and test it independently. Add the new object as an additional function parameter while retaining the old parameters, then update each caller to construct and pass the correct object. Once the new argument is present everywhere, replace uses of the old parameters inside the function with fields or accessors from the object. Remove the old parameters one at a time, testing after each change. This staged sequence keeps behavior stable while the interface evolves. Adding the object and changing its use in separate steps also makes it easier to identify mistakes in construction or property mapping.
Study instruction: - Apply the sequence to a function that accepts minimum and maximum values.
- Keep the old parameters temporarily, update callers, switch one field at a time, and test after each migration step.
Key point: - Introduce the new object before removing old arguments.
- Migrate callers and parameter use incrementally.
38. Q: How can a parameter object evolve into a richer domain abstraction?
A: Show answer
After the repeated values are grouped, inspect how callers and functions use them. Common operations may belong on the new object, allowing callers to ask it a domain question instead of reconstructing the logic. A range can implement a contains operation, and callers can then use that operation instead of comparing against minimum and maximum values themselves. Other opportunities may include value-based equality, validation, normalization, or comparison. The object becomes valuable because it centralizes rules that otherwise remain scattered. This evolution should be driven by repeated domain behavior rather than by a desire to add methods to every data structure.
Study instruction: - Identify duplicated operations that use the new parameter object's fields.
- Move one coherent operation onto the object and update callers to use the domain-level method.
Key point: - Parameter objects can become domain abstractions.
- Move behavior when it belongs naturally to the grouped data.
39. Q: What is Combine Functions into Class, and what code pattern suggests using it?
A: Show answer
Combine Functions into Class groups functions that operate closely on the same body of data into a class that owns or represents that data. A common signal is that several functions repeatedly receive the same record and derive related values from it. The class makes the shared environment explicit, reduces repeated parameter passing within the group, and gives clients a single object to collaborate with. It also provides a natural place to add further calculations or validation associated with the data. The refactoring is especially useful when the source data may change, because derived values can be computed from the current object state rather than stored as potentially stale fields.
Study instruction: - Find two or more functions that accept the same record and calculate related results.
- Identify the data and behavior that belong together and sketch a class interface for clients.
Key point: - Cohesive functions and shared data are candidates for a class.
- The class creates an explicit home for related behavior.
40. Q: Why is Encapsulate Record often a preparation step for Combine Functions into Class?
A: Show answer
Functions can be moved more safely when the common data already has a controlled representation. Encapsulate Record replaces direct access to a raw record with an object or other encapsulated interface. Once the data's fields are accessed through that interface, calculations can be moved into the class without exposing every internal field to clients. If the data is not already grouped, Introduce Parameter Object can first create a common record. The resulting class becomes a clear owner of the data and its related operations. This sequence reduces coupling between clients and the original storage layout.
Study instruction: - Take a raw record used by several calculations and define a class with accessors for its meaningful fields.
- Move one calculation into the class and verify that clients no longer depend on the raw record layout.
Key point: - Encapsulate common data before moving its behavior.
- Stable accessors make structural changes easier to stage.
41. Q: How are functions moved into a class during Combine Functions into Class?
A: Show answer
After the shared data has been encapsulated, take each function that uses the common record and move it into the new class. Arguments that are now available as members or accessors can be removed from the method's parameter list. Additional computations can be extracted into functions first and then moved as methods. Client code constructs or receives the new object and calls the relevant behavior through it. This should be performed incrementally, preserving the old calculation until the moved method is verified. The final design should make it obvious which data the class owns and which calculations are derived from that data.
Study instruction: - Move one function at a time from a module into the class that owns its input record.
- Remove parameters made redundant by object state and test both the method and its client.
Key point: - Move behavior close to the data it operates on.
- Remove redundant parameters only when the class now provides the same information.
42. Q: How can derived properties on a class support mutable source data?
A: Show answer
A class can expose derived values through methods or getters that calculate from the object's current stored data. If the underlying reading or order changes, the derived property is recomputed when accessed, so it does not remain stale merely because an earlier calculation was cached in a separate field. This is one reason a class is useful when clients may mutate core data. The class can also control updates and enforce invariants. If calculations are expensive, caching may be considered, but then the class must manage invalidation correctly. The simple on-demand approach keeps the relationship between source values and derived behavior explicit.
Study instruction: - Build a class with a stored quantity and a derived total, then change the quantity and verify that the total reflects the new value.
- If adding caching, design a test for invalidation after every relevant mutation.
Key point: - On-demand derived properties stay aligned with current object state.
- Cached derivations require a deliberate invalidation strategy.
43. Q: What is the difference between Combine Functions into Class and simply placing functions in a utility module?
A: Show answer
A utility module may collect functions by broad technical category while leaving the data they operate on elsewhere. Combine Functions into Class establishes a stronger relationship: the class represents the data and owns the calculations that are meaningful for that data. This can reduce repeated arguments, improve discoverability, and make the domain concept visible to clients. A utility module may still be appropriate for stateless operations that do not belong to a particular object. The deciding question is whether the functions form a cohesive unit around a shared data model or merely happen to be convenient to store together.
Study instruction: - Compare a collection of unrelated formatting helpers with several calculations over one reading record.
- Decide which group benefits from a class and which remains better as stateless functions.
Key point: - Group by domain cohesion, not merely by technical convenience.
- Use utility functions when no meaningful data owner exists.
44. Q: What is Combine Functions into Transform, and what does the transformation produce?
A: Show answer
Combine Functions into Transform creates a function that accepts a source record, copies it, calculates selected derived values, adds those values to the copy, and returns the enriched record. The calculations are gathered in one place so clients can reuse consistent derivations instead of repeating formulas or depending on scattered helper functions. A transform is especially convenient in a functional or data-pipeline style where records are treated as read-only inputs and enriched outputs flow to later processing. The transformation should make its boundary clear: it returns a new record with additional information rather than silently changing the source.
Study instruction: - Identify two calculations repeated against the same input record.
- Design an enrichment function that returns the original fields plus named derived fields, and document which clients should use the enriched result.
Key point: - A transform centralizes related derivations in one data-processing step.
- Its contract should distinguish the source record from the enriched result.
45. Q: Why should a transformation function usually copy its input record?
A: Show answer
Copying the input prevents the transformation from unexpectedly modifying data that callers may still use. The function can then add derived fields to its private result and return that result, preserving the caller's original record. A deep copy is often used when nested data must also remain isolated. The exact copy strategy depends on the data structure and its mutability contract. Since callers may assume that enrichment is non-destructive, a test should compare the original input with a saved copy after the transform runs. The test protects an important behavioral promise that is not necessarily obvious from the function's name.
Study instruction: - Save a deep copy of a representative input record, run the transformation, and assert that the original is unchanged.
- Include a nested object in the test so that shallow-copy behavior cannot pass unnoticed.
Key point: - Copying protects the input from accidental mutation.
- Test non-mutation explicitly, especially for nested data.
46. Q: When is mutation acceptable inside a transformation function?
A: Show answer
A transform can use mutation on the newly created result object while it is being assembled, even when it preserves the original input. This keeps the implementation straightforward without exposing those intermediate changes to other parts of the program. The important boundary is that the original record remains unchanged and the returned enriched result is treated according to a clear contract. This is different from mutating shared source data. Immutability at boundaries can provide strong safety while local mutation inside a private scope remains practical. The implementation should make ownership of the result obvious and avoid leaking partially constructed state.
Study instruction: - Implement an enrichment function that copies an input and adds two derived fields to the copy.
