- Verify that the source remains unchanged and that only the completed result is returned.
Key point: - Local mutation of a private result can coexist with a non-mutating public contract.
- Keep ownership and exposure boundaries explicit.
47. Q: What is the main risk of storing derived fields in an enriched record?
A: Show answer
If a client changes a source field after enrichment, the stored derived fields may no longer correspond to the source data. For example, changing quantity while retaining a previously calculated base charge creates an inconsistent record. This is the central weakness of transforms when source data remains mutable. The design is safer when records are read-only after enrichment or when the programming environment supports immutable data structures. If clients need to update source data and expect derivations to remain current, a class with on-demand derived properties is often a better choice. Recomputing the transform after every change is another possibility, but the ownership and update rules must be explicit.
Study instruction: - Enrich a record, mutate one source field, and demonstrate how a stored derived field can become stale.
- Decide whether the application should prohibit mutation, recompute enrichment, or use a class with derived getters.
Key point: - Mutable source data can invalidate stored derivations.
- Choose transforms primarily when the enriched record is read-only or immutability is enforced.
48. Q: How should a team choose between Combine Functions into Transform and Combine Functions into Class?
A: Show answer
Choose a class when the source data may be updated and derived values should reflect the current state through methods or getters. Choose a transform when data is naturally processed as an input and output record, especially when the source is read-only or immutable. A transform centralizes derivations in a pipeline step and returns enriched data; a class binds data and behavior into one object and can manage mutation. Existing program style also matters: a functional pipeline may make transforms easier to integrate, while an object-oriented model may make a class more natural. The key decision is how the source data changes and who owns consistency between source and derived values.
Study instruction: - Compare a reporting pipeline with a mutable order-management object.
- For each, identify data ownership, mutation points, and the risk of stale derivations before choosing a transform or class.
Key point: - Mutable state generally favors a class with derived behavior.
- Read-only data pipelines often favor transforms.
49. Q: What is Split Phase, and what kinds of mixed responsibilities does it separate?
A: Show answer
Split Phase divides code that performs two distinct kinds of processing into sequential phases, each with a clear responsibility and module boundary. One common case is converting raw input into a convenient structured representation before performing business calculations. Another is separating calculations that use different data and rules, such as product pricing and shipping. The split allows changes to one concern without requiring the developer to understand the details of the other. Compilers provide a larger example: tokenizing, parsing, transforming, and code generation are distinct stages. The technique applies at smaller scales whenever the code has naturally sequential responsibilities.
Study instruction: - Inspect a function that parses input and then calculates a result.
- Identify which statements belong to preparation and which belong to the main computation, then define a boundary between them.
Key point: - Separate sequential responsibilities that change for different reasons.
- Each phase should have a clear input and output.
50. Q: What clues indicate that code should be separated into phases?
A: Show answer
A useful clue is that different parts of a fragment use different sets of data, functions, or rules. The first part may normalize, parse, or organize input, while the second part performs calculations using the normalized form. Another clue is that upcoming changes are likely to affect one stage independently of the other. If the code mixes transport parsing, domain rules, and output formatting, a phase boundary may reduce the number of concerns a developer must hold in mind. The split is not justified merely because two blocks occur sequentially; each stage should represent a meaningful responsibility with a useful interface.
Study instruction: - Mark every variable and helper used in a mixed function, grouping them by the stage that needs them.
- Identify whether the groups reveal distinct responsibilities or whether the apparent split is only cosmetic.
Key point: - Different dependency sets often reveal separate phases.
- Split where responsibilities and likely change patterns genuinely differ.
51. Q: What role does the intermediate data structure play in Split Phase?
A: Show answer
The intermediate data structure is the explicit contract between the phases. The first phase creates or populates it with values needed by the second phase, and the second phase consumes it rather than depending on all the temporary variables and raw inputs of the original function. This makes the information crossing the boundary visible and reduces a long parameter list. It also helps reveal which values belong to the first phase, which are external inputs to the second, and which are truly shared. The structure should contain the data needed for the next stage, not become an indiscriminate container for every variable in the original function.
Study instruction: - Design an intermediate record for a pricing workflow and list each field with the phase that produces it and the phase that consumes it.
- Remove any field that is not needed across the boundary.
Key point: - The intermediate record makes phase communication explicit.
- Include only the information required by the next stage.
52. Q: What are the mechanics for applying Split Phase safely?
A: Show answer
First extract the second phase into its own function, passing the values it currently needs, and test. Introduce an intermediate data structure as an additional argument and test again. Examine each parameter of the second phase: if the first phase creates or uses that value, move it into the intermediate structure and remove the redundant parameter, testing after each move. Parameters that are used only by the second phase remain separate. Once the intermediate structure is complete, extract the first-phase code into a function that returns it. The original function then coordinates the two phases by passing the returned structure to the second.
Study instruction: - Refactor a function with preparation and calculation stages using the stated sequence.
- Keep a test checkpoint after extracting phase two, adding the structure, moving each field, and extracting phase one.
Key point: - Extract the consumer phase first, then establish the data contract.
- Move fields incrementally and test each boundary change.
53. Q: Why is it useful to pass all second-phase inputs first and reduce them later?
A: Show answer
When the second phase is initially extracted, passing its required values as individual parameters is a straightforward way to preserve behavior. The parameter list may be long, but it exposes the current data dependencies. The intermediate structure can then be introduced and populated gradually. As each parameter is examined, values produced by or belonging to the first phase can be moved into the structure, while values used only by the second phase remain independent. This staged reduction avoids guessing the final data contract too early. It also makes the refactoring mechanics observable and easier to test after each change.
Study instruction: - Begin with a deliberately explicit second-phase signature, then classify each parameter as first-phase output, shared input, or second-phase-only dependency.
- Move only the first-phase outputs into the intermediate object.
Key point: - Start with a behavior-preserving interface, then simplify it with evidence.
- Do not move second-phase-only dependencies into the intermediate record without a reason.
54. Q: How does a compiler illustrate the value of Split Phase?
A: Show answer
A compiler transforms source text into an executable representation through several stages with distinct responsibilities. Tokenization turns characters into tokens, parsing organizes tokens into a syntax structure, later transformations may analyze or optimize that structure, and code generation emits the target representation. Each phase has a defined input and output and can be reasoned about without understanding every detail of the others. This separation makes it possible to evolve a parser, an optimization pass, or a backend independently within the constraints of the intermediate representations. The example illustrates the broader principle that complex work becomes more manageable when meaningful stages are explicit.
Study instruction: - Sketch a compiler pipeline and identify the data representation passed between each pair of phases.
- Apply the same analysis to a smaller system such as request parsing followed by validation and business processing.
Key point: - Explicit intermediate representations support independent reasoning about stages.
- Phase boundaries reduce the amount of unrelated detail each stage must manage.
55. Q: How should a developer test that a refactoring preserves behavior?
A: Show answer
Tests should verify the externally relevant behavior before and after each structural change, not merely confirm that the new function can be called. Focused tests should cover the values crossing new boundaries, mutation behavior, evaluation order, and important edge cases. For a non-mutating transform, compare the original input with a saved copy. For an extracted calculation, verify that returned values match the prior calculation. For a signature migration, exercise both the temporary compatibility path and the new interface until all callers have moved. Static analysis and compilation add useful checks but do not replace behavioral tests. Frequent checkpoints make failures easier to localize.
Study instruction: - Select one refactoring from the chapter and write tests for its key invariant before changing the code.
- Run the tests after each mechanical step and record exactly which behavior each test protects.
Key point: - Refactoring should preserve observable behavior at every safe checkpoint.
- Tests must cover data flow and side effects, not just syntax.
56. Q: What are common failure modes when applying the chapter's refactorings too aggressively?
A: Show answer
Common failures include extracting a function with too many reassigned locals, inlining a polymorphic method, moving a side-effecting expression into a variable that changes evaluation timing, renaming shared state without locating every reference, and storing derived fields that become stale after mutation. A rushed signature change can also leave callers using incompatible arguments, while a poorly chosen parameter object can merely hide a long list without representing a meaningful concept. These failures share a pattern: the developer changes structure without first understanding scope, ownership, dynamic dispatch, or the behavior that must remain stable. Small steps and focused tests address much of this risk.
Study instruction: - Review a proposed refactoring and create a risk checklist covering scope, side effects, polymorphism, callers, mutation, and derived data.
- Identify the single highest-risk assumption and design a test or static check for it.
Key point: - Most refactoring failures come from hidden dependencies or changed behavior.
- Make assumptions explicit before changing boundaries.
57. Q: How do the local refactorings in this chapter support larger architectural change?
A: Show answer
Extract Function and Extract Variable create meaningful names for local behavior. Change Function Declaration and Rename Variable improve the interfaces and vocabulary through which code communicates. Encapsulate Variable protects data boundaries, while Introduce Parameter Object reveals concepts that recur across function calls. Combine Functions into Class or Transform then organizes related behavior around those concepts, and Split Phase separates larger processing responsibilities. Each small step can make a later move or redesign easier because it clarifies dependencies and ownership. The chapter's techniques therefore work as a progression from local readability to more deliberate modular structure without requiring a single disruptive rewrite.
Study instruction: - Choose a small messy function and sketch a sequence of two or three refactorings that would reveal a larger domain abstraction.
- Explain which intermediate tests and boundaries make the later architectural move safer.
Key point: - Local clarity creates leverage for larger structural change.
- Build architecture through tested, incremental improvements.
58. Q: How can a developer decide whether to extract now, inline first, or introduce a broader abstraction?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying what currently makes the code difficult to understand or change. If a fragment has a clear purpose but is buried in a larger function, Extract Function may be the right first move. If the existing helper adds no meaning or creates excessive delegation, Inline Function may expose the real structure before a better extraction. If a complex expression needs a local explanation, Extract Variable is smaller and more appropriate. If the concept is shared across functions or tied to a data model, consider a parameter object, class, or transform. If two stages have distinct inputs and rules, Split Phase may be the stronger boundary. The smallest useful change is usually the safest starting point.
Study instruction: - For three code smells, select the smallest refactoring that directly addresses each one.
- State what evidence would justify moving from a local change to a broader abstraction.
Key point: - Match the refactoring to the scale of the problem.
- Prefer the smallest step that creates a meaningful improvement.
59. Q: What practical review questions help determine whether a refactoring has improved the design?
A: Show answer
A useful review asks whether names now reveal intent, whether callers contain less irrelevant detail, whether dependencies are explicit, whether shared mutable data has a controlled boundary, and whether related behavior is easier to find. It should also ask whether the new abstraction has a coherent responsibility, whether parameter lists represent real concepts, and whether phase boundaries pass a clear data contract. Finally, reviewers should check that tests demonstrate preserved behavior and that the refactoring has not introduced needless wrappers, stale derived values, or duplicated state. A refactoring is successful when future changes become easier and the code's structure better reflects the concepts it implements.
Study instruction: - Review a completed refactoring using the questions in the answer.
- Record one measurable readability improvement and one remaining risk or opportunity rather than judging the change only by line count.
Key point: - Evaluate clarity, cohesion, dependency direction, and behavioral safety.
- Better design is measured by easier reasoning and change, not fewer lines.

Encapsulation for Maintainable Software Design
1. Q: Why is encapsulation a central principle when decomposing software modules?
A: Show answer
Encapsulation identifies details that a module should control rather than expose to the rest of the system. Data structures are common hidden details, but the principle also applies to primitive values, temporary calculations, class responsibilities, object relationships, and algorithms. When clients depend only on a stable interface, internal changes affect fewer parts of the system. This reduces coupling, makes change safer, and gives a clear place to enforce invariants and coordinate updates.
Study instruction:
- "Review a module you use and list the implementation details that clients currently need to know."

2. Q: When should mutable records be converted into objects?
A: Show answer
A mutable record is a candidate when many parts of the program read or change its fields, when its structure is implicit, or when field access must be governed by rules. A class can hide storage, expose named accessors, validate changes, and provide behavior that belongs with the data. An immutable value that is created once and not subsequently changed may remain a simple record, provided its structure is clear and safe to share. The decision is about controlling change, not converting every data structure into a class.
Study instruction:
- "Identify whether a record is mutable, widely shared, structurally implicit, or governed by business rules before selecting the refactoring."

3. Q: What problem does Encapsulate Record solve?
A: Show answer
Encapsulate Record replaces a publicly manipulated record with an object that hides its raw storage and exposes accessors or domain-specific operations. The record may initially be wrapped so existing behavior can continue while callers are migrated. Over time, readers and writers are changed to use the object interface, and the raw-record access path is removed. This makes field renaming, validation, calculated values, and later structural changes possible without requiring every client to understand the underlying representation.
Study instruction:
- "Trace all readers and writers of a shared record and identify which accessors or operations would form its replacement interface."

4. Q: Why does Encapsulate Record begin with a temporary raw-data accessor?
A: Show answer
A deliberately searchable accessor such as getRawDataOfOrganization creates a migration seam. It gives existing callers one identifiable route to the record, allowing the record to be wrapped in a class without requiring a disruptive one-step rewrite. Each caller can then be moved to an object getter or setter and tested independently. Because the accessor exposes the implementation detail that the refactoring is intended to hide, it should be temporary and removed when no caller needs it.
Study instruction:
- "Practice a staged migration: introduce one searchable raw accessor, wrap the record, migrate a small group of callers, and test each change."

5. Q: Why should updates to a nested record be encapsulated before read access is redesigned?
A: Show answer
Updates are the most important first target because they alter the underlying state and can bypass the owning module's rules. A direct nested assignment, such as changing a customer's monthly usage through several map levels, should become a named operation on the data-owning object. Once writes are centralized, the system can reliably validate changes, preserve invariants, and reveal all mutation points. Read access can then be selected according to usability, performance, and the degree of protection required.
Study instruction:
- "Find every statement that changes nested data and design a named method for each meaningful update."
Key point:

6. Q: What options exist for providing read access to a deeply nested data structure?
A: Show answer
A class can expose specific query methods, return a defensive copy of the data, or provide a read-only view or proxy. Specific methods produce the clearest domain API and the strongest control but can become numerous when clients need many different views. Copies preserve a convenient collection-shaped interface but consume time and memory and can confuse callers who expect edits to affect the source. Read-only proxies can reflect current underlying values while blocking mutation, although their implementation and language support vary.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the expected read patterns, size of the structure, mutation risk, and performance cost before choosing a read-access strategy."
Key point:
- R

- Catalog of Refactorings
7. Q: What are the risks of returning a copy of nested data from an encapsulating object?
A: Show answer
A copy prevents clients from changing the original structure through the returned reference, but copying a large structure can be expensive. A shallow copy may also leave nested objects shared, so callers can still mutate deeper data unless those objects are copied or protected too. Callers may misunderstand the semantics and assume a change to the copy will update the source. The implementation should therefore define whether the copy is shallow or deep and document that returned values are detached from the original.
Study instruction:
- "Test both top-level and nested mutations against the original object, and measure copy cost using realistic data volumes."

8. Q: When is a read-only proxy preferable to copying a record?
A: Show answer
A read-only proxy can expose a familiar data-shaped interface while preventing callers from changing the underlying state. Unlike a copy, a proxy can reflect changes made by the owner after the proxy was obtained. It avoids duplicating the entire structure, but it introduces implementation complexity and must reliably block every relevant mutation route. In languages where immutable or read-only collection views are built in, a proxy or view may be preferable; otherwise, a defensive copy may be safer and easier to understand.
Study instruction:
- "Check whether the language or collection library provides a tested read-only view before implementing a custom proxy."

9. Q: How should encapsulation be applied recursively to nested records?
A: Show answer
When fields contain other records or collections, encapsulating only the outer reference may leave internal structures exposed. The design can recursively introduce record and collection wrappers, or use a mixture of domain operations and protected views for the parts that matter. Full recursive object modeling gives strong control but may create excessive classes and boilerplate. Apply it where the nested data has meaningful behavior, invariants, or independent change needs, rather than simply mirroring every JSON level.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one nested structure and decide which levels need their own abstraction based on update rules and domain meaning."

10. Q: Why is a getter that returns a mutable collection not sufficient encapsulation?
A: Show answer
A getter may hide the collection field while still returning the actual collection object. Callers can then add, remove, reorder, or replace elements without the owning class knowing that its state has changed. This bypasses validation and any rules that should apply to collection membership. Proper encapsulation controls both the reference and the collection's contents, usually through explicit operations and a protected read interface.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect collection getters and identify whether a caller can mutate the live collection returned by the getter."

11. Q: How does Encapsulate Collection establish control over membership changes?
A: Show answer
Encapsulate Collection adds owner methods such as addCourse and removeCourse, then changes callers to use those methods instead of calling push, splice, or other mutators on the returned collection. The owner can validate elements, enforce uniqueness, maintain related state, and decide what happens when removal is requested for an absent item. Once direct mutation has been eliminated, the getter can return a copy or read-only view. The objective is to make the owner the sole authority for membership changes.
Study instruction:
- "Replace direct collection mutators in a sample class with named owner methods and test each mutation path."

12. Q: What should a collection owner do when a client tries to remove an element that is absent?
A: Show answer
The owner must define predictable semantics for this case. It may treat the request as a harmless no-op, or it may signal an error when absence indicates a programming mistake or violated expectation. The chapter's example defaults to raising a range error but allows a caller-supplied alternative action. Whichever contract is chosen should be explicit, consistently applied, and covered by tests so callers do not have to guess how removal behaves.
Study instruction:
- "Write tests for removal of an existing element, an absent element, and repeated removal of the same element."

13. Q: Should a collection setter remain after add and remove methods are introduced?
A: Show answer
A setter that replaces the entire collection may be unnecessary once callers can perform controlled additions and removals. Removing it narrows the API and prevents bulk replacement from bypassing owner rules. If bulk replacement is a legitimate use case, the setter should take a defensive copy and may validate the complete replacement before committing it. The choice depends on whether replacement is a real domain operation or merely a convenience that exposes internal state.
Study instruction:
- "Search for collection assignment sites and decide whether each should become a sequence of owner operations or a validated bulk replacement."

14. Q: What is the trade-off between returning a copy and returning a read-only collection view?
A: Show answer
A copy isolates the caller from the owner's collection and is straightforward to reason about, but it incurs copying cost and does not reflect later owner changes. A read-only view can avoid copying and can show current membership, but it must truly block all mutation operations and may expose language-specific complexity. The correct choice depends on collection size, access duration, expected freshness, and the language's collection facilities. Teams should use one consistent convention so callers understand the semantics of every accessor.
Study instruction:
- "Benchmark representative collection sizes and write tests that confirm callers cannot mutate owner state through the chosen accessor."

15. Q: Why should a codebase use one consistent collection-protection mechanism?
A: Show answer
If some getters return live collections, others return copies, and others return proxies, callers must remember different mutation and freshness rules for each class. This increases the likelihood of accidental changes and incorrect assumptions. A consistent convention reduces cognitive load and makes APIs easier to review. Exceptions may be justified by measured performance or domain needs, but they should be deliberate and visible rather than accidental.
Study instruction:
- "Review collection accessors across a codebase and document the standard policy, including any justified exceptions."

16. Q: When should a primitive value be replaced with a domain object?
A: Show answer
A string, number, or code should become an object when it begins to require behavior beyond simple storage or display. Examples include a priority that needs validation and ordering, a telephone number that needs formatting, or a measurement that needs unit-aware operations. The new class initially wraps the primitive, providing a place to add behavior without distributing it across many callers. This can eliminate duplicated rules and make the domain concept explicit in the type system.
Study instruction:
- "Find repeated validation, formatting, comparison, or interpretation logic around a primitive and determine whether it belongs in a value class."

17. Q: How does Replace Primitive with Object reduce duplicated logic?
A: Show answer
Without a domain object, each caller may independently validate, format, compare, or interpret the same string or number. These rules can drift as the system evolves. A value class centralizes the behavior so callers use the same comparison or conversion operation. The class can also reject invalid values when constructed, preventing invalid data from spreading. The result is more than a wrapper: it becomes a reusable unit of domain knowledge.
Study instruction:
- "Search for duplicated operations on one primitive and design a single object method that replaces those operations."

18. Q: Why might a domain object's conversion method be clearer than a simple value getter?
A: Show answer
A conversion method such as toString communicates that the caller is asking for a representation of the object, rather than accessing its underlying identity or state. This distinction matters when a class offers both a rich domain object and a textual form for display or comparison. The surrounding API should make clear whether a caller receives a Priority object or a string describing a priority. Clear names help prevent code from silently depending on a primitive representation.
Study instruction:

19. Q: What additional behavior can be added after a primitive becomes a value object?
A: Show answer
Once a primitive is wrapped, the new class can validate permitted values, provide comparison operations, define equality, and control conversion to strings. For a priority, the class might maintain an ordered set of legal values and expose higherThan, lowerThan, and equals operations. This makes client code express domain intent instead of repeating string comparisons. The value class should remain focused on behavior that belongs to that value rather than becoming a general-purpose service.

20. Q: How should equality and mutability influence the design of a new value object?
A: Show answer
A value object is generally understood by the value it represents rather than by a unique object identity. Its equality operation should therefore compare the relevant value, and it is often best made immutable so that its meaning cannot change unexpectedly after sharing. If identity and shared mutable state are important, the design may instead require a reference object. The book points to Change Reference to Value and Change Value to Reference as follow-on choices that clarify this distinction.
Study instruction:
- "Decide whether two separately created instances with the same underlying value should compare equal and whether mutation after creation is legitimate."
Key point:
- V
- a
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
21. Q: Which temporary variables are suitable for Replace Temp with Query?
A: Show answer
A temporary is a good candidate when its value is fully determined before use and can be recalculated consistently whenever requested. A variable that is assigned once is the simplest case, although several assignments may be extracted together if they form one calculation. The technique is unsuitable when the variable intentionally preserves a snapshot of earlier state, when its value changes between uses, or when recalculation would alter behavior. These conditions must be checked before replacing storage with a query.
Study instruction:
- "For each candidate temporary, record all assignments and uses and determine whether recomputing it produces the same result at every use."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
22. Q: How can Replace Temp with Query make a long function easier to decompose?
A: Show answer
A temporary variable can create a dependency between the calculation and every later part of a function that uses it. Extracting the calculation into a query gives it a named interface and can allow other methods to call it without receiving the temporary as a parameter. This reduces the amount of local state that must be passed when extracting functions and can reveal clearer boundaries within a large method. The technique works especially well when the class provides the shared context needed by the extracted query.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a long function with derived temporaries and identify which calculations could become stable queries on the owning object."
Key point:
- Q
- u
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
23. Q: Why must an extracted query be free of side effects?
A: Show answer
A query should report information without changing program state. If calling it changes data, repeated calls may produce different results or create behavior that did not exist when the value was calculated once. Replace Temp with Query may cause the new function to be invoked at several locations, so hidden side effects can be multiplied. Where a calculation is mixed with modification, Separate Query from Modifier should be considered before replacing the temporary.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect the extracted calculation for assignments, I/O, mutation, or calls that change state; separate those operations before using it as a query."
Key point:
- Q
- u
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
24. Q: What test sequence makes Replace Temp with Query safer?
A: Show answer
First confirm that the temporary is assigned before use and is not serving as a historical snapshot. If possible, make it constant so missed reassignments become visible. Extract the right-hand-side calculation into a method, verify that the method has no side effects, and run tests. Then inline the local variable so callers use the query, and repeat the process for other suitable temporaries. Testing after each small step helps isolate a defect to a specific transformation.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the steps to one temporary at a time and run focused tests after extraction and after inlining."
Key point:
- S
- m
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
25. Q: What signs indicate that a class should be split using Extract Class?
A: Show answer
A class is a candidate when it has grown difficult to understand and a subset of its data and methods forms a coherent responsibility. Fields that change together, methods that repeatedly use the same subset of fields, and behavior that can be removed together are useful clues. Subtyping that affects only part of a class can also reveal that the class contains more than one responsibility. The aim is not to enforce an arbitrary class-size limit but to produce clearer responsibilities and dependencies.
Study instruction:
- "Group fields and methods by the information they use and the reasons they change, then test whether a coherent secondary responsibility emerges."
Key point:
- C
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
26. Q: How can a developer determine which responsibility to extract from a large class?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying the responsibilities that the class currently serves, then look for data and operations that naturally belong together. Ask what other fields and methods would become meaningless if a particular field or method were removed. Consider which features change for different reasons and whether subclasses affect only a subset of behavior. These questions help reveal a meaningful boundary instead of arbitrarily moving code to a new class.
Study instruction:
- "Create a responsibility map for a large class and justify the proposed split using cohesive data, methods, and change drivers."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
27. Q: Why should Extract Class move lower-level methods before higher-level methods?
A: Show answer
Lower-level methods are those that are called by other methods rather than orchestrating many operations themselves. Moving them first can establish the new class's basic behavior and reduce dependencies before moving higher-level operations. The new class is created and linked from the original, then fields and methods are moved incrementally with tests after each move. Interfaces are reviewed at the end so delegating or obsolete methods can be removed and names can reflect the new responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Order a proposed extraction by moving foundational fields and low-level operations first, then move methods that depend on them."
Key point:
- M
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
28. Q: Should an extracted child object be exposed to clients?
A: Show answer
Exposing the new object can make its behavior reusable and give clients a direct domain abstraction, as with a telephone number. However, exposing it also makes clients aware of that object's interface and lifecycle. If clients should treat it as a value, Change Reference to Value may help establish safe value semantics before exposure. If the object should remain an internal implementation detail, the original class can retain a narrow interface and hide the child.
Study instruction:
- "List the clients that would benefit from direct access and identify the coupling introduced by exposing the new object."
Key point:
- E
- x
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
29. Q: When is Inline Class preferable to keeping a small class?
A: Show answer
Inline Class is useful when a class no longer carries a meaningful independent responsibility, perhaps because refactoring moved most of its behavior elsewhere. It can also simplify a larger reorganization in which two classes will eventually be separated along different boundaries. Combining them temporarily may make it easier to move individual fields and methods before extracting a better structure. A class should not be retained merely because it once had a purpose.
Study instruction:
- "Review a small class and determine whether it still has an independent reason to change or merely forwards data and behavior."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
30. Q: How can Inline Class be performed without breaking clients?
A: Show answer
Create delegating methods on the target class for the source class's public interface. Change clients to use those delegators and test each migration. Then move the source class's methods and data into the target incrementally, testing after each move, until the source class is empty and can be deleted. The staged approach preserves a working interface during the transition and limits the scope of each change.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one method and one field from a candidate class and plan the sequence of delegation, caller migration, transfer, and deletion."
Key point:
- K
- e
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
31. Q: How are Extract Class and Inline Class related during structural redesign?
A: Show answer
Extract Class separates a cohesive set of responsibilities into a new class, while Inline Class collapses a class that no longer has a useful independent role. They are complementary tools for changing where responsibilities live. Sometimes it is easier to combine two contexts first and then extract a new set of responsibilities; at other times moving one feature at a time is clearer. The direction should be chosen according to which sequence provides the simplest safe path to the desired design.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch both possible sequences for a proposed redesign and compare the number of intermediate dependencies and tests needed."
Key point:
- E
- x
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
32. Q: What dependency does Hide Delegate remove?
A: Show answer
When a client calls through an object to reach an object stored inside it, the client knows about both the server and its delegate. A change to the delegate's interface can then affect every client that navigates through the server. Hide Delegate adds a forwarding method on the server and changes clients to call that method. The dependency on the delegate is then concentrated in the server, which is responsible for adapting to the delegate's internal changes.
Study instruction:
- "Find message chains that reveal a server's internal object and replace one chain with a server-level operation."
Key point:
- H
- i
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
33. Q: What is the safe migration sequence for Hide Delegate?
A: Show answer
For each delegate method needed by clients, create a corresponding delegating method on the server. Change clients to call the server's new method, testing after each change. Once no client needs the delegate accessor, remove that accessor and test again. This staged migration avoids changing all callers and the object relationship in one operation. The interface should expose only the operations clients actually need rather than duplicating the entire delegate by default.
Study instruction:
- "Use a search for delegate references to verify that clients no longer navigate through the server to reach the delegate."
Key point:
- A
- d
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
34. Q: When can Hide Delegate improve changeability in an object model?
A: Show answer
Hide Delegate is valuable when clients repeatedly navigate through an object's fields to reach another object's behavior. If the delegate's structure changes, the server can absorb that change while client code remains stable. It is particularly useful when the server is the natural domain entry point for the requested operation. It should not become a reason to create dozens of pass-through methods that add no coherent responsibility.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a message chain used by multiple callers and evaluate whether the server is the right owner for the requested behavior."
Key point:
- H
- i
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
35. Q: Why can too much delegation create a Middle Man problem?
A: Show answer
Every delegating method has a maintenance cost and expands the server's public interface. If clients continually need new delegate capabilities, the server may become a thin forwarding layer that contributes little behavior of its own. This can make the code harder to navigate and forces the server to mirror another object's interface. The design should be reviewed as the system evolves rather than treating maximal hiding as an absolute rule.
Study instruction:
- "Count pass-through methods on a server and determine which express real domain behavior versus forwarding with no added value."
Key point:
- E
- n
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
36. Q: How does Remove Middle Man reverse Hide Delegate?
A: Show answer
Remove Middle Man exposes the delegate through an accessor and changes clients from calling the server's forwarding methods to using the delegate directly. Once all callers have moved, the unused delegating methods can be deleted. This reduces interface bloat when the server no longer adds value to the delegated operations. The choice is not permanent: system changes can make hiding useful again, or make a previously useful wrapper cumbersome.
Study instruction:
- "Select one forwarding method and migrate a caller through the delegate accessor, testing before removing the forwarding method."
Key point:
- R
- e
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
37. Q: How should a team decide between Hide Delegate and Remove Middle Man?
A: Show answer
The decision depends on which object should own the responsibility and how much knowledge clients should have about internal relationships. Hide Delegate is appropriate when the server provides a meaningful stable entry point and should shield clients from the delegate. Remove Middle Man is appropriate when the server only relays calls and the delegate is the natural object for clients to use. A mixture is reasonable when some delegate operations are common enough to deserve forwarding while others are better accessed directly.
Study instruction:
- "For each delegated operation, identify the domain owner and the client knowledge required; avoid applying one rule indiscriminately."
Key point:
- T
- h
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
38. Q: When is Substitute Algorithm preferable to incremental edits inside an existing algorithm?
A: Show answer
Substitute Algorithm is useful when a clearer or more suitable approach has become available, perhaps because the problem is better understood or a library now provides the needed capability. Small edits to a tangled algorithm may be harder to reason about than replacing the whole implementation. The existing method should first be decomposed so its behavior is understandable and the replacement can be compared accurately. The objective is to improve the implementation while preserving externally observable behavior unless a separate intentional behavior change is being made.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an algorithm that has become unnecessarily complex and evaluate whether a clearer approach can be expressed as one complete function."
Key point:
- R
- e
Source links:
- Refactoring by Martin Fowler
39. Q: How can tests establish confidence when substituting an algorithm?
A: Show answer
Create tests that exercise the old function's observable behavior before replacing it. Implement the alternative behind the same function boundary, run static checks, and compare old and new outputs over representative cases. When results differ, retain the old implementation as a comparison reference while investigating the discrepancy. Tests should include normal cases, boundaries, empty inputs, unusual values, and error conditions that the original algorithm handled. A successful substitution demonstrates equivalence for the tested behavior, not mathematical proof for every possible input.
Study instruction:
- "Build a test set from existing examples and boundary cases, then run both implementations against the same inputs."
Key point:
- B
- e
Source links:
- Refactoring by Martin Fowler
40. Q: Why should an algorithm be decomposed before a wholesale replacement?
A: Show answer
Large algorithms combine many decisions and side effects, making it difficult to understand which behavior must be preserved. Breaking the method into smaller functions clarifies its inputs, outputs, and responsibilities and makes targeted tests easier to create. Once the existing behavior is captured at a clean function boundary, the replacement can focus on one defined problem. This reduces the risk of confusing a design improvement with an unintended functional change.
Study instruction:
- "Extract cohesive steps from a complex algorithm before attempting a replacement and test each extracted behavior."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Refactoring by Martin Fowler
41. Q: How do the chapter's refactorings support a common strategy for controlling state?
A: Show answer
Encapsulate Record and Encapsulate Collection put data changes behind an owner; Replace Primitive with Object gives domain values a type and behavior; Replace Temp with Query turns stable calculations into named operations. Extract Class and Inline Class adjust responsibility boundaries, while Hide Delegate and Remove Middle Man adjust object relationships. Together, these techniques make state, behavior, and dependencies more explicit. They should be selected based on the design problem rather than applied mechanically.
Study instruction:
- "Map each refactoring to the state, responsibility, or dependency it is intended to control."
Key point:
- E
- n
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
42. Q: How should tests be used throughout encapsulation work?
A: Show answer
Tests should be run after each meaningful migration step: wrapping a record, changing an accessor, adding collection operations, moving a field, extracting a class, or replacing an algorithm. Tests help reveal callers that still depend on raw structures or behavior that changed during the move. Static checks can complement tests by detecting type or reference errors, but they do not replace behavioral verification. The smaller the change, the easier it is to identify and correct a failure.
Study instruction:
- "Create a test checkpoint for every change in an encapsulated interface and avoid combining unrelated structural moves into one untested edit."
Key point:
- I
- n
Source links:
- Refactoring by Martin Fowler
43. Q: What performance considerations arise when encapsulation introduces defensive copies?
A: Show answer
Copying collections or nested records can improve safety but adds allocation and processing cost. A large data structure copied for every read may become a meaningful bottleneck, while a small list may have negligible cost. Performance should be measured with representative workloads rather than assumed from intuition. If copying is expensive, consider a read-only view, narrower query methods, immutable data, or copies at less frequent boundaries, while preserving the required protection against mutation.
Study instruction:
- "Measure read frequency, collection size, allocation volume, and latency before replacing copies with a less protective interface."
Key point:
- D
- o
Source links:
- Encapsulated Collection by Martin Fowler
44. Q: How can aliasing undermine an otherwise encapsulated object?
A: Show answer
Aliasing occurs when code outside an object holds another reference to the same mutable structure stored inside it. Even if the object exposes only accessors, a caller that retained the original input object can change the data without using the object's methods. Copying incoming data during construction or assignment breaks this shared reference; returning copies or read-only views protects the outgoing boundary. Both directions matter: encapsulation must account for references entering and leaving the object.
Study instruction:
- "Test whether changing the constructor input after object creation changes the object's internal state, and whether changing returned data affects it."
Key point:
- P
- r
Source links:
- JavaScript Object.freeze documentation
45. Q: How can API compatibility be maintained while migrating callers to an encapsulated interface?
A: Show answer
A temporary wrapper or deprecated accessor can preserve existing behavior while callers are updated gradually. New accessors or domain methods are introduced first, then callers are migrated in small groups with tests. Usage searches or compiler references help identify remaining dependencies. Once migration is complete, the raw accessor or old interface can be removed. Keeping both interfaces indefinitely defeats the purpose and leaves clients free to depend on the representation being hidden.
Study instruction:
- "Plan a migration checklist that records old callers, new methods, tests, deprecation status, and the condition for removing the legacy accessor."
Key point:
- C
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
46. Q: What controls help verify that a collection is truly encapsulated?
A: Show answer
A review should check that clients cannot mutate the live collection through getters, retained references, setters, iterators, or nested objects. Static searches can locate direct mutators, while tests should attempt unauthorized additions, removals, sorting, and nested changes. The owner methods should be the only supported mutation path and should enforce the collection's invariants. For complex cases, read-only views or defensive copies must be verified for the language's actual semantics rather than assumed to be immutable.
Study instruction:
- "Create a mutation-path checklist and test each way an external client could try to change the collection."
Key point:
- E
- n
Source links:
- Encapsulated Collection by Martin Fowler
47. Q: How would you encapsulate a customer data map containing nested usage records?
A: Show answer
First wrap the map in a CustomerData class and centralize every usage update in a method such as setUsage(customerId, year, month, amount). Extract the nested assignment into a function, then move that function into the new class. After all updates are controlled, decide how clients should read usage: expose explicit query methods, a deep copy, a read-only view, or recursively encapsulated customer and usage objects. The choice should reflect how much of the map clients need and how costly full copying would be.
Study instruction:
- "Model a nested record, centralize one write operation, and compare explicit query methods with protected data views for reads."
Key point:
- C
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
48. Q: A client sorts a person's courses after reading the courses getter. What could go wrong?
A: Show answer
If the getter returns the live array, an in-place sort changes the owner's collection even though the client did not call an explicit update method. That can alter ordering for other clients and bypass any rules the Person class should enforce. Returning a copy prevents the sort from changing internal state; a read-only view can also block mutation if the language supports it. The class should expose separate domain operations if reordering is a legitimate change to the owned collection.
Study instruction:
- "Test the getter with in-place operations such as sorting, pushing, and splicing, then verify whether the internal collection changes."
Key point:
- M
- u
Source links:
- Encapsulated Collection by Martin Fowler
49. Q: A large class has telephone fields and methods mixed with unrelated person behavior. How should it be refactored?
A: Show answer
Create a TelephoneNumber class and instantiate it from Person. Move the telephone fields and related behavior one at a time, updating Person's accessors to delegate to the new object and testing after each move. Rename methods within TelephoneNumber to remove context-specific terms such as office when they no longer make sense. Decide whether clients should receive the telephone-number object directly or continue using Person's simpler interface. If exposed as a value, ensure its equality and immutability semantics are appropriate.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a cohesive data-and-method cluster in a large class and plan its extraction with caller compatibility in mind."
Key point:
- M
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
50. Q: A server class has many trivial forwarding methods to a delegate. What should the team consider?
A: Show answer
The team should determine whether those methods represent meaningful server-level operations or merely mirror the delegate's interface. If the server adds no domain value, expose an accessor and migrate clients to the delegate, then remove the redundant forwarding methods. If some operations are common and provide a useful stable entry point, keep only those delegations. The aim is not to maximize or minimize indirection but to place responsibility where clients can understand and maintain it.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each forwarding method as domain behavior, useful façade, or redundant pass-through before removing or retaining it."
Key point:
- R

Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
51. Q: What is the most important safeguard when replacing a complex algorithm with a simpler one?
A: Show answer
The key safeguard is to preserve the existing function's observable behavior unless a deliberate behavior change is separately specified. Establish a function boundary, capture its behavior with tests, implement the alternative, and compare both versions across representative and boundary inputs. When discrepancies appear, use the old algorithm as a diagnostic reference rather than immediately discarding it. Only after the differences are understood should the replacement become the production implementation.
Study instruction:
- "Before replacing an algorithm, build a regression suite that records current outputs and error behavior for representative inputs."
Key point:
- R
- e
Source links:
- Refactoring by Martin Fowler
52. Q: How can a developer choose the smallest effective refactoring from Chapter 7?
A: Show answer
Start with the design pressure: uncontrolled record mutation suggests Encapsulate Record; external collection changes suggest Encapsulate Collection; repeated primitive rules suggest Replace Primitive with Object; stable temporary calculations suggest Replace Temp with Query. A class with multiple cohesive responsibilities may need Extract Class, while a redundant class may need Inline Class. Message-chain coupling points toward Hide Delegate, interface bloat toward Remove Middle Man, and a convoluted implementation toward Substitute Algorithm. Choosing the narrowest technique that addresses the pressure keeps the change focused.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the observed design problem before naming a refactoring, then justify the selected technique against its alternatives."
Key point:
- R
- e
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
53. Q: How do behavior preservation and encapsulation work together?
A: Show answer
Encapsulation changes where clients obtain data or behavior, but it should not silently change what the program does. A safe migration preserves public results while gradually moving access behind new methods and classes. Tests verify the behavior during each step, while the improved interface creates room for later changes. If the intended design also requires changing behavior, make that a separate, explicit change so failures can be diagnosed independently.
Study instruction:
- "Separate structural refactoring commits from feature or business-rule changes and run regression tests between them."
Key point:
- E

- Refactoring by Martin Fowler

Moving Features Across Software Boundaries
1. Q: Why is moving features between contexts an important part of refactoring?
A: Show answer
A program's best grouping of behavior and data becomes clearer as developers learn more about the domain and the system changes. A function, field, or statement may initially be placed in a reasonable context but later fit better elsewhere. Moving it can bring related behavior together, reduce dependencies on unrelated modules, and make future changes local. The goal is not movement for its own sake; it is to align program structure with the relationships and responsibilities that the code actually expresses.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a feature that frequently reaches into another module and explain which context should own it and why."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
2. Q: What is the central motivation for Move Function?
A: Show answer
Move Function places a function in the context where its data, collaborators, callers, or future use make it fit best. A function that uses another class's data more than its current class's data may be misplaced. It may also belong where its callers live or where a forthcoming capability needs it. The decision requires looking at both sides of the move: what the function uses, what calls it, and what other behavior depends on it.
Study instruction:
- "For a candidate method, list its callers, callees, field references, and likely future callers before selecting a destination."
Key point:
- M
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
3. Q: How should a developer decide whether a function belongs in another class or module?
A: Show answer
Compare the source and target contexts by examining which data the function reads or changes, which methods it calls, which methods call it, and which module should control the relevant rule. A function that depends heavily on another context may be more cohesive there. However, moving it can require parameters or references to source objects, so the destination should not create awkward dependencies. The best location is a design judgment that can be revisited as the code evolves.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a small dependency map for the method and compare the coupling created by keeping it versus moving it."
Key point:
- F
- u
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
4. Q: Why should a function's dependencies be reviewed before Move Function is applied?
A: Show answer
A function rarely stands alone. It may rely on fields, helper functions, constants, or other methods that should move with it. If a required dependency remains behind, the moved function may need extra parameters or a reference to the original object. Reviewing the dependency cluster first helps determine whether to move helpers together, pass simple values, or create a new abstraction. Moving dependent functions in the right order can reduce temporary coupling and avoid an unnecessarily complicated destination interface.
Study instruction:
- "List every symbol used by the method and mark each as move with it, pass as a parameter, or remain in the source context."
Key point:
- M
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
5. Q: What is a safe sequence for moving a function?
A: Show answer
First inspect the function's dependencies and determine whether related methods should move too. Check whether the method is polymorphic and account for superclass and subclass declarations. Copy the function into the target context and adapt its references and name. Run static analysis, then arrange for the source context to call the new function, often through a temporary delegating method. Test the behavior, update callers, and remove the delegation when it is no longer useful.
Study instruction:
- "Write a step-by-step plan for moving one method and identify a test checkpoint after each structural change."
Key point:
- C
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
6. Q: When should a helper function move together with the function that uses it?
A: Show answer
A helper should move with its caller when it is tightly coupled to that caller and has no meaningful independent role in the source context. Moving a dependent helper first can make the main function easier to move because the dependency is already available in the target. Helpers that are general-purpose or used widely may be better left in a shared module. The decision should follow actual cohesion and reuse rather than a rule that all callees must move together.
Study instruction:
- "Check whether the helper has other callers and whether its behavior is specific to the moving function or generally reusable."
Key point:
- M
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
7. Q: How should parameters and object references be chosen when moving a function?
A: Show answer
When a moved function still needs information from its former context, pass the smallest stable values it requires or pass a reference to the source object. Simple parameters often keep dependencies explicit and focused. Passing the whole object can be more appropriate when the function needs several related values or when those requirements are likely to evolve. However, passing a large object can create broad coupling and make the destination depend on more of the source interface than necessary.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the number and stability of required values before choosing between explicit parameters and a source-object reference."
Key point:
- P
- a
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
8. Q: Why must polymorphic methods be considered before moving a function?
A: Show answer
A polymorphic method may be declared or overridden across a superclass and several subclasses. Moving only one implementation can break dispatch, leave inconsistent method contracts, or change which behavior executes for particular instances. The developer must identify all declarations and overrides, understand the intended variation, and move or adapt the hierarchy consistently. Tests should cover each relevant subtype rather than only the base class.
Study instruction:
- "Search the inheritance hierarchy for declarations and overrides and build test cases for each concrete subtype before moving the method."
Key point:
- P
- o
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
9. Q: What role does a temporary delegating function play during Move Function?
A: Show answer
After the new method is established in the target context, the old method can temporarily delegate to it. This keeps existing callers working while allowing the new location to be tested. The delegation provides a controlled transition: clients can be migrated gradually without simultaneously changing every caller and the implementation. It can remain as a compatibility or façade method when useful, but should be removed if it adds no value and callers can reach the target directly.
Study instruction:
- "Identify existing callers and decide which can migrate immediately and which require a temporary compatibility method."
Key point:
- D
- e
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
10. Q: How can a nested function be moved to a top-level function safely?
A: Show answer
Copy the nested function to the top level under a temporary searchable name, then resolve every reference that depended on the nested scope. Values such as a points collection should normally become explicit parameters. Helpers such as distance and radians may move with it if they form a cohesive dependency cluster. Once static analysis and tests confirm that the copy works, change the original nested function to delegate to the new function, test again, and then remove the old function if it has no remaining purpose.
Study instruction:
- "Move one nested function in a small example and use linting to identify unresolved references from the former scope."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
11. Q: How should a developer decide whether to pass a value or move a helper dependency with a function?
A: Show answer
A simple value should usually be passed when it represents a small, stable input from the source context. A helper function may be moved with the main function when it is strongly related and does not need the old context. If the helper is broadly reused, it may belong in a common module instead. The objective is to avoid unnecessary dependencies while keeping the target function's interface understandable and its behavior cohesive.
Study instruction:
- "For each external dependency, compare passing an input, moving the dependency, and extracting a shared abstraction."
Key point:
- S
- m
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
12. Q: What design problem does Move Field address?
A: Show answer
Move Field relocates data to the object or record that better represents its meaning and controls the related behavior. A field may be misplaced when it is repeatedly passed alongside another object, changes whenever another object's data changes, or is maintained in multiple places. Moving it can make the underlying data model clearer and reduce synchronization work. Since a field's users may be numerous, encapsulated accessors make it possible to redirect those users without changing every caller at once.
Study instruction:
- "Find fields that travel together, change together, or are duplicated across structures and evaluate whether they share one natural owner."
Key point:
- D
- a
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
13. Q: Why should a field be encapsulated before it is moved?
A: Show answer
Encapsulation creates a controlled access boundary around the source field. Once reads and writes use accessors or methods, the storage can be relocated by changing those accessors while callers continue to use the same interface. Without encapsulation, direct references throughout the codebase make it harder to identify and update every dependency safely. In a bare record, accessor functions may need to be introduced first, or the record may be converted into a class.
Study instruction:
- "Search all reads and writes of a candidate field and route them through a controlled interface before changing its storage location."
Key point:
- E
- n
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
14. Q: What are the main steps in Move Field?
A: Show answer
Ensure the source field is encapsulated and test the existing behavior. Create the field and accessors in the target object, then establish a reference from the source to the target. Redirect the source accessors to use the target field and test again. If the target is shared, consider temporarily updating both locations and adding an assertion to detect inconsistent values. Once the new location is confirmed, remove the source field and run the tests again.
Study instruction:
- "Create a checklist covering source encapsulation, target field, object reference, accessor redirection, consistency checks, and source-field removal."
Key point:
- R
- e
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
15. Q: Why can object construction order matter during Move Field?
A: Show answer
The target object must exist before an accessor that writes the relocated field tries to use it. If a constructor initializes the field through a method before constructing the target object, the new accessor may dereference an undefined reference. The solution is to adjust statement order so the target is initialized first, test that change, and only then redirect the accessors. This is a sequencing dependency rather than a problem with the concept of field movement.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect constructor order and ensure target references are initialized before any relocated-field accessor is called."
Key point:
- I
- n
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
16. Q: When should an assertion be introduced while moving a field to a shared object?
A: Show answer
An assertion is useful when a field currently exists separately on many source objects but is intended to become a shared property of one target object. Before consolidating the data, verify that the separate values already agree with the shared value. An assertion or temporary diagnostic can detect records that violate the assumption. If values differ, moving the field would change observable behavior and is not a behavior-preserving refactoring until the data discrepancy is resolved or the domain rule is intentionally changed.
Study instruction:
- "Add a temporary consistency check between source and target values and review its failures before switching all reads to the shared field."
Key point:
- S
- h
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
17. Q: How can Move Field be applied when the source is a bare record?
A: Show answer
Bare records lack accessor methods, so all functions that read or write the field may need to be changed to use new accessor functions. The migration is more difficult because access is not naturally controlled by the record itself. If the field is immutable, the old and new locations can sometimes be populated together while reads are migrated gradually. For a mutable record, encapsulating the record in a class first is often a safer starting point.
Study instruction:
- "Identify every read and write in the bare-record code and consider converting the record to a class before moving data."
Key point:
- B
- a
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
18. Q: What risk arises when a field moves from individual objects to a shared type object?
A: Show answer
The move can change behavior if individual objects previously held different values. After the move, all objects sharing the target will observe the same field value. Before consolidating, verify that existing records satisfy the intended shared-value assumption, for example by checking a database or adding a temporary assertion. Any differences must be understood and resolved or the new shared design must be treated as a deliberate behavior change rather than a refactoring.
Study instruction:
- "Compare all source values that will share the target and identify outliers before changing the getter."
Key point:
- A
-
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
19. Q: When should a statement be moved into a function?
A: Show answer
Move Statements into Function is appropriate when the same statements are repeatedly executed around calls to a function and those statements are best understood as part of the function's behavior. Centralizing them removes duplication and gives future changes one place to occur. If the statements merely need to execute alongside the function but are not conceptually part of it, extracting a separate combined operation may be more appropriate. The function's name should accurately describe the expanded responsibility.
Study instruction:
- "Compare several call sites and decide whether the repeated statements belong to the called function's abstraction or to a separate workflow."
Key point:
- R
- e
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
20. Q: How can Move Statements into Function be performed when there are multiple callers?
A: Show answer
First make the repeated statements adjacent to the function call, using Slide Statements if needed. Extract one call site together with the repeated statements into a temporary function with an easy-to-search name. Convert each remaining caller to use the new function and test after each conversion. When all callers use the combined operation, inline the original function into it and rename the result to the original function name or a better name. This gradual process avoids a one-step change across all callers.
Study instruction:
- "Use a temporary grep-friendly name and track each caller as it migrates to the combined operation."
Key point:
- M
- i
Source links:
- Catalog of Refactorings
21. Q: When is direct cut-and-paste sufficient for Move Statements into Function?
A: Show answer
If the target function has only one caller, the repeated statements can often be moved directly into it, followed by a test. The more elaborate extraction and migration sequence is valuable when multiple callers must be updated because it gives a temporary combined function through which each caller can transition safely. The simpler procedure should be used only when the caller structure makes it clear that no other use needs to be migrated.
Study instruction:
- "Count the target function's callers and choose the simplest safe sequence supported by that call graph."

- T

- Catalog of Refactorings
22. Q: Why might statements be moved out of a function and into its callers?
A: Show answer
Move Statements to Callers is useful when part of a function's behavior needs to vary by caller. A shared function may still own common work, while a statement such as formatting, output, or caller-specific processing belongs outside it. Moving that behavior outward gives callers freedom to customize it without introducing conditionals or parameters into the shared function. If the function boundary is fundamentally wrong, it may be clearer to inline it and extract new functions around the correct responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a statement that is shared by most callers but requires different behavior in one caller, and determine whether it belongs in that caller."
Key point:
- C

- Catalog of Refactorings
23. Q: What is the safe migration process for Move Statements to Callers?
A: Show answer
For a complex multi-caller case, first extract the statements that should remain in the function into a temporary searchable helper. Then inline the original function at each call site so callers can take ownership of their varying statements. After all callers have been converted, remove the original function and rename the extracted helper to the original name or a better name. When methods are overridden, the extraction must be applied consistently across the relevant subclasses so the remaining methods have matching behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Create a migration plan for each caller and test after inlining the function at every call site."
Key point:
- P

- Catalog of Refactorings
24. Q: How should inherited methods be handled when statements move to callers?
A: Show answer
If the target method is overridden in subclasses, the extraction should be applied to all relevant implementations so that the remaining common method is identical in each class. After the varying statements have been moved to callers, the duplicate subclass methods may become unnecessary and can be removed. The developer must preserve the intended polymorphic behavior and test representative instances from every affected subclass.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect the inheritance tree and compare the base and overridden bodies before performing the extraction."

- Catalog of Refactorings
25. Q: When is Replace Inline Code with Function Call appropriate?
A: Show answer
Use an existing function when inline code performs the same operation and the function name makes sense as a description of that code. The replacement reduces duplication, centralizes behavior, and makes the caller express intent rather than mechanics. Similar-looking code should not be replaced automatically if its purpose is different or if future changes to the existing function should not affect it. The quality of the function name is a useful test of whether the abstraction really matches.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the meaning and expected future change behavior of an inline fragment with the candidate function before replacing it."
Key point:

- Catalog of Refactorings
26. Q: Why is a meaningful function name important when replacing inline code?
A: Show answer
A name allows a reader to understand the purpose of a call without reconstructing its implementation. If the name makes sense where the inline code appears, it is evidence that the existing function captures the same concept. If it does not, the function may need renaming or may represent a different responsibility. A poor name can make a correct reuse appear misleading, while an unrelated function should not be reused merely to eliminate duplicate-looking statements.
Study instruction:
- "Read the proposed function call in its surrounding sentence and decide whether its name accurately describes the work being performed."
