- Martin Fowler — Consolidate Conditional Expression
14. Q: How can Extract Function complement Consolidate Conditional Expression?
A: Show answer
After combining the component conditions, extract the complete predicate into a function named for the unified rule. This turns a low-level boolean expression into a meaningful domain decision and allows the caller to focus on what should happen when that rule is true. The function can also become a single location for testing, documenting, and later modifying the eligibility or validation rule. Choose a name whose positive or negative meaning is unambiguous, and ensure callers interpret its return value consistently.
Study instruction:
- Extract a combined predicate into a named function and write unit tests for representative true and false cases.
Key point:
- Consolidation reduces fragmented logic; extraction explains the resulting rule.
- Use names that make the truth meaning unmistakable.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Consolidate Conditional Expression
- Martin Fowler — Decompose Conditional
15. Q: What risks arise when several boolean operators are mixed in one consolidated condition?
A: Show answer
A condition that combines AND and OR can be difficult to interpret because operator precedence and grouping may not match the reader’s assumptions. A missing or misplaced set of parentheses can change the result and create subtle eligibility defects. Even when precedence is technically correct, a long expression may conceal separate concepts. Use parentheses to show intended grouping, extract meaningful subconditions into named functions or variables, and add tests for boundary and mixed-truth cases. Consolidation should reveal a coherent rule rather than compress several rules into an opaque line.
Study instruction:
- Write a truth table for a condition containing both AND and OR, then add parentheses or named predicates to show the intended groups.
Key point:
- Explicit grouping reduces ambiguity in compound boolean expressions.
- Tests should cover combinations that are easy to misread.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Consolidate Conditional Expression
16. Q: What is Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses?
A: Show answer
Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses converts checks for exceptional or non-core situations into early exits. Instead of nesting the main processing inside several else blocks, the function checks conditions that prevent normal processing and returns or throws when one is met. The remaining code then represents the main path directly. This changes the emphasis of the function: exceptional cases are handled first and the core behavior is no longer buried inside nested branches. The refactoring is appropriate when one branch is an early termination rather than an equally important alternative to normal processing.
Study instruction:
- Identify a function with nested pre-checks and rewrite it so that each non-core case exits before the main calculation.
Key point:
- Guard clauses make exceptional conditions explicit at the beginning of a function.
- The main path becomes flatter and easier to follow.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
17. Q: How do guard clauses communicate the difference between exceptional cases and normal behavior?
A: Show answer
An if-else structure gives both branches visual prominence and can imply that each is a regular alternative. A guard clause communicates that a condition is outside the function’s main purpose: handle that condition and leave immediately. The remaining statements then show the normal work without additional indentation. This is especially useful for preconditions such as an employee no longer being eligible for a calculation or an instrument having an invalid parameter. The distinction is semantic, not just stylistic; if both outcomes are equally central, a conventional if-else may communicate the logic better.
Study instruction:
- For each branch in a nested conditional, classify it as normal behavior or an early-termination case and justify the classification.
Key point:
- Guard clauses emphasize that an exceptional case is handled and the function exits.
- Use if-else when both alternatives are normal parts of the function’s behavior.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
18. Q: What sequence should be followed when converting nested conditionals into guard clauses?
A: Show answer
Select the outermost condition that represents an early exit and replace that branch with a return or appropriate exception. Run tests, then move to the next nested condition and repeat. When the nesting has been removed, reassess temporary variables and control structures that may no longer be needed. If several guard clauses return the same result, consider Consolidate Conditional Expression. Incremental conversion is important because it makes the effect of each change clear and exposes mistakes in branch order or return values quickly.
Study instruction:
- Refactor a nested function one condition at a time and record the test result after each guard clause is introduced.
Key point:
- Replace the outermost eligible check first, then test and repeat.
- Reassess variables and repeated outcomes after nesting is removed.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
19. Q: How should a nested condition be reversed when turning it into a guard clause?
A: Show answer
A guard clause must exit when the original condition for continuing normal processing is false. Therefore, the predicate may need to be negated. For example, a nested condition requiring positive capital, interest, and duration can be expressed as early returns when any required value is non-positive. De Morgan’s laws help translate a negated conjunction into a disjunction of negated terms. Make the inversion in small steps, retain the original boundary operators, and test every boundary case because a single incorrect inequality can change the allowed range.
Study instruction:
- Take a nested condition with two or more required predicates, negate it using De Morgan’s laws, and test each boundary value.
Key point:
- A guard clause exits when the normal-path condition is not satisfied.
- Negation must preserve exact boundaries and boolean meaning.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
- Martin Fowler — Consolidate Conditional Expression
20. Q: What should be done when multiple guard clauses return the same value?
A: Show answer
Once the early exits are visible, inspect whether several conditions represent separate reasons for the same result. If they are logically one exclusion rule, combine them into a single predicate using OR and consider extracting that predicate into a named function. This can reduce visual repetition and explain the shared policy. Do not combine checks that require different logging, exception details, or other effects. The return value may be the same while the handling obligation remains distinct, so preserve distinctions that matter to the program or its operators.
Study instruction:
- Review the guard clauses in a function, group only those with the same semantic outcome, and test the combined rule.
Key point:
- Repeated identical early exits may be consolidated when they form one rule.
- Keep distinct handling visible when it carries different meaning or effects.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
- Martin Fowler — Consolidate Conditional Expression
21. Q: Must a function have only one exit point after guard clauses are introduced?
A: Show answer
No. A single exit point is not a universal requirement for clear or correct functions. Modern languages already provide one entry point to a function, but multiple explicit returns can improve readability when they represent clear early exits. The correct choice depends on which structure makes the function’s purpose easiest to understand and maintain. Multiple exits should still be deliberate: ensure each return produces the correct result, releases required resources, and does not bypass essential cleanup or transaction handling. Language constructs such as finally blocks or structured resource management can help preserve cleanup guarantees.
Study instruction:
- Compare a single-return version and a guard-clause version of the same function; evaluate readability, cleanup behavior, and test coverage.
Key point:
- Clarity is more important than a blanket one-return rule.
- Early exits must still preserve cleanup and resource-management obligations.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
22. Q: How can guard clauses eliminate unnecessary mutable result variables?
A: Show answer
Nested code often assigns different outcomes to a result variable and returns it after all branches finish. When exceptional cases become early returns, the main calculation can often be returned directly, removing the need for a variable that merely stores one final value. This reduces mutable state and makes the result of each path explicit. Before deleting the variable, verify that it is not used later, that no required side effect depends on its assignment, and that each guard returns the correct structure or type. A variable that accumulates several steps may still be necessary.
Study instruction:
- Convert a nested assignment-based function to guard clauses and identify whether its final result variable can be removed safely.
Key point:
- Early returns can make temporary mutable result variables unnecessary.
- Remove only variables whose state no longer carries useful information.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
23. Q: How should guard clauses be ordered when a function has several preconditions?
A: Show answer
Order guard clauses to make the function’s logic understandable and to preserve the original semantics. A check that prevents all meaningful processing may be placed first, while checks that depend on values established earlier must remain after those values are available. Consider whether an early check is inexpensive, whether it can safely run before another validation, and whether the order communicates priority. Do not reorder checks solely for speed without confirming that their evaluation is side-effect-free and that the change cannot alter which error or outcome is observed.
Study instruction:
- Map the dependencies and observable effects of each precondition, then justify the order of the guard clauses.
Key point:
- Preserve dependencies and observable behavior when ordering early exits.
- A readable order must also be semantically safe.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses
24. Q: What is Replace Conditional with Polymorphism?
A: Show answer
Replace Conditional with Polymorphism moves type-dependent behavior from conditional statements into methods implemented by different object types. A switch that selects an algorithm according to a type code can become a call to a common operation such as calculateSpeed or describePlumage. Each concrete type supplies its own behavior, so the caller no longer needs to know every type-specific rule. This can clarify systems where several operations repeatedly switch on the same type or where a base case has meaningful variants. It is a structural refactoring and usually requires a suitable object model and a reliable way to create the correct type.
Study instruction:
- Find multiple operations that switch on the same type code and identify the operations that could become polymorphic methods.
Key point:
- Move type-specific behavior behind a common interface.
- The caller should depend on the operation, not on every concrete type.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
25. Q: What signs indicate that a repeated type switch may be a candidate for polymorphism?
A: Show answer
A strong sign is that several functions contain similar switch or if-else logic on the same type field, with each case implementing a different version of an operation. Adding a new type then requires editing multiple functions, creating a risk that one switch is missed or treated inconsistently. Another sign is a base calculation with recurring variant-specific adjustments that make the common case difficult to understand. Before refactoring, confirm that the cases represent stable domain variations and that a common interface can express the behavior cleanly.
Study instruction:
- Search a codebase for switches on the same discriminator and create a matrix of types against the operations that contain type-specific branches.
Key point:
- Repeated type-based branching across operations signals duplicated variation logic.
- Confirm that the variations form a coherent domain model before introducing classes.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
26. Q: How does a factory function support replacing a type switch with polymorphism?
A: Show answer
A factory function centralizes the mapping from incoming type information to the appropriate object. Callers request the object through the factory instead of constructing concrete classes or branching on the type themselves. Once the object is created, the caller invokes a shared method and polymorphic dispatch selects the appropriate implementation. The factory should define what happens for unknown or unsupported type codes, and its mapping should be covered by tests. Centralizing creation prevents type-selection logic from spreading through the application.
Study instruction:
- Implement a factory for three concrete variants and test each known type plus an unsupported type.
Key point:
- The factory centralizes type selection; the object handles its own behavior.
- Define an explicit policy for unrecognized type values.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
27. Q: How can polymorphism represent a base case and its special variations?
A: Show answer
A superclass can implement the normal or common behavior, while subclasses override only the parts that differ for particular variants. This is useful when most objects follow the same rules but a well-defined category requires different calculations or decisions. The base implementation should remain understandable without variant-specific clutter, and each subclass should express its behavior in terms of its differences from that base. A method may call a smaller overridable method for a variation point, but that method should have a precise name rather than combining unrelated concerns.
Study instruction:
- Separate a shared calculation from one variant-specific adjustment and identify which part belongs in the base class and which part belongs in a subclass.
Key point:
- Put stable common behavior in the base and genuine differences in variants.
- Keep variation points narrowly defined and clearly named.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
28. Q: What are the principal mechanics for introducing polymorphism into conditional logic?
A: Show answer
First create the classes needed for the variants and a factory that selects the correct instance. Change callers to use the factory. Move the conditional operation to a common class or interface, then replace one branch at a time with an overriding method in the appropriate subclass. Test after each branch is moved and retain a deliberate default behavior in the base class, or make the operation abstract and fail explicitly when a subclass must implement it. Repeat for each type-specific operation only after the earlier conversion is stable.
Study instruction:
- Plan a staged refactoring for one switch statement, showing class creation, factory introduction, one override, tests, and the final default behavior.
Key point:
- Introduce the object structure before moving each branch incrementally.
- Preserve a safe and explicit default or abstract contract.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
29. Q: Does Replace Conditional with Polymorphism always require a superclass hierarchy?
A: Show answer
No. In a language with structural typing or dynamic object behavior, different objects can provide methods with the same name without sharing a formal superclass. A factory can return objects that conform to the required interface, and callers can dispatch through those methods. A superclass may still be useful to express a domain relationship, share implementation, or define a default behavior. Choose the least complicated structure that communicates the domain and enforces the needed contract; do not add inheritance solely to satisfy a pattern diagram.
Study instruction:
- Compare a subclass-based implementation with a set of interface-compatible objects and identify which structure better expresses the actual domain relationship.
Key point:
- Polymorphism is about substitutable behavior, not mandatory inheritance.
- Use a hierarchy when it adds useful structure or shared behavior.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 10
30. Q: Why is a deliberate default case important when moving conditional behavior into a class hierarchy?
A: Show answer
The default case defines what happens when the system encounters a type that is not represented by a specialized variant. It may return a legitimate general value, preserve an unknown state, or throw an explicit error when an unsupported type indicates a programming defect. Silently returning an arbitrary value can hide incomplete factory mappings or newly introduced types. The design should distinguish expected unknown data from impossible internal states, and tests should verify both the default and all specialized cases.
Study instruction:
- Define expected behavior for an unknown discriminator and test that the factory and base class apply that policy consistently.
Key point:
- Unknown types need explicit, intentional handling.
- A default must not conceal a missing variant implementation.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
31. Q: How should tests support a migration from type switches to polymorphic methods?
A: Show answer
Tests should characterize the existing behavior for every known type, important boundary condition, and default case before the switch is changed. After creating the factory and moving a branch, rerun those tests to verify that dispatch selects the same calculation and returns the same result. Add tests for subclass overrides and factory selection, and consider tests for combinations of variants with other input conditions. This approach detects a wrong discriminator, omitted branch, accidental change to a shared method, or incorrect default behavior before the old switch is removed completely.
Study instruction:
- Build a behavior matrix for each type and operation, then use it as a regression suite during the polymorphism refactoring.
Key point:
- Characterization tests protect the behavior of every existing branch.
- Factory selection and variant-specific calculations both require coverage.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 10
32. Q: Why should polymorphism not replace every if-else or switch statement?
A: Show answer
Simple conditionals are often the clearest way to express straightforward decisions. Introducing classes and factories has a cost: more objects, more navigation, a larger conceptual model, and possible indirection in behavior that was previously visible. Polymorphism is most valuable when type-specific logic is complex, repeated, or expected to vary independently. It is less attractive for a small one-off choice or where the cases do not represent stable types. The decision should balance the cost of repeated branching against the complexity introduced by the new abstraction.
Study instruction:
- Compare a simple two-way conditional with a polymorphic design and explain why the simpler form may be preferable.
Key point:
- Conditional statements remain appropriate for many ordinary decisions.
- Introduce polymorphism when it clarifies meaningful and recurring variation.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
33. Q: How can an awkward subclass override method indicate that polymorphic variation needs further decomposition?
A: Show answer
A method introduced only to create a subclass variation point can become a vague container for several unrelated adjustments. A name that joins multiple concepts with “and” is a warning that the method may represent more than one responsibility. Extract the separate factors into focused methods in both the base and specialized classes, then move or rename the calculations so each override expresses one meaningful variation. The goal is not simply to relocate a conditional but to expose the independent dimensions of the domain rule.
Study instruction:
- Review an override whose name combines multiple concepts and split its logic into independently named factors with separate tests.
Key point:
- A variation point should represent one coherent dimension of behavior.
- Further extraction can make base and subclass differences easier to reason about.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Conditional with Polymorphism
- Martin Fowler — Decompose Conditional
34. Q: What is Introduce Special Case, and why is it broader than the Null Object pattern?
A: Show answer
Introduce Special Case creates a dedicated value, object, or record to represent a recurring exceptional value and centralize the behavior associated with it. Many clients may encounter a missing, unknown, or otherwise special value and independently substitute the same defaults. A special-case object provides those common responses through the same interface as an ordinary value, reducing repeated comparisons. Null Object is a particular application for null or absent values; the broader technique applies to any special category that needs consistent behavior.
Study instruction:
- Identify a special value that many callers test and document the common response that could be represented by a dedicated special-case object.
Key point:
- Centralize repeated special-case behavior in one value or object.
- Null Object is one form of the broader Special Case pattern.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
35. Q: What conditions suggest that a repeated special-case check should become a Special Case object?
A: Show answer
The strongest signal is that many clients compare a value with the same special marker and then perform substantially the same substitute behavior. For example, when a customer record is unknown, several callers may use a default name, default plan, and zero delinquency value. Centralizing those responses prevents each caller from reimplementing the policy. The approach is less useful when only one caller needs special handling or when different clients legitimately require different outcomes. A special-case abstraction should capture stable common behavior, not hide important differences among consumers.
Study instruction:
- Search for repeated comparisons to the same marker and group the default values or actions used by each caller.
Key point:
- Repeated comparisons plus a common response indicate a useful abstraction opportunity.
- Do not centralize behavior that is not actually common.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
36. Q: How does a Special Case object simplify client code?
A: Show answer
The special-case object implements the common properties and operations that callers need, so clients can interact with it through the ordinary interface. Instead of asking whether a customer is unknown before reading the name, a client can request the name directly and receive the defined default. A missing payment history can similarly be represented by an object that returns a zero delinquency count. This reduces conditional branching at call sites and moves the policy for exceptional values to the object that represents that state. Clients that truly need different behavior can retain an explicit special-case check.
Study instruction:
- Replace a caller’s conditional default expression with a property or method on a special-case object and verify the result for both ordinary and exceptional values.
Key point:
- Clients use a common interface instead of repeatedly testing the special marker.
- Retain explicit checks where a caller has a genuinely different requirement.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
37. Q: How can a large codebase adopt a Special Case object without changing every client at once?
A: Show answer
Begin by adding an explicit predicate or query function for the existing special marker, then change callers one at a time to use that function and test each change. The predicate can temporarily recognize both the old marker and the new special-case representation. After all relevant clients use the abstraction, change the container or factory to return the special-case object instead of the marker. A validation trap can detect unexpected values during migration. This staged approach reduces the risk of an incomplete bulk change and provides a controlled point at which the old representation can be removed.
Study instruction:
- Create a migration plan with a compatibility predicate, incremental caller updates, a representation switch, and a final removal of old comparisons.
Key point:
- Introduce a common predicate before changing the underlying representation.
- Migrate callers incrementally and remove compatibility code only after verification.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
38. Q: When should a Special Case be implemented as a class rather than an object literal?
A: Show answer
Use a class when the special case needs behavior, state management, setters, or a consistent implementation of several related methods. A class is particularly useful when the replacement object must safely participate in operations that ordinary objects support. A simple object literal may be sufficient when clients only read a small number of fixed values and no behavior or mutation is required. The choice should follow the complexity of the contract, not a preference for classes as a general rule. Both forms should expose the properties expected by clients and define a consistent special-case indicator if one is needed.
Study instruction:
- Design both a class-based and literal representation for an unknown record, then compare the required methods, state, and client operations.
Key point:
- Use a class for richer behavior or state; use a literal for simple read-only values.
- The chosen representation must satisfy the client-facing contract.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
39. Q: Why should a Special Case object generally be immutable?
A: Show answer
A special-case object usually represents a stable substitute value rather than an independent entity whose state should evolve. If callers mutate its default name, plan, or nested values, one client may affect what another client observes and undermine the consistency of the special-case policy. Immutability ensures that the special-case representation continues to provide predictable responses. If an ordinary object is mutable but the special case intentionally ignores updates, the behavior should be explicit and documented; alternatively, expose an immutable interface and prevent callers from treating the substitute as an ordinary mutable entity.
Study instruction:
- Identify which properties of a special-case object are fixed and choose a language-appropriate way to prevent unintended mutation.
Key point:
- Stable substitute values should not be changed by unrelated clients.
- Make ignored updates or immutable behavior explicit in the contract.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
40. Q: How should a Special Case object handle properties that return other objects?
A: Show answer
If a special-case customer exposes a payment-history object, returning null for that nested property simply moves the same problem one level deeper. The client would still need to test the payment history before reading its delinquency value. A better design often supplies a nested special-case object that implements the expected payment-history interface and returns the appropriate default. The principle is recursive: when a special case must provide related objects, those objects may also need special-case representations so that callers can use the ordinary navigation path safely.
Study instruction:
- Trace every object-valued property of a proposed special case and identify where a nested null value would force another caller-side conditional.
Key point:
- Special-case object graphs should provide consistent substitutes at the levels clients traverse.
- Avoid pushing null checks into nested consumers.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
41. Q: How can a data transformation step introduce a Special Case into record-oriented data?
A: Show answer
When the application works with plain records rather than domain objects, an enrichment or transformation function can normalize incoming data before clients use it. The transformation can copy the raw record, detect the special marker, and replace it with a record containing the default properties. Ordinary records can also be given a consistent special-case indicator. Clients then read the normalized structure without repeating marker checks. The transformation should preserve the original source data when necessary, avoid mutating shared input unexpectedly, and make its normalization contract clear to every consumer.
Study instruction:
- Build a pure transformation that converts an unknown customer marker into a normalized record and test that it does not mutate the original input.
Key point:
- Normalize special values at a data boundary rather than throughout the client code.
- Make copying, immutability, and transformation guarantees explicit.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
42. Q: When should a client retain an explicit test for a Special Case object?
A: Show answer
Most clients should use the common properties and methods supplied by the special case, but some may need genuinely different treatment. For example, most clients may display a default occupant name, while one reporting function must display a distinct phrase for unknown customers. That client should retain an explicit query such as isUnknown rather than forcing the special-case object to provide an inappropriate default for everyone. The purpose is to eliminate duplicated common behavior, not to prohibit all conditional logic involving the special case.
Study instruction:
- Review each caller after introducing a special case and classify it as common behavior or a legitimate exception to the shared policy.
Key point:
- Remove checks where the object can provide the common response.
- Keep explicit checks for valid client-specific differences.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Special Case
43. Q: What is Introduce Assertion, and what role does it play in simplifying conditional logic?
A: Show answer
Introduce Assertion makes an assumption about program state explicit by adding a condition that must always be true at a particular point. An assertion documents an invariant and can expose programming errors when the invariant is violated. For example, a discount calculation may assume that a discount rate is non-negative. An assertion communicates that assumption to readers and helps detect a defect near the point where the invalid state is encountered. It does not replace ordinary control flow or business validation; its purpose is to state a condition that correct program execution is expected to satisfy.
Study instruction:
- Identify an implicit invariant in a calculation and write an assertion that expresses it precisely.
Key point:
- Assertions make important assumptions about valid program state explicit.
- Assertion failure normally signals a defect in the program or its internal assumptions.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Assertion
44. Q: How are assertions different from validation of external input?
A: Show answer
Validation checks data that may legitimately be missing, malformed, malicious, or outside an allowed range, and it must produce an appropriate user-facing or system-level response. An assertion expresses an internal condition that the program assumes should already be true; its failure points to a programming error or broken invariant. External data should not be trusted merely because an assertion is present, since assertions may be disabled or treated differently by the runtime. Validate external input explicitly at the system boundary, then use assertions to document and verify invariants inside the trusted logic where appropriate.
Study instruction:
- Classify several checks as input validation or internal assertions and explain who controls the input and what response is required.
Key point:
- Validate untrusted external data through normal application logic.
- Use assertions for invariants that should hold during correct execution.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Assertion
45. Q: Where should an assertion be placed when an invalid value originates from a state update?
A: Show answer
Place the assertion as close as practical to the boundary where the invariant can be violated, such as a setter or controlled state-update method. If a negative discount rate enters through a setter, checking it there helps identify the source of the bad state instead of discovering the problem much later during a calculation. The assertion should describe the full valid domain, including any legitimate null or absent value. Centralizing the invariant reduces duplication, but it must not be used as a substitute for validating untrusted inputs before they enter the trusted object model.
Study instruction:
- Trace an invariant violation back to the state-changing operation and determine whether the assertion belongs in a setter, constructor, or internal method.
Key point:
- Check invariants near the operation that establishes or changes the state.
- Express permitted exceptional values explicitly in the assertion condition.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Assertion
46. Q: What are the risks of overusing assertions?
A: Show answer
Assertions can become noisy or misleading when they repeat checks already enforced elsewhere, duplicate logic that changes frequently, or test conditions that are not true invariants. Excessive assertions may also create a false sense of safety if developers assume they validate external input or will always run in production. Use them for conditions that genuinely must hold, and extract shared predicates when the same invariant is checked in multiple places. Keep the assertion side-effect-free and ensure that normal program behavior does not depend on whether assertions are enabled.
Study instruction:
- Audit assertions in a module and remove duplicate or non-invariant checks while retaining those that document essential assumptions.
Key point:
- Assert important invariants, not every condition that appears useful to check.
- Program correctness must not depend on assertion execution.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Assertion
47. Q: How should assertion failures be handled in relation to normal application errors?
A: Show answer
An assertion failure should be treated as evidence that an internal assumption has been violated, not as an ordinary alternative result for callers to interpret. The program should not rely on downstream components to catch an assertion and continue as though the input were valid. Normal application errors, such as rejected requests or unsupported user choices, require explicit validation and error handling. Separating these categories makes defect diagnosis clearer and prevents assertions from becoming hidden control-flow branches.
Study instruction:
- Review exception handling around assertions and confirm that ordinary business errors are validated and reported independently.
Key point:
- Assertions diagnose broken internal assumptions rather than expected business exceptions.
- Keep normal error handling explicit and independent of assertions.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Introduce Assertion
48. Q: How should a team choose among decomposition, consolidation, guard clauses, polymorphism, Special Case, and assertions?
A: Show answer
Choose the refactoring according to the source of the complexity. Use Decompose Conditional when the condition or branch hides intention; use Consolidate Conditional Expression when several side-effect-free tests form one decision with a common result; use Guard Clauses when exceptional preconditions obscure the main path; use polymorphism when multiple operations repeatedly vary by type or stable variant; use Special Case when many clients repeat the same response to a special value; and use assertions to state internal invariants. These techniques solve different problems and may be combined, but each change should have a clear reason and behavior-preserving tests.
Study instruction:
- Take five conditional code examples and select the most appropriate technique for each, explaining why the alternatives are less suitable.
Key point:
- Diagnose the kind of complexity before choosing a refactoring.
- Techniques can complement one another but should not be applied mechanically.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 10
- Martin Fowler — Catalog of Refactorings
49. Q: How can a refactoring team demonstrate that conditional simplification has preserved behavior?
A: Show answer
Before changing the code, identify the observable outcomes for each meaningful branch, including boundary values, unknown types, missing records, and exceptional states. Capture those outcomes in automated tests or characterization tests. Apply one small refactoring at a time and run focused tests after each step, followed by the broader regression suite. For structural changes, verify not only output values but also relevant side effects, ordering, exceptions, and interactions with callers. Code review should assess whether the new structure communicates the same policy more clearly without silently altering it.
Study instruction:
- Build a decision table for one conditional and use it to create regression tests before and after a refactoring.
Key point:
- Behavior preservation includes outputs, side effects, exceptions, and ordering.
- Small changes plus targeted tests reduce the risk of regressions.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 10
50. Q: What practical warning signs suggest that conditional logic should be reviewed during code maintenance?
A: Show answer
Review conditionals when a new requirement forces edits in several switches on the same type, when nested branches are difficult to explain, when identical default expressions appear across many callers, or when developers cannot confidently describe boundary behavior. Other warning signs include predicates with hidden side effects, repeated conditions with different outcomes, assertions being used to handle user input, and fallback branches that silently mask unknown cases. These symptoms point to different remedies, so first identify whether the underlying issue is unclear intent, duplicate policy, exceptional flow, type variation, special values, or an unstated invariant.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a focused code review using these warning signs and record the problem category, proposed refactoring, and tests required for each finding.
Key point:
- Conditional complexity has recognizable symptoms that can guide review.
- Match each symptom to the technique that addresses its underlying cause.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 10
- Martin Fowler — Catalog of Refactorings
Rows: 50
Table 11
Designing Clearer and More Adaptable APIs
1. Q: What is the central purpose of refactoring APIs?
A: Show answer
An API is the interface through which one part of a program uses another. Its design determines how clearly callers express intent, which side owns each responsibility, and how much code must change when the implementation evolves. Refactoring improves these interaction points without changing required behavior. The chapter addresses read-versus-write separation, repeated algorithms, flag parameters, data passed between modules, dependency direction, object mutability, construction flexibility, and the choice between a function and a command object. A strong API makes correct use straightforward and exposes the essential contract without requiring clients to inspect implementation details.
Study instruction:
- "Review a module’s public operations and identify unclear signatures, hidden side effects, repeated data extraction, and responsibilities assigned to the wrong side of a call."
Key point:
- API quality depends on clarity and safe use by callers.
- Refactoring should improve the contract while preserving behavior.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
2. Q: What is Separate Query from Modifier?
A: Show answer
Separate Query from Modifier divides a function that both returns information and changes observable state into two operations. The query returns a value without an externally visible action; the modifier performs the state change. A caller that needs information can call the query without unexpectedly sending a message, updating a record, or activating an alarm. A caller that also needs the action can invoke both operations explicitly. The split makes the API predictable and helps callers decide when side effects should occur.
Study instruction:
- "Find a function that returns a value and changes state, then sketch a read-only query and a separately named modifier."
Key point:
- Queries report information; modifiers change observable state.
- An API should not hide an action inside an operation presented as a query.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Separate Query from Modifier
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
3. Q: What counts as an observable side effect in a query?
A: Show answer
Observable effects include changing persistent data, sending an email, triggering an alarm, writing to an external service, or mutating shared state that changes later externally visible results. Internal caching may update an implementation field without violating query behavior if callers cannot observe the cache and repeated queries still return the same logical result. The key distinction is the effect on the program’s contract, not whether memory changes internally. Hidden external actions make a query unsafe to repeat, move, or use in a conditional expression.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each operation in a candidate query as externally observable or an internal implementation detail; state what another component could detect."
Key point:
- Side-effect freedom concerns observable behavior, not every internal write.
- A query should be safe to call repeatedly without triggering an unexpected action.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Separate Query from Modifier
4. Q: How should a search function that also raises an alarm be separated?
A: Show answer
Create a query that searches the collection and returns the first matching item or a clearly defined not-found value without activating the alarm. Keep a modifier that performs the alarm action. A caller that only needs the match invokes the query; a caller that needs both obtains the result and then invokes the modifier. The modifier can often reuse the query rather than duplicate its search loop. This arrangement separates discovering a condition from responding to it and makes the operation’s effect apparent at the call site.
Study instruction:
- "Refactor a search-and-alert routine into find and alert operations; test both the no-match case and multiple matches when only the first should trigger action."
Key point:
- Discover the condition separately from acting on it.
- Share internal search logic without recombining the public responsibilities.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Separate Query from Modifier
5. Q: What is the safe sequence for applying Separate Query from Modifier?
A: Show answer
Copy the original function and name the copy for its query purpose. Remove observable effects from the new query and run static checks. Find each use of the original method; where its return value is needed, replace the call with the query followed by the modifier, testing each caller. After no caller depends on the modifier’s return value, remove that return value and test again. Finally, remove duplicated logic, commonly by having the modifier use the query. The old mixed contract should not be deleted until all dependent callers have migrated.
Study instruction:
- "Create a migration checklist covering query extraction, side-effect removal, caller changes, modifier return removal, duplication cleanup, and regression tests."
Key point:
- Migrate callers incrementally and test every intermediate interface.
- Remove the mixed return-and-modify contract only after its dependencies are gone.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Separate Query from Modifier
6. Q: How can splitting a query from a modifier create duplicated work?
A: Show answer
During migration a caller may query for a result and then call the modifier, which repeats the same search. This can be a safe temporary state but should be reviewed when migration is complete. The modifier may call the query to determine whether the action is needed, or both public operations may use a shared internal calculation. Preserve the clean public distinction while avoiding unnecessary duplicated algorithms. If the query is expensive, measure the cost and share computation deliberately rather than restoring a hidden side effect.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect the finished query and modifier for repeated loops or predicates; extract a common internal operation when that improves clarity and performance."
Key point:
- Temporary duplication can support a safe migration.
- Share implementation without merging the query and modifier contracts.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Separate Query from Modifier
7. Q: What is Parameterize Function?
A: Show answer
Parameterize Function replaces similar functions that differ only in literal values with one function that accepts those values as arguments. Two salary-raise functions that apply different percentages, for example, can become one raise function with a factor parameter. The common algorithm is maintained once, while callers choose the required variation. This is appropriate when the functions are genuinely one operation with data differences. It is not a reason to merge independent workflows or to hide distinct behavior behind a growing collection of mode parameters.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two similar functions and identify which literal values vary while the algorithm and responsibility remain the same."
Key point:
- Parameterization consolidates duplicated algorithms that differ by values.
- A parameter should express a real dimension of one operation.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Parameterize Function
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
8. Q: When should similar functions remain separate rather than be parameterized?
A: Show answer
Keep functions separate when their responsibilities differ, their algorithms are diverging, or the proposed common function would need several flags and tangled branches. The fact that two functions share a few lines does not prove they represent one concept. Separate names can be clearer when callers perform distinct domain actions. Parameterize only when a caller is selecting values for the same underlying operation and the common function remains cohesive and understandable.
Study instruction:
- "Document the shared algorithm and all behavioral differences in two candidate functions; decide whether the differences are data or distinct responsibilities."
Key point:
- Consolidate genuine duplication, not merely textual similarity.
- An over-generalized function can be harder to understand than two focused functions.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Parameterize Function
9. Q: What mechanics make Parameterize Function safe?
A: Show answer
Choose one of the similar functions and add parameters for the values that vary. Update its callers to supply their original literal values and test. Change the implementation to use the new parameters, testing after each substitution. Then migrate callers of each similar function to the parameterized version one at a time. If a variant requires an adjustment to the common function, make and test that adjustment before migrating the next family of callers. Delete the old functions only when all call sites have moved and behavior remains equivalent.
Study instruction:
- "Perform parameterization in stages and keep regression tests for each original function’s result until its callers are migrated."
Key point:
- Add the parameter and update callers before consolidating implementations.
- Test each migrated call site and each original variation.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Parameterize Function
10. Q: What risks arise from over-parameterizing a function?
A: Show answer
A routine with too many parameters may become a general-purpose decision engine with multiple modes and a large number of combinations. Its signature becomes harder to understand, the caller must know implementation details, and tests must cover a growing state space. Flags may be concealing several operations that should have explicit names or separate objects. Review whether each parameter is a true data input or a selector for a different workflow. Split responsibilities when the common abstraction no longer communicates one coherent operation.
Study instruction:
- "Review a function with several mode parameters and map the behaviors they select; identify cohesive operations that should become explicit functions."
Key point:
- Parameterization should reduce duplication without making the API opaque.
- A large set of mode parameters can signal that a function does too much.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Parameterize Function
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
11. Q: What is a flag argument?
A: Show answer
A flag argument is a parameter that the caller uses as a literal value to select which logic the function executes. It may be a boolean, enum, string, or symbol. A call such as deliveryDate(order, true) does not reveal the meaning of true unless the reader consults the function body. Flag arguments therefore obscure the caller’s intent and make the available operations less discoverable. The problem is not the parameter’s data type alone; it is its role as a literal behavior selector in the call and control flow.
Study instruction:
- "Locate calls that pass literal booleans, enum values, or mode strings and explain each call’s intended behavior in ordinary language."
Key point:
- Literal flags hide intent at the call site.
- Explicit operation names make available behaviors easier to discover.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
12. Q: Does every boolean parameter qualify as a flag argument?
A: Show answer
No. A boolean parameter may represent real data calculated elsewhere, rather than a caller selecting a mode with a literal. A function receiving isRush from a scheduling calculation is different from callers passing true or false directly to request two distinct operations. The function may still benefit from decomposing its internal condition, but Remove Flag Argument is not automatically appropriate. Inspect actual call sites and how the argument affects control flow before changing the API.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect all callers of a boolean-parameter function and classify arguments as literal selectors or values derived from runtime data."
Key point:
- The boolean type alone does not make a parameter a flag argument.
- Caller intent and control-flow use determine whether removal is appropriate.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
13. Q: How does Remove Flag Argument make callers clearer?
A: Show answer
Replace the flag-driven operation with explicit functions whose names state the desired behavior. A call such as deliveryDate(order, true) can become rushDeliveryDate(order), while the other case becomes regularDeliveryDate(order). The name carries the mode and avoids requiring callers to memorize the meaning of a boolean or code. Explicit functions also improve code completion and static analysis. A common helper may remain internal while data-driven clients still need it, but callers using literal flags should move to the intention-revealing API.
Study instruction:
- "Replace literal-flag calls with named functions and confirm that every name describes the exact behavior formerly selected by the flag."
Key point:
- Put the caller’s intent in the operation name.
- Retain a shared helper only where it still serves data-driven callers.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
14. Q: How can a clear dispatch conditional be used to remove a flag?
A: Show answer
When the function’s top-level conditional dispatches directly on the flag, use Decompose Conditional to extract a function for each behavior. Move literal-flag callers to the corresponding explicit function one by one and test after each migration. Once all such callers have moved, remove the original dispatch function if it is no longer needed. If some callers provide a dynamically calculated flag, keep a data-driven path or helper for them rather than forcing an inappropriate explicit call.
Study instruction:
- "Extract both branches from a top-level flag switch and migrate literal callers independently of dynamic-data callers."
Key point:
- A clear top-level dispatch makes explicit-function extraction straightforward.
- Preserve legitimate dynamic uses rather than changing their semantics.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
15. Q: How can wrapper functions remove a flag when its logic is tangled?
A: Show answer
When the flag influences several parts of the function and cannot easily be isolated into a top-level dispatch, introduce wrappers that call the original helper with fixed values. For example, rushDeliveryDate(order) can delegate to deliveryDate(order, true). Callers can migrate to the wrappers without immediately rewriting the algorithm. If dynamic-flag callers remain, retain the helper for them and make its limited role apparent through visibility or naming conventions. This separates the public intent first and allows internal decomposition later.
Study instruction:
- "Write wrappers for each literal flag mode and test them against the corresponding old calls before migrating clients."
Key point:
- Wrappers expose an explicit API before the implementation is fully decomposed.
- Keep the flag-based helper internal when a data-driven use still exists.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
16. Q: Why can multiple flag arguments indicate excessive function responsibility?
A: Show answer
With n independent boolean flags a routine can expose up to 2ⁿ combinations, many of which may not represent meaningful operations. Each new flag complicates the caller’s choices and increases testing combinations. The flags may be separating behaviors that should be distinct functions or composable steps. Rather than creating an explicit function for every theoretical combination, identify the cohesive responsibilities and build smaller operations that can be combined deliberately. A flag should remain when it is genuine data or a necessary part of the operation’s contract.
Study instruction:
- "Count the behavior combinations created by a function’s flags and group those combinations into coherent domain operations."
Key point:
- Many flags can create combinatorial complexity.
- Split responsibilities instead of generating an unmanageable menu of combinations.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Flag Argument
17. Q: What is Preserve Whole Object?
A: Show answer
Preserve Whole Object replaces several values extracted from one record with the record or object itself as a parameter. Instead of passing a temperature range’s lower and upper bounds separately, the caller passes the range and the callee reads the required properties. The signature becomes shorter and the relationship between the values remains visible. If the callee later needs another field from the same object, the caller need not change the parameter list. The trade-off is that the callee now depends on the whole object and should only receive it when that dependency is appropriate.
Study instruction:
- "Find a call that passes several properties from one record and redesign the callee to accept the original object."
Key point:
- Keep related values together when the object is the right abstraction.
- Whole-object parameters can reduce future signature changes.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Preserve Whole Object
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
18. Q: What are the benefits and trade-offs of Preserve Whole Object?
A: Show answer
The technique reduces long parameter lists, keeps related data together, and can eliminate repeated extraction logic in callers. It can also reveal that operations using several fields belong on the object itself. However, passing a large domain object gives the callee access to more fields and behavior than it may need. Across module boundaries that can create coupling and expose implementation details. When the whole object is not a suitable dependency, prefer a focused parameter object or explicit data-transfer structure that represents only the information the callee needs.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a whole-object parameter across a module boundary and list the data and behavior actually required by the receiving module."
Key point:
- Whole-object passing can improve cohesion and reduce parameter churn.
- Avoid exposing broad domain dependencies across inappropriate boundaries.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Preserve Whole Object
19. Q: How is Preserve Whole Object related to Feature Envy?
A: Show answer
If a function repeatedly extracts several values from one object and performs logic on them, the behavior may belong on that object rather than in the caller or a helper. This is a possible Feature Envy smell. Moving behavior can improve cohesion by putting data and operations together. If several operations repeatedly use the same subset of a large object, that subset may represent a separate domain concept and an opportunity for Extract Class. Preserve Whole Object can therefore reveal misplaced behavior or a missing abstraction, not just shorten a signature.
Study instruction:
- "Review code that unpacks the same object repeatedly and decide whether to move the logic or extract a focused class."
Key point:
- Repeatedly unpacking data may reveal misplaced behavior.
- Use cohesion and module boundaries to guide the next refactoring.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Preserve Whole Object
20. Q: What is the safe migration sequence for Preserve Whole Object?
A: Show answer
Create a new function with the desired object parameter and an easily searchable temporary name. Initially implement it as a wrapper that extracts the needed fields and delegates to the old function. Change each caller to use the new function, removing obsolete local extraction when possible, and test after every caller change. Once all callers have migrated, inline the old implementation into the new function and rename the new function to the original public name. The wrapper keeps a working interface throughout the change.
Study instruction:
- "Use a temporary wrapper, migrate each caller, run tests, inline the old function, and perform the final rename."
Key point:
- A compatibility wrapper enables incremental adoption.
- Inline only after the old calling pattern is no longer used.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Preserve Whole Object
21. Q: When should an object pass itself rather than several of its own fields?
A: Show answer
When a method passes multiple pieces of its own state to another operation, those values may be better represented by a self-reference such as this. The receiving function can then obtain the fields it legitimately needs from one object. This reduces repeated parameter plumbing and allows the callee to use additional related information later without changing every intermediate signature. It should not be used to expose internal state indiscriminately or to bypass a module boundary that should remain independent.
Study instruction:
- "Find a method passing several of its fields and compare passing this with passing a smaller purpose-built data object."
Key point:
- A self-reference expresses that the object is the unit of information.
- Pass the object only when the callee should depend on it as a whole.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Preserve Whole Object
22. Q: What is Replace Parameter with Query?
A: Show answer
Replace Parameter with Query removes a parameter when the callee can appropriately obtain the value itself from information already available to it. References to the parameter are replaced by a query or calculation, then the parameter is removed from the function declaration. This simplifies callers when they were computing a value that the callee can determine from another explicit argument or its stable state. The technique must not introduce unwanted dependencies or cause a previously predictable function to read mutable global data.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a parameter whose value can be derived from other information already owned by the callee."
Key point:
- Remove redundant inputs when the callee properly owns the calculation.
- Do not create hidden or unstable dependencies just to shorten a signature.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Parameter with Query
23. Q: How should a developer decide whether a parameter can be obtained just as easily by the callee?
A: Show answer
Consider the source of the value, the work needed to derive it, and which module should own that knowledge. If the value is a simple query on another parameter or on stable state within the callee’s responsibility, moving the calculation inward can simplify callers. If obtaining it requires a new service dependency, access to global state, or knowledge that should remain with the caller, keep the parameter. The design question is where responsibility belongs, not merely how many arguments appear in the signature.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the origin of a candidate value and compare the dependency cost of deriving it inside the callee."
Key point:
- Transfer responsibility only to the component that properly owns the information.
- A shorter signature is not a sufficient reason to introduce coupling.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Parameter with Query
24. Q: Why should Replace Parameter with Query avoid mutable global data?
A: Show answer
A function with referential transparency produces the same result when invoked with the same relevant inputs and unchanged dependencies. Replacing an explicit parameter with a query against mutable global data can weaken that property because the result may change even when the formal arguments do not. This increases hidden coupling and makes tests depend on external setup. The refactoring is safest when the value is derived from another explicit argument or from state whose stability is part of the function’s contract, not from uncontrolled mutable global state.
Study instruction:
- "Run the same function with identical explicit arguments while varying a proposed global dependency; assess whether the new behavior remains predictable."
Key point:
- Do not hide mutable inputs inside a function to reduce its argument count.
- Preserve referential transparency when it benefits reasoning and testing.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Parameter with Query
25. Q: What are the mechanics for Replace Parameter with Query?
A: Show answer
Extract the parameter calculation into a named function if the expression is complex. Replace each internal reference to the parameter with a call to the query, testing after every replacement. Once the function body no longer relies on the supplied parameter, remove it from the declaration and update callers. This order separates the behavioral change from the signature change and helps reveal any remaining dependency on the old value. Ensure that tests cover boundary conditions and that the new query returns the same value the former caller supplied.
Study instruction:
- "Replace internal uses first, then remove the parameter and verify the complete set of callers."
Key point:
- Change the implementation before changing the declaration.
- Tests should prove that the new query matches the former supplied value.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Parameter with Query
26. Q: What is Replace Query with Parameter?
A: Show answer
Replace Query with Parameter moves responsibility for obtaining a value from the called function to its caller. A function that previously read a global object or surrounding module state instead receives the required value through its signature. This can reduce coupling, make data flow explicit, and support pure or referentially transparent calculations. It also makes callers responsible for obtaining and passing the value, so the API may become more cumbersome. The best use is where a dependency should be removed from a module, especially when the new parameter makes the function easier to test independently.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a function that queries a global or surrounding object and make the queried value an explicit input."
Key point:
- Explicit parameters reveal dependencies at the function boundary.
- Moving a query outward increases responsibility for callers.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Query with Parameter
27. Q: How does Replace Query with Parameter improve testability?
A: Show answer
A function that reads mutable state from its scope may produce different results for the same explicit arguments. Moving that state to an explicit parameter makes the dependency visible and lets tests provide controlled values. The function can then focus on computation while an outer layer handles I/O and changing environment state. This is useful when separating pure business logic from gateways, user interfaces, or other environment-specific components. Testability improves because tests no longer need to arrange hidden global conditions to exercise the calculation.
Study instruction:
- "Separate environment access from a calculation and test the inner function with fixed input values."
Key point:
- Explicit inputs make tests repeatable and dependencies visible.
- Keep I/O and variable environment state in an outer boundary where practical.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Query with Parameter
28. Q: What costs can Replace Query with Parameter impose on callers?
A: Show answer
The caller must now obtain the value that the callee used to query, which may require more knowledge, additional parameters, or access to services. If several layers pass the value through without using it, the change can create parameter plumbing and weaken encapsulation. A caller-facing API can therefore become less convenient even while the target function becomes more independent. Weigh improved module boundaries and testability against caller complexity; parameterize only when the dependency being removed is genuinely undesirable.
Study instruction:
- "Map all callers and intermediate functions that would need to provide a proposed new parameter; identify where that knowledge belongs."
Key point:
- Dependency reduction may move complexity upstream.
- Avoid parameter plumbing that merely relocates unwanted coupling.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Query with Parameter
29. Q: How do Replace Parameter with Query and Replace Query with Parameter differ?
A: Show answer
They are inverse techniques that allocate responsibility in opposite directions. Replace Parameter with Query asks the callee to determine a value that the caller previously supplied; this can simplify calls but may add dependencies to the callee. Replace Query with Parameter asks the caller to determine a value that the callee previously read; this can isolate the callee but complicates callers. The choice depends on ownership of the information, module boundaries, referential transparency, and testability rather than a universal preference for short or long signatures.
Study instruction:
- "Write both forms of a function that currently queries a service and compare the direction of dependency and caller burden."
Key point:
- One refactoring moves responsibility inward; the other moves it outward.
- Choose the direction that yields the clearest dependency boundary.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Parameter with Query
- Martin Fowler — Replace Query with Parameter
30. Q: What is Remove Setting Method?
A: Show answer
Remove Setting Method eliminates a setter for a field that should not change after an object has been created. A setter tells clients that the field is mutable, even when the domain says it is fixed. Moving the initial value into the constructor and removing the setter communicates that the value is established during construction. This is useful for identifiers or stable configuration fields, but not for attributes that legitimately change during the object’s lifecycle. The public API should reflect actual domain rules rather than accidental construction habits.
Study instruction:
- "Review setters and classify each field as constructor-only or legitimately mutable throughout an object’s lifecycle."
Key point:
- A setter advertises post-construction mutability.
- Remove it when the field is meant to remain stable after creation.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Setting Method
31. Q: How does removing a setter clarify immutability?
A: Show answer
Constructor-only initialization narrows the set of state transitions available to clients. It communicates that a value belongs to the object’s identity or initial configuration and cannot be casually changed later. Fewer state transitions simplify reasoning, testing, sharing, and sometimes concurrency. A language-supported readonly field or immutable value can reinforce the contract. However, making one field immutable does not automatically make nested objects immutable, so the full object graph and any indirect mutation paths should be considered.
Study instruction:
- "Move an identifier into constructor initialization and inspect all remaining ways the value might be changed, including indirect references."
Key point:
- Constructor-only initialization communicates stable object properties.
- Immutability reduces unintended state changes but must cover relevant references.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Setting Method
32. Q: What is the safe sequence for removing a setting method?
A: Show answer
Add the field’s initial value to the constructor if it is not already an argument, and initialize the value there. Update every object-creation script to pass the value when constructing the object, removing each later setter call one caller at a time. Test after every migration. When no caller relies on the setter, inline or remove it and make the field immutable if possible. If callers mutate a shared reference object and cannot replace it by creating a new instance, abandon this refactoring rather than changing object identity semantics.
Study instruction:
- "Trace creation sites, migrate setter calls incrementally, and test identity-sensitive consumers before removing the setter."
Key point:
- Move initialization before deleting the mutator.
- Do not break shared-reference behavior merely to remove a setter.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Setting Method
33. Q: How can a team distinguish a truly immutable field from one that is merely set once today?
A: Show answer
Use the domain lifecycle and contract rather than call frequency alone. An identifier may be guaranteed never to change after creation, while a person’s name, status, or configuration may change through legitimate business events. Check persistence rules, synchronization, external interfaces, and future lifecycle transitions. Tests and documentation should make the invariant explicit. Removing a setter is appropriate when mutation is invalid in the domain, not simply because current code happens to initialize the field once.
Study instruction:
- "For each candidate field, document valid state transitions after construction and verify whether any are required."
Key point:
- Immutability is a domain contract, not an assumption based on current usage.
- Base setter removal on lifecycle rules and business requirements.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Setting Method
34. Q: What is Replace Constructor with Factory Function?
A: Show answer
Replace Constructor with Factory Function changes direct construction into a call to a named function that returns the desired object. The factory can have a descriptive name, validate or normalize input, choose a subclass or proxy, and hide a concrete construction strategy. It may call the constructor internally, but as a regular function it is not constrained by all language rules for constructors. Callers depend on the creation contract rather than the exact new-expression or concrete implementation.
Study instruction:
- "Wrap a direct constructor call in a named factory and identify which construction choices it should own."
Key point:
- A factory makes creation intent explicit and flexible.
- The caller should depend on the factory contract rather than construction details.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Constructor with Factory Function
35. Q: What constructor limitations can a factory function overcome?
A: Show answer
Depending on the language, constructors may have fixed names, a special invocation form, and restrictions on which object they can return. A factory can select a subclass or proxy based on the environment or input, return a cached or specialized instance, or provide a name such as createEngineer that communicates the intended kind. These capabilities are valuable when creation logic is conditional or the constructor API is awkward. A factory should not be introduced without a concrete benefit, because a simple constructor can remain the clearest way to create a straightforward object.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a constructor limitation in the target language and show how a regular factory can address that limitation."
Key point:
- Factories permit richer creation decisions and expressive names.
- Introduce a factory to solve an actual creation problem, not as unnecessary ceremony.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Constructor with Factory Function
36. Q: When is a specialized factory clearer than a generic factory with a type code?
A: Show answer
When the caller already knows the intended category, a specialized factory such as createEngineer(name) is clearer than createEmployee(name, "E"). The specialized name hides the code and reduces the chance that callers supply an invalid or misunderstood literal. A generic factory remains appropriate when the type is genuinely data-driven, such as when it comes from a validated record. Both can coexist: the generic function handles dynamic selection, while named factories express common, explicit creation intentions.
Study instruction:
- "Replace a literal type-code call with a named factory and identify callers that still need dynamic type selection."
Key point:
- Named factories communicate known choices directly.
- Keep generic factories for genuinely data-driven construction.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Constructor with Factory Function
37. Q: How should direct constructor calls be migrated to a factory?
A: Show answer
Create a factory that initially delegates to the existing constructor. Replace each direct constructor call with a factory call and test after each change. Where callers pass literal type codes, consider specialized factories with domain-oriented names. Once relevant callers have migrated, restrict constructor visibility to the extent the language allows. This creates a stable creation boundary before adding more complex selection logic and avoids changing every caller at the same time as the construction algorithm.
Study instruction:
- "Introduce a delegating factory, migrate direct construction sites one at a time, then consider restricting constructor access."
Key point:
- A delegating factory creates a safe migration seam.
- Limit direct construction after clients have adopted the new interface.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Constructor with Factory Function
38. Q: What is Replace Function with Command?
A: Show answer
Replace Function with Command encapsulates an operation and its inputs in a dedicated object with an execution method. The command can hold parameters and intermediate state in fields and can support additional lifecycle operations, undo behavior, hooks, inheritance, or staged configuration. It can also provide structure for breaking down a very complex function whose local variables are difficult to pass among extracted helpers. The object is justified when this flexibility is useful; it is not automatically better than an ordinary function.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a complex function with many locals and sketch a command class, its constructor parameters, execute method, and potential helper operations."
Key point:
- A command encapsulates a request and its execution state.
- Use it when lifecycle or decomposition needs justify the added structure.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
39. Q: When is a command object preferable to an ordinary function?
A: Show answer
Use a command when the operation needs capabilities that a plain function does not express conveniently, such as undo, queued execution through a common interface, staged configuration, hooks, specialization, or shared mutable state across helper methods. It can also be useful in languages with limited support for first-class or nested functions. If the computation is simple and needs none of these capabilities, a regular function is easier to understand, test, and call. The decision should be based on a concrete need, not on an assumption that every operation belongs in an object.
Study instruction:
- "List which command capabilities a proposed design actually requires and remove speculative capabilities from the justification."
Key point:
- Commands are valuable for lifecycle, customization, and shared state.
- Prefer functions when those facilities are unnecessary.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
40. Q: What are the mechanics for replacing a function with a command?
A: Show answer
Create an empty class named after the operation and move the function into an execute or call method. Keep the old function as a forwarding wrapper while the move is in progress so existing callers continue to work. Then move arguments into constructor fields, one at a time, and test after each change. Once the execute method uses the stored fields, its signature can be simplified. The command can then be decomposed into smaller methods that share its state, with the forwarding wrapper retained or removed according to the final API.
Study instruction:
