- "Move the function body first, retain a forwarding wrapper, then transfer one argument at a time into command fields."
Key point:
- A forwarding wrapper preserves the existing call interface during migration.
- Move inputs and state incrementally so each step remains testable.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
41. Q: Why can moving local variables into command fields simplify a long algorithm?
A: Show answer
A long function may have local variables read and modified by many parts of the algorithm. Extracting helpers can be difficult because they depend on large sets of locals or on scope-specific state. Moving these variables into command fields gives helper methods a shared, explicit place to read and update that state. This makes it easier to extract coherent algorithmic steps. The trade-off is mutable object state, which must be initialized correctly and should not be accidentally reused across executions with stale values.
Study instruction:
- "Move one local variable into a command field and identify which helper owns and modifies it; verify initialization on every execution."
Key point:
- Command fields can support decomposition of complex shared state.
- Define initialization and reuse rules for mutable command state.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
42. Q: How should command arguments move from execute to the constructor?
A: Show answer
Add one argument to the constructor and store it in a field. Update the forwarding function to pass that argument when creating the command, while the execute method may temporarily keep the parameter. Replace one use in the method body with the field and run tests, then repeat for the remaining uses. Once the method no longer depends on the supplied parameters, remove them from execute. This incremental approach exposes missed references and makes a failed test easier to associate with a particular migration step.
Study instruction:
- "Transfer each execute argument separately and test that the corresponding field is used before removing the old parameter."
Key point:
- Move inputs into command state incrementally.
- Remove execute parameters only after their uses are replaced and tested.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
43. Q: What risks arise when a simple function is turned into a command object?
A: Show answer
The command adds a class, constructor, method indirection, object allocation, and lifecycle concerns that may not be needed. It can make a small calculation harder to follow and may introduce stale state if an instance is reused. It may also create an unnecessary framework around a single operation. Use commands when their fields and methods genuinely simplify a complex function or enable meaningful lifecycle behavior; otherwise use function extraction, nested functions, or direct code. The simplest suitable abstraction is usually preferable.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the original function and proposed command; identify the concrete benefit of every new class, field, and method."
Key point:
- Command flexibility carries additional conceptual and lifecycle cost.
- Do not add an object layer without a meaningful design benefit.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
44. Q: What is Replace Command with Function?
A: Show answer
Replace Command with Function reverses the command-object transformation when the computation is no longer complex enough to justify its class. The constructor, execute method, and command-only helpers are collapsed into a regular function with explicit inputs and a direct result. This removes unnecessary indirection and dead command structure. It is appropriate after an algorithm has been simplified or when the original lifecycle and customization needs no longer exist. The goal is to fit the abstraction to the current computation rather than preserve a command merely because it was once useful.
Study instruction:
- "Review a command class and determine whether its lifecycle, state, and helper methods still justify an object."
Key point:
- Use a function when a command no longer provides meaningful capabilities.
- Remove structure that has become unnecessary as the algorithm simplifies.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Command with Function
45. Q: What are the mechanics for converting a simple command back into a function?
A: Show answer
Extract a function around command creation and invocation so callers use a named function. Inline command-only helpers into the execute method, extracting local variables where needed to preserve clarity. Add constructor parameters to the execution method, replace field references with parameters one at a time, and test each substitution. Then inline construction and execution into the new function, run the tests, and remove the now-unused command class. Keeping each step separate makes missed field references easier to detect.
Study instruction:
- "Perform the conversion in stages and verify parameter substitutions before deleting the command class."
Key point:
- Move constructor data back to explicit function parameters before removing the command.
- Remove the class only after all execution behavior has transferred and passed tests.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Command with Function
46. Q: How should supporting methods be treated when collapsing a command into a function?
A: Show answer
Classify each supporting method as an independently useful domain operation or a helper that exists only to divide the command’s implementation. A command-only helper can often be inlined into the main calculation; extract a local variable first if the helper returns a value used in the expression. Keep methods that represent reusable or meaningful domain operations. The resulting function should remain readable rather than flattening every step into an opaque block. The aim is to remove unnecessary command-specific indirection while preserving useful abstractions.
Study instruction:
- "Review each helper in a command and justify whether it is independently reusable or merely a local implementation step."
Key point:
- Inline command-only helpers when their separate identity is no longer useful.
- Retain meaningful reusable domain operations.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Command with Function
47. Q: How do Replace Function with Command and Replace Command with Function relate?
A: Show answer
They are inverse refactorings used at different stages of software evolution. The first adds an object boundary when a function needs shared state, staged inputs, extensibility, undo, or richer lifecycle control. The second removes the object boundary when the computation becomes straightforward and those capabilities are no longer needed. Their relationship demonstrates that abstractions should respond to current needs rather than become permanent by habit. A design may move in either direction as requirements and algorithm complexity change.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a realistic sequence in which a long function becomes a command for decomposition and later returns to a function after simplification."
Key point:
- The techniques are inverse tools for adapting abstraction to the work.
- Refactoring may add or remove structure as needs change.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Function with Command
- Martin Fowler — Replace Command with Function
48. Q: How should public API compatibility be preserved during refactoring?
A: Show answer
When callers exist in many modules or external applications, introduce the new interface alongside the old one. Keep a forwarding wrapper or compatibility adapter while callers migrate individually. Deprecate the old form where appropriate, document the replacement, and remove it only when compatibility obligations are met. A public library may require a deprecation period, release notes, or a major-version change. Internal APIs can move more quickly, but tests should still protect all callers and ensure the wrapper preserves the original observable behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a migration plan for a public signature change, including wrappers, caller updates, deprecation, versioning, and removal criteria."
Key point:
- Transitional wrappers allow incremental interface changes.
- External consumers may require formal deprecation and release planning.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
49. Q: How should automated tests protect API refactorings?
A: Show answer
Tests should capture observable behavior before changing signatures or object lifecycles. For query/modifier separation, test both the returned information and the action. For parameterization and flag removal, cover every original variant and call form. For object passing and parameter direction, test boundary data and dependency behavior. For setters, factories, and commands, test initialization, object type, results, side effects, and lifecycle. Run focused tests after each small change, followed by the broader regression suite. Static analysis and type checking can additionally detect callers that still use the old signature.
Study instruction:
- "Build a test matrix for normal, boundary, exceptional, and compatibility cases before starting an API refactoring."
Key point:
- Verify the complete observable contract, not just a return value.
- Incremental testing makes signature and lifecycle changes safer.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
50. Q: How should a developer decide where a value should be obtained in an API?
A: Show answer
Determine which component has the domain knowledge and appropriate access to the source of the value. If the callee can derive it from stable information it already owns, Replace Parameter with Query may simplify callers. If the callee should not depend on a global or external service, Replace Query with Parameter can make its inputs explicit. If several values form a cohesive record, Preserve Whole Object may be appropriate. Balance caller convenience, dependency direction, testability, referential transparency, and the stability of the module boundary.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the data flow from source to consumer for a complex call and assign responsibility for obtaining each value."
Key point:
- An API signature allocates knowledge and dependencies across modules.
- Choose parameter direction and granularity based on domain ownership and testability.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Parameter with Query
- Martin Fowler — Replace Query with Parameter
- Martin Fowler — Preserve Whole Object
51. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 11, Refactoring APIs?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats APIs as design boundaries that should make intent, responsibilities, and dependencies explicit. Separate Query from Modifier distinguishes reading from changing state. Parameterize Function removes genuine algorithm duplication, while Remove Flag Argument makes distinct operations visible. Preserve Whole Object keeps related data together when that dependency is appropriate. Replace Parameter with Query and Replace Query with Parameter allocate responsibility and control coupling. Remove Setting Method communicates stable object state; factories provide flexible creation; commands add structure for complex computations when justified. Replace Command with Function removes that structure when it is no longer useful. Across all techniques, incremental changes and tests protect existing callers.
Study instruction:
- "Create a decision table for all ten techniques showing the API smell, main trade-off, and tests needed before applying it."
Key point:
- Good APIs expose intent, separate responsibilities, and make dependencies visible.
- Use only the abstraction that solves the current problem and preserve behavior through testing.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 11: Refactoring APIs
Rows: 51
Table 12
Choosing and Refactoring Inheritance Relationships
1. Q: What is the central purpose of refactoring inheritance relationships?
A: Show answer
Inheritance is a powerful mechanism for sharing behavior and expressing substitutability, but its value can change as a system evolves. The chapter provides techniques for moving common features up a hierarchy, moving specialized features down, creating or removing classes, replacing type codes with subclasses, and replacing an unsuitable inheritance relationship with delegation. The goal is not to maximize or eliminate inheritance. It is to make the hierarchy reflect real behavioral commonality and valid domain relationships while controlling duplication, coupling, and the cost of change.
Study instruction:
- Draw a class hierarchy from a current project and mark duplicated features, features used by only a few subclasses, type-dependent behavior, and questionable subtype relationships.
Key point:
- Use inheritance where it expresses shared behavior and valid substitutability.
- Refactor the hierarchy as the system’s responsibilities and variation points evolve.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 12: Dealing with Inheritance
2. Q: When is inheritance a useful way to share behavior?
A: Show answer
Inheritance is useful when subclasses are genuine specializations of a common concept, share stable data or behavior, and can be used wherever the superclass is expected. The superclass can provide common implementation while subclasses add or override well-defined differences. This reduces duplication and allows polymorphic calls through a common interface. It becomes problematic when subclasses do not satisfy the parent’s contract, inherit operations that make no sense for them, or are forced to share a class solely to reuse implementation. Model the semantic relationship, not just the similarity of code.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed hierarchy, test whether every instance of each subclass can validly be used as an instance of its superclass.
Key point:
- Inheritance combines code reuse with a substitutable type relationship.
- Shared implementation alone is not sufficient reason to create a subtype.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 12: Dealing with Inheritance
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
3. Q: What problem does Pull Up Method solve?
A: Show answer
Pull Up Method moves identical or behaviorally equivalent methods from several subclasses into their superclass. Duplicate implementations are dangerous because later fixes may be applied to one copy but missed in another. Once a shared method is in the superclass, all subclasses inherit the same implementation. The refactoring is straightforward when method bodies match exactly; when they differ only in names, parameters, or small details, normalize those differences first. Similar workflows with different steps may need a template-method design rather than a direct pull-up.
Study instruction:
- Find methods in sibling subclasses that perform the same operation and compare their full behavior before moving either one.
Key point:
- A shared method belongs in the common superclass when its behavior is genuinely common.
- Removing duplicates prevents inconsistent fixes across subclasses.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
4. Q: How should Pull Up Method be performed safely?
A: Show answer
Inspect candidate methods and confirm that they do the same thing, including boundary behavior and side effects. Normalize different names or signatures with function-declaration changes where necessary. Check that every field and method referenced in the body is accessible from the superclass. Copy one implementation into the superclass, run static checks, remove one subclass copy, and test. Repeat for the remaining subclasses only after the first removal succeeds. Looking carefully at differences often reveals missing tests or behavior that should not be unified.
Study instruction:
- Compare the candidate implementations line by line and execute tests after moving the first copy and deleting each remaining copy.
Key point:
- Verify semantics and dependencies before moving the method.
- Move and remove copies incrementally with tests at each step.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
5. Q: What if a method being pulled up refers to subclass-only fields or methods?
A: Show answer
The superclass cannot safely host a method that depends on state or behavior available only in one subclass unless the dependency is moved or represented by a suitable abstraction. First consider Pull Up Field or Pull Up Method for the required feature if it is genuinely common. If the feature is not common, parameterize or restructure the operation so the superclass can work through an appropriate contract. In a dynamically typed language, code may appear to work despite an undeclared dependency, but a trap or abstract method can make the subclass responsibility explicit. Static languages usually require the common contract to be declared.
Study instruction:
- List every field and method used by a candidate method and identify whether the superclass can legally access each dependency.
Key point:
- A pulled-up method must depend only on superclass-visible capabilities.
- Dynamic-language success does not eliminate hidden subclass dependencies.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
6. Q: How can Parameterize Function prepare methods for Pull Up Method?
A: Show answer
Two subclass methods may follow the same algorithm but use different constants or values. Parameterize Function can first make those differences explicit inputs so the method bodies become equivalent. Once the variations are represented as data, one implementation can be moved to the superclass. This small-step sequence is safer than attempting to move a nearly identical method and leaving subclass-specific assumptions embedded in the superclass. If the differing steps represent separate domain behavior rather than values, a template-method or polymorphic design may be more appropriate.
Study instruction:
- Parameterize the varying literal values in two sibling methods, compare their resulting bodies, and only then attempt a pull-up.
Key point:
- Normalize data differences before unifying behavior.
- Do not force genuinely different workflows into one superclass method.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
7. Q: What is Pull Up Field?
A: Show answer
Pull Up Field moves equivalent fields declared separately in sibling subclasses into their superclass. It removes duplicated data declarations and can enable methods that use the field to move upward as well. Fields need not have identical names at first, but their values and usage must represent the same concept. In dynamic languages where fields are created by assignment, the change may be closely related to moving common constructor initialization. In static languages, visibility and access rules must be considered when choosing the new superclass field.
Study instruction:
- Compare candidate fields in sibling classes and record how each is initialized, read, written, and interpreted.
Key point:
- A field can be shared when the subclasses use it in the same conceptual way.
- Moving data upward can enable related behavior to move upward too.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
8. Q: How do you verify that two subclass fields represent the same concept?
A: Show answer
Inspect more than the field names. Compare their types, initialization rules, default values, mutability, invariants, and every place that reads or writes them. Fields named differently may still hold the same domain information, while identically named fields may have different meanings. If one subclass stores a monthly cost and another stores a total cost, they should not be unified merely because both are numeric. A valid pull-up establishes one superclass-level contract for the shared value.
Study instruction:
- Trace all reads and writes of the candidate fields and document their units, invariants, and lifecycle before moving them.
Key point:
- Field equivalence is semantic, not merely textual.
- A misleading shared field can create incorrect assumptions throughout the hierarchy.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
9. Q: What are the mechanics for Pull Up Field?
A: Show answer
Inspect all uses of the candidate field to ensure equivalent meaning and usage. Rename fields in subclasses if different names obscure their common role. Create the field in the superclass with visibility that allows required subclass access, then remove the duplicate declarations from the subclasses. Run tests after the move and verify constructor initialization, defaults, access control, serialization, and any reflection-based code. If the fields are established in constructors, align the initialization paths as part of the migration rather than leaving duplicated assignments behind.
Study instruction:
- Normalize names, move the field, delete subclass copies, and test each subclass’s construction and access behavior.
Key point:
- Confirm common use before centralizing the field.
- Choose visibility and initialization rules that preserve the superclass contract.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
10. Q: What is Pull Up Constructor Body?
A: Show answer
Pull Up Constructor Body moves common initialization statements from subclass constructors into the superclass constructor. Constructor code cannot always be treated like ordinary methods because the superclass constructor call must occur in a prescribed order. The refactoring identifies common assignments, positions them after the super call in each subclass, and then transfers them to the superclass constructor. Values used by the common code become superclass constructor parameters. This centralizes shared object initialization while preserving subclass-specific construction work in the subclasses.
Study instruction:
- Compare sibling constructors and highlight common statements, their required arguments, and statements that must remain subclass-specific.
Key point:
- Common initialization belongs in the superclass constructor when it applies to all subclasses.
- Respect constructor ordering and call semantics while moving code.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Constructor Body
11. Q: What sequence makes Pull Up Constructor Body safe?
A: Show answer
Define a superclass constructor if one does not exist and ensure subclass constructors invoke it. Slide shared statements to a common position immediately after the superclass call. Remove those statements from the subclasses and place them in the superclass constructor, passing any required constructor arguments through the super call. Test after the shared initialization has moved. If common logic cannot be placed at the start because it depends on subclass state initialized later, extract that logic into a method and pull the method up after its call can occur safely.
Study instruction:
- Move one common constructor assignment at a time, respecting initialization order and testing both superclass and subclass construction.
Key point:
- Move shared initialization only after constructor ordering has been made compatible.
- Use a pulled-up method for common work that cannot execute in the superclass constructor body directly.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Constructor Body
12. Q: How should common constructor logic that must run after subclass initialization be handled?
A: Show answer
Some shared behavior depends on values that only the subclass can initialize. Moving that logic directly into the superclass constructor would run it too early. Extract the later common statements into a method called after the subclass has initialized the required state, then use Pull Up Method to move that method into the superclass. This preserves the timing of the behavior while centralizing its implementation. Be careful with constructor-time virtual dispatch, since calling overridable methods during construction can expose partially initialized objects.
Study instruction:
- Identify the dependency that prevents constructor code from moving upward, extract a completion method, and verify when it is invoked in the object lifecycle.
Key point:
- Initialization order determines whether code can move directly into a superclass constructor.
- A post-initialization method can centralize shared late work when its timing is safe.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Constructor Body
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
13. Q: What is Push Down Method?
A: Show answer
Push Down Method moves a method from the superclass into the subclass or subclasses that actually need it. A method that is relevant to only one specialized type should not necessarily appear in the public interface of every superclass instance. Moving it down makes the class contract more precise and reduces the chance that clients call an operation on an object for which it has no meaningful behavior. The refactoring is appropriate when callers already know they are dealing with the relevant subtype; otherwise, callers may need a polymorphic contract or a deliberate default behavior.
Study instruction:
- Find superclass methods used by only one or a small group of subclasses and inspect whether callers know the required subtype.
Key point:
- Keep specialized operations on the types that support them.
- Do not remove a superclass capability while unknown callers still depend on it.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Method
14. Q: Why must callers know the subtype before applying Push Down Method?
A: Show answer
After a method leaves the superclass, a caller that holds only a superclass reference can no longer rely on that method being available. If the caller’s behavior depends on which subtype it has, the system may need a polymorphic operation with a default implementation or a different dispatch design. Push Down Method is safe when callers already have a type-specific context or can be updated to use a subtype-specific interface. The key is that moving a method changes the API surface available to superclass-typed references.
Study instruction:
- Trace every caller of the candidate method and check the declared type available at each call site.
Key point:
- A method can move down only when its callers can still access the correct subtype contract.
- Use polymorphism when callers need to work uniformly across several subtypes.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Method
15. Q: What is Push Down Field?
A: Show answer
Push Down Field moves a field from a superclass to the subclass or subclasses that actually use it. This narrows the superclass’s state to what is common across the hierarchy and places specialized data with the type that owns its meaning. It is useful when a field such as sales quota applies only to sales roles rather than every employee. The change can clarify the model, reduce irrelevant state, and make invalid field usage harder. Existing serialization, persistence, and constructors must be updated consistently.
Study instruction:
- Identify superclass fields that are read or written only by a subset of subclasses and list their valid owners.
Key point:
- Fields should live with the types for which they have meaning.
- Removing irrelevant superclass state clarifies the model.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Field
16. Q: How are Push Down Method and Push Down Field performed?
A: Show answer
For a method, copy it into each subclass that needs it, remove the superclass version, test, then remove unnecessary copies from subclasses that do not need it and test again. For a field, declare it in each subclass that needs it, remove the superclass field, test, then remove any newly created subclass fields that are not required. The separate steps preserve availability while the hierarchy is being changed. Do not assume the method and field have exactly the same set of owners; verify each usage independently.
Study instruction:
- Create an ownership matrix for candidate methods and fields, then migrate each feature only to its actual consumers.
Key point:
- Copy or declare the specialized feature before removing its superclass form.
- Test after each stage to reveal incomplete ownership changes.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Method
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Field
17. Q: How do Pull Up and Push Down refactorings complement each other?
A: Show answer
Pull Up Method and Pull Up Field move genuinely shared behavior and data toward the common parent. Push Down Method and Push Down Field move specialized capabilities toward the subclasses that own them. These operations can be used iteratively as requirements reveal which features are common and which are variant-specific. The goal is not to move everything in one direction but to keep the hierarchy balanced: the superclass should contain the stable common contract, and subclasses should contain their meaningful differences. Each movement must preserve access and caller expectations.
Study instruction:
- Choose one feature in a class hierarchy and justify whether it belongs at the root, in one child, or in multiple children.
Key point:
- Pull common features up and specialized features down.
- The correct location follows usage and domain meaning, not hierarchy preference.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Method
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Field
18. Q: What is Replace Type Code with Subclasses?
A: Show answer
Replace Type Code with Subclasses replaces an enum, string, symbol, or numeric field that selects a kind of object with concrete subclasses representing those kinds. The subclasses can provide polymorphic behavior and contain fields or methods valid only for their category. This is useful when several operations branch on the same type code or when particular data applies only to one type. It is not required for every type code; a simple code may be entirely adequate if no variant-specific behavior or structure is needed.
Study instruction:
- Find repeated type-code switches or fields valid only for particular values and decide whether subclasses would express those differences more directly.
Key point:
- Subclasses make stable variations explicit in the type system.
- Use them when the code selects meaningful behavior or data, not for every enum.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
19. Q: How should a developer choose between direct and indirect subclasses for a type code?
A: Show answer
Direct inheritance makes each specialized class a subclass of the main domain class, which is simple when the type is stable and the object has no other inheritance need. Indirect inheritance wraps the type code in its own type object and creates subclasses of that type object. This is useful when the main object already has a separate subclass hierarchy or when the type can change during the object’s lifetime. Direct inheritance is less complex, but it consumes the object’s single inheritance path and makes changing type harder.
Study instruction:
- Check whether the object already has subclasses and whether the type may change; use those facts to choose direct or indirect specialization.
Key point:
- Direct subclasses are simpler when the type is stable and no other hierarchy is needed.
- An indirect type object preserves other inheritance options and supports mutable classification.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
20. Q: What are the mechanics for replacing a type code with subclasses?
A: Show answer
First encapsulate the type code behind an accessor so callers no longer depend directly on the field. Select one value, create a subclass that represents it, and override the type accessor to return the appropriate value. Add selector logic that maps incoming type information to the new subclass, often in a factory function for direct inheritance. Test this first case, then add the remaining subclasses one at a time. Once every value is represented, remove the original type-code field and replace remaining behavioral conditionals with polymorphic methods or pushed-down behavior.
Study instruction:
- Encapsulate the discriminator, create one subclass, route its construction through a selector, test, and repeat for each value.
Key point:
- Change the representation incrementally and preserve a factory boundary.
- Remove the type code only after all construction and behavior use the new hierarchy.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
- undefined
21. Q: Why might a type code need to be represented by an indirect type hierarchy?
A: Show answer
An object may already use inheritance for another dimension, such as employment status or account category. It cannot also inherit directly from each type-code variant if the language permits only one superclass. A type may also change over time, while changing an object’s class is often impractical or unsafe. Wrapping the code in a dedicated value object creates a separate hierarchy for the type dimension. The owner object then holds a type object that can be replaced without changing the owner’s own class.
Study instruction:
- Design a class with two independent classification dimensions and show why separate type objects may be clearer than multiplying subclasses.
Key point:
- A class hierarchy usually represents one principal inheritance axis.
- A separate type object can model another axis or a mutable classification.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
22. Q: What risks arise when using type-code subclasses for a mutable category?
A: Show answer
If the category may change after the object is created, representing it through the object’s own subclass makes that change difficult because the runtime class is normally fixed. A setter cannot safely turn one instance of a subclass into another. Indirect inheritance solves this by storing a replaceable type object, but introduces another level of dispatch and object relationships. Before choosing the pattern, determine whether the type is a stable identity or a mutable attribute, and select the simpler representation that respects the lifecycle.
Study instruction:
- List possible type changes and determine whether the class identity or only a contained type value should change.
Key point:
- Direct subclasses fit stable categories better than mutable classifications.
- Use a replaceable type object when classification must vary independently of object identity.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
23. Q: What is Remove Subclass?
A: Show answer
Remove Subclass eliminates a subclass whose differences no longer justify a separate class. A subclass may have been created for behavior that was later moved elsewhere, or for features that were never implemented. The remaining distinction can often be represented by a field on the superclass, allowing the superclass to handle the small variation directly. Before removing it, inspect callers for subtype checks and behavior that may still be worth moving into the superclass. A subclass that only adds conceptual overhead is a candidate for removal.
Study instruction:
- Find a subclass with little unique behavior and determine whether its remaining distinction can be represented as data.
Key point:
- A subclass should justify its cost through real variation or behavior.
- When differences shrink to a value, a field may be clearer than a class.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Subclass
24. Q: What is the safe sequence for Remove Subclass?
A: Show answer
Encapsulate construction with a factory before changing the representation. Extract subtype tests into named functions and move those tests to the superclass so clients no longer refer directly to concrete classes. Add a field to represent the subtype distinction, update behavior and callers to use the field, then update the factory to create the superclass with the appropriate value. Remove the subclass and test. For a group of subclasses, establish the factory and encapsulated type tests first, then fold each subtype into the superclass in turn.
Study instruction:
- Replace direct construction and instanceof checks with a factory and superclass query before removing the first subclass.
Key point:
- Hide subtype knowledge in a controlled creation and query interface.
- Remove one subclass at a time and keep behavior tests around each conversion.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Subclass
25. Q: Why should direct instanceof checks be encapsulated before removing a subclass?
A: Show answer
Clients that use instanceof embed knowledge of a particular implementation class. Deleting that class would require finding and changing every such test. Extracting each test into a named query and moving it to the superclass creates a stable API independent of the concrete subclass representation. Once clients call the query, the underlying test can be changed to a field comparison or another representation without touching those clients. This is a general strategy for changing implementation: encapsulate the representation before replacing it.
Study instruction:
- Search for direct class checks and replace them with a domain query on the superclass; confirm no clients require the concrete class.
Key point:
- Subtype queries are part of the abstraction and should not leak unnecessarily.
- Encapsulating type tests makes later representation changes safer.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Subclass
26. Q: What is Extract Superclass?
A: Show answer
Extract Superclass creates a shared parent for two or more classes that have common behavior or data. The new superclass provides a place for common fields, constructors, and methods, while the original classes retain their specific features. It can be discovered during evolution rather than designed from a fixed real-world taxonomy. Extract Superclass is often a simpler first way to unify duplicate behavior than delegation, although delegation may be appropriate later if the inheritance relationship becomes too tight or semantically wrong.
Study instruction:
- Compare two classes that perform similar work and identify the common data and behavior suitable for a new superclass.
Key point:
- A shared superclass centralizes genuine commonality across related classes.
- Inheritance can emerge from refactoring as a design is understood more clearly.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
27. Q: What mechanics make Extract Superclass reliable?
A: Show answer
Create an empty superclass and make the existing classes extend it, adjusting constructors as needed. Test that the empty hierarchy preserves behavior. Move common constructor initialization, fields, and methods upward one by one using Pull Up Constructor Body, Pull Up Field, and Pull Up Method. For methods with similar flows, extract common steps and consider a template-method approach. Finally, review callers of the original classes to see whether they can depend on the new superclass interface. Incremental movement keeps the hierarchy valid while commonality is established.
Study instruction:
- Introduce an empty parent, test, move one shared feature at a time, and update client types only after the interface stabilizes.
Key point:
- Create the inheritance link before moving features.
- Verify each feature is genuinely common and each client can use the new abstraction.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Constructor Body
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
28. Q: How should a team choose between Extract Superclass and Extract Class?
A: Show answer
Extract Superclass uses inheritance to share behavior while preserving an is-a relationship between the original classes and the new parent. Extract Class uses composition or delegation to create a separate collaborator. If the two classes are genuine variants of one concept and clients can use them polymorphically, a superclass may be simpler. If their commonality is an implementation service but their domain identities are different, a separate class is safer. A superclass creates a closer coupling because changes to the parent can affect all children, so it should represent a coherent contract, not just convenient code reuse.
Study instruction:
- For two classes with duplicated behavior, test the semantic relationship and the expected direction of change before selecting inheritance or delegation.
Key point:
- Use inheritance for a valid shared type relationship.
- Use composition when the shared feature is a collaborator rather than a subtype identity.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
29. Q: Why should shared data often be moved before shared methods during Extract Superclass?
A: Show answer
When fields and constructor state are centralized first, methods can use a consistent superclass-level representation. Moving the data can also reveal whether apparent method duplication is genuinely the same behavior: two similar methods may rely on different values or different domain meanings. A practical sequence is to introduce the superclass, pull up the shared constructor state and fields, then move methods after their required data is available. The sequence is not an absolute rule, but it often reduces friction and clarifies the common contract.
Study instruction:
- Start an Extract Superclass exercise by listing constructor state and shared fields before selecting methods to move.
Key point:
- Shared data can establish the common foundation for shared behavior.
- Confirm common meaning before moving either state or methods.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
30. Q: What is Collapse Hierarchy?
A: Show answer
Collapse Hierarchy merges a class with its parent when they have become so similar that keeping both adds more complexity than value. A hierarchy may evolve through repeated pull-up and push-down changes until two neighboring classes no longer represent meaningful distinctions. Collapse Hierarchy selects one class to keep, moves the other class’s fields and methods into it, updates references, and removes the empty class. The retained name should be the one that best describes the future role of the combined concept.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a parent and child after several refactorings and decide whether the remaining distinction justifies two separate class identities.
Key point:
- A hierarchy is useful only while its levels represent meaningful differences.
- Merge neighboring classes when their separate concepts have disappeared.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Collapse Hierarchy
31. Q: How do you perform Collapse Hierarchy without losing features?
A: Show answer
Choose the class whose name best represents the combined future concept. Use Pull Up Field and Pull Up Method for features moving from child to parent, and Push Down Field or Push Down Method for features moving in the opposite direction. Ensure constructor behavior and references are transferred, then update clients to use the retained class. Remove the now-empty class and run tests. If neither name is clearly better, choose one deliberately and keep the naming decision consistent through call sites, documentation, and persistence mappings.
Study instruction:
- Build a member-by-member migration list and update references before deleting the class that will disappear.
Key point:
- Select the surviving abstraction before moving members.
- Transfer all state, behavior, construction, and references before removing the hierarchy level.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Collapse Hierarchy
32. Q: What is Replace Subclass with Delegate?
A: Show answer
Replace Subclass with Delegate moves subclass-specific behavior into a separate object owned by the former superclass. The host forwards requests to that delegate when it is present, while retaining its default behavior otherwise. This is useful when the host needs more than one independent axis of variation, when a category must change at runtime, or when the tight coupling between parent and child becomes difficult to manage. Delegation allows several independent collaborators and clearer interfaces, but adds forwarding methods, dispatch logic, and often a back-reference from the delegate to its host.
Study instruction:
- Identify a subclass that occupies the only inheritance slot or needs to change dynamically; sketch the delegate and the host interface.
Key point:
- Delegation represents a variation as a contained collaborator rather than a subclass.
- The added indirection is justified only when flexibility or decoupling is valuable.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
33. Q: Why might Replace Subclass with Delegate be necessary when inheritance currently works?
A: Show answer
Inheritance can be a good fit until the object needs another independent variation, such as both a species category and a wild-or-captive category. A class can inherit from only one parent in many languages, so one hierarchy may consume the available inheritance axis. A subclass may also need to be enabled or disabled dynamically without replacing an object referenced throughout the system. Delegation makes the variable behavior a field that can be changed, and permits multiple delegate objects for different concerns.
Study instruction:
- Model two independent variation dimensions and compare the number of subclasses required with a host that owns separate delegates.
Key point:
- Delegation supports independent and potentially mutable axes of variation.
- Do not replace a clean hierarchy unless a real limitation or coupling problem exists.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
34. Q: What are the main mechanics for Replace Subclass with Delegate?
A: Show answer
If many callers construct the subclass, first introduce factory functions to encapsulate construction. Create an empty delegate with subclass-specific data and usually a back-reference to the host. Add a delegate field to the superclass and initialize it for former subclass instances. Move one subclass method at a time into the delegate, retaining forwarding code in the original class during migration. Add guarded dispatch to the superclass when appropriate, test, and repeat. Once all behavior has moved, make factories return the superclass and remove the subclass.
Study instruction:
- Introduce factories, create the delegate and host reference, move one method at a time, test, then migrate construction.
Key point:
- Build the delegate structure before moving behavior.
- Preserve forwarding behavior until all callers and methods have migrated.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
35. Q: Why does a delegate often need a back-reference to its former superclass?
A: Show answer
A subclass can directly access inherited state and behavior, but a delegate is a separate object and cannot do so. If moved behavior still needs the host’s data, such as a show, date, or peak-day calculation, the delegate needs a controlled reference back to the host. This is sometimes called a host reference. The relationship should be kept explicit and narrow: the delegate should use only the host capabilities it requires. A back-reference creates a two-way object relationship, so initialization and lifecycle should be designed carefully to avoid cycles that complicate ownership or memory management.
Study instruction:
- List all host data used by a method before moving it and decide which accessors the delegate should receive through its back-reference.
Key point:
- A delegate does not inherit the host’s fields or methods automatically.
- Use a narrow back-reference to preserve required host collaboration.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
36. Q: How should a subclass override that calls super be moved to a delegate?
A: Show answer
A delegate cannot simply call the host’s overridden method to obtain the base behavior, because the host method may dispatch back to the delegate and recurse. Extract the superclass’s original calculation into a separate private method or redesign the delegate operation as an extension that accepts the base result. The host can calculate the base value and pass it to the delegate, which adds the specialized adjustment. Both approaches preserve the shared part without creating an infinite dispatch cycle.
Study instruction:
- Refactor a subclass override that calls super into a base calculation plus delegate extension and test both default and specialized results.
Key point:
- Delegation changes the dispatch relationship, so super calls need explicit redesign.
- Separate base calculation from delegated extension to avoid recursion.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
37. Q: How can a whole subclass hierarchy be replaced with delegate objects?
A: Show answer
Work through the subclasses incrementally, starting with the simplest variation. Create a delegate class, select and initialize the correct delegate from the type information, and move that subclass’s behavior to the delegate. Update the host to dispatch to the delegate or provide default behavior when no specialization exists. Repeat for the other variants. When several delegates share data or defaults, extract a superclass for the delegates themselves. This leaves the host hierarchy available for another variation while keeping species-specific or strategy-specific inheritance narrowly scoped.
Study instruction:
- Replace one simple subclass first, then add each remaining delegate and move its behavior with regression tests after every variant.
Key point:
- Migrate an entire hierarchy one subtype at a time.
- Delegate hierarchies can still use inheritance internally for shared variation behavior.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
38. Q: What are the disadvantages of Replace Subclass with Delegate?
A: Show answer
The refactoring does not necessarily improve code by itself. It can add host fields, delegates, back-references, forwarding methods, and runtime conditionals that were unnecessary in a simple hierarchy. Inheritance often expresses a clear base case and differences with less code. Delegation becomes worthwhile when the class must support another inheritance dimension, dynamic category changes, or reduced coupling between modules or teams. Compare the benefit with the complexity of maintaining the new object graph and dispatch rules.
Study instruction:
- Estimate the new fields, forwarding methods, and dispatch conditions before converting a subclass; document the concrete problem the conversion solves.
Key point:
- Delegation is not an automatic improvement over inheritance.
- Use it when dynamic variation or independent composition outweighs its added complexity.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
39. Q: What is Replace Superclass with Delegate?
A: Show answer
Replace Superclass with Delegate removes an inheritance relationship by making the former subclass hold an instance of the superclass and forwarding only the operations it actually needs. This is useful when the subclass inherits methods that do not make sense for it, or when the relationship is not a valid is-a relationship. A stack should not inherit every list operation simply to reuse storage; it can contain a list and expose only push and pop behavior. Delegation makes the distinction between the two concepts explicit.
Study instruction:
- Find a subclass that exposes superclass methods clients should not call and redesign it with a contained collaborator.
Key point:
- Delegate to reuse behavior without inheriting an inappropriate public interface.
- A composition relationship can express has-a more accurately than is-a.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
40. Q: How does the Liskov-style substitutability test help identify bad inheritance?
A: Show answer
Ask whether every instance of the proposed subclass can safely be used wherever the superclass is expected, including through the superclass’s complete public interface. The subclass should honor the parent’s behavioral promises, not merely share its implementation. A stack that inherits from a list may expose arbitrary insertion or removal operations that violate stack expectations. A car model class is not automatically a physical car just because both share name and engine-size fields. If substitutability fails, composition or delegation is usually a clearer model.
Study instruction:
- List the superclass operations and verify that the proposed subclass can honor each operation’s meaning and invariants.
Key point:
- A subtype must be valid wherever its supertype is expected.
- Shared data or implementation does not prove a valid subtype relationship.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
41. Q: Why can Replace Superclass with Delegate reduce coupling?
A: Show answer
A subclass is tightly coupled to superclass implementation and lifecycle rules. Changes to the parent can unintentionally break inherited behavior, and the subclass is exposed to the parent’s public interface even when many operations are not relevant. A delegate creates a separate object boundary with a limited set of forwarded methods. The host can expose only the capabilities it needs and can change its internal collaborator without claiming the same type relationship. The price is explicit forwarding code and the need to manage the delegate’s lifecycle.
Study instruction:
- Compare the superclass API with the methods the subclass actually needs; design a delegate interface containing only those operations.
Key point:
- Delegation narrows the reused interface and reduces inheritance coupling.
- Forwarding methods are the cost of a more explicit composition boundary.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
42. Q: What are the mechanics for Replace Superclass with Delegate?
A: Show answer
Add a field to the subclass that refers to a new instance of the former superclass and initialize it with the required data. For each inherited feature used by the subclass, add a forwarding method or property that delegates to the contained object. Test related method groups as the forwarding interface grows; getter and setter pairs should be migrated together. When all required superclass members have been replaced by forwarders, remove the inheritance link. Then review which additional data should belong to the host and which to the delegate.
Study instruction:
- Introduce the delegate field, forward one coherent group of methods, test, and remove inheritance only when forwarding coverage is complete.
Key point:
- Build the replacement interface before removing the parent relationship.
- Forward only the superclass capabilities the host should expose.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
43. Q: How does the scroll and catalog example illustrate an incorrect superclass relationship?
A: Show answer
A physical scroll and a catalog item describing a work are related but are not the same entity. Several physical copies may share one catalog entry, whereas a scroll has its own cleaning date and physical identity. Inheriting from CatalogItem makes the scroll appear to be a catalog item and duplicates catalog data across physical copies. Replacing the superclass with a delegate allows each scroll to reference the correct catalog item. A repository can then share a single catalog-item object among several scrolls, making identity and update behavior accurate.
Study instruction:
- Model a work’s catalog record and several physical copies; identify which fields belong to the shared record and which belong to each copy.
Key point:
- Separate conceptual identity from physical instances.
- Delegation can expose the correct ownership and sharing structure.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
44. Q: What extra modeling step may follow Replace Superclass with Delegate when data is shared?
A: Show answer
After delegation, each host may initially contain its own copy of the former superclass object. If several hosts should refer to the same logical catalog item, use Change Value to Reference and introduce a repository that retrieves the shared object by identity. The host then keeps its own identifier and points to the shared catalog record. This requires distinguishing the physical instance’s ID from the catalog item’s ID and updating constructors and loading code accordingly. Delegation creates the composition boundary; value-to-reference conversion establishes correct shared identity.
Study instruction:
- Identify repeated catalog-like objects and design a repository lookup using the shared record’s stable identifier.
Key point:
- Delegation changes the relationship; shared-reference modeling may be a separate follow-up.
- Keep host identity distinct from the identity of its delegate.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
45. Q: How do Replace Subclass with Delegate and Replace Superclass with Delegate differ?
A: Show answer
Replace Subclass with Delegate moves specialized behavior from a child class into a collaborator owned by its former superclass. It preserves the host’s base type and allows specialization to be composed or changed. Replace Superclass with Delegate removes a parent relationship because the child should not claim to be a subtype; the former parent becomes a contained service and only required features are forwarded. The first changes where variant behavior lives; the second changes an invalid or overly broad type relationship into composition.
Study instruction:
- For a class hierarchy, decide whether the problem is subclass variation that must become dynamic or a superclass interface that is semantically inappropriate.
Key point:
- Subclass delegation replaces an inherited variation with a collaborator.
- Superclass delegation replaces an is-a claim with has-a composition.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
46. Q: How should default behavior be represented in a delegate hierarchy?
A: Show answer
A host may need a default behavior when no specialized delegate exists, or each delegate may share a default implementation. Repeating the same default method in every concrete delegate creates duplication. Extracting a delegate superclass can centralize common fields, back-references, and default behavior, while specialized delegates override only what differs. If a default delegate is always present, the host’s forwarding methods become simpler because they no longer need null checks. This pattern should be used where the common default is meaningful and not merely to add another unnecessary class.
Study instruction:
- Find repeated default methods among delegates and decide whether an always-present base delegate can own them.
Key point:
- Centralize shared delegate behavior where it genuinely applies.
- An always-present default delegate can simplify host dispatch but adds an object.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
47. Q: Why is explicit instanceof checking usually a poor substitute for polymorphic dispatch?
A: Show answer
A client that asks whether an object is a specific concrete class embeds knowledge of the implementation hierarchy. Adding or changing variants then requires editing these checks across the system. Named queries or polymorphic methods let the object answer questions in domain terms without exposing its implementation class. During a refactoring that removes subclasses, such queries can be moved into the superclass and changed to use a field or delegate. Explicit class checks may occasionally be necessary at infrastructure boundaries, but they should not replace normal behavioral dispatch.
Study instruction:
- Search for instanceof or concrete-class comparisons and identify which can become domain queries or polymorphic methods.
Key point:
- Concrete type checks spread hierarchy knowledge into clients.
- Prefer behavior or domain queries over implementation-class tests.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Remove Subclass
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
48. Q: How should inheritance refactoring be tested?
A: Show answer
Tests should cover every subclass and inherited operation, constructor path, type-code mapping, default case, and client that depends on the public hierarchy. For pull-ups, ensure all variants preserve the same behavior and that no subclass-only dependency was lost. For push-downs, verify that callers can still access the correct subtype. For type-code replacement, test each code and the unknown-code path. For delegation, test both default and delegated behavior, back-reference use, and any lifecycle transition. Run tests after each small move, and use static analysis where available.
Study instruction:
- Create a coverage matrix mapping each hierarchy transformation to subclasses, constructors, public methods, and caller assumptions that require regression tests.
Key point:
- Inheritance changes affect dispatch as well as data and methods.
- Test every variant, fallback, and client-visible contract during migration.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 12: Dealing with Inheritance
49. Q: How can constructor and factory boundaries reduce risk during hierarchy changes?
A: Show answer
Direct construction exposes concrete subclasses throughout a codebase. Introducing factory functions first centralizes the mapping from input data to object type. Later, subclasses can be replaced with fields or delegates without requiring every client to change its construction expression. Factories are especially valuable when type codes arrive from an external source, because selector logic can validate and normalize those values. The factory should define unknown-value behavior explicitly and should not silently create a generic object when invalid input represents a data error.
Study instruction:
- Find direct subclass construction calls and move type selection into one tested factory before changing the class representation.
Key point:
- Factories isolate clients from hierarchy implementation details.
- Explicitly handle unknown or invalid type codes at the creation boundary.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
- Martin Fowler — Remove Subclass
50. Q: What mistakes commonly occur when pulling methods or fields up the hierarchy?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include assuming same names mean same meaning, ignoring different units or invariants in fields, moving code that calls subclass-only features, and deleting a method copy before checking every caller and behavior. Constructors add ordering constraints, while different method signatures may need normalization first. Dynamic languages can hide missing superclass contracts until runtime. To avoid these errors, inspect all uses, compare semantics, add explicit abstract or trap methods where useful, and move one element at a time with tests.
Study instruction:
- Audit a proposed pull-up for semantic equivalence, visibility, subclass-specific dependencies, constructor order, and call-site coverage.
Key point:
- A successful pull-up depends on a true shared contract.
- Static checks and incremental tests expose hidden dependencies.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Field
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Constructor Body
51. Q: How should a team choose between Remove Subclass and Replace Type Code with Subclasses?
A: Show answer
These transformations move in opposite directions. Replace Type Code with Subclasses creates classes when a type code represents stable behavior or data differences that benefit from polymorphism. Remove Subclass eliminates classes when those differences have become small enough to represent as a field. The decision depends on whether the subclass contains meaningful variant-specific operations or state, whether clients use it polymorphically, and whether adding or removing variants is likely. Neither a class nor a type code is inherently superior; the representation should match the complexity and stability of the variation.
Study instruction:
- Compare a type field and its subclass alternatives; list the behavior and state each representation would own.
Key point:
- Use subclasses for meaningful stable variation that benefits from polymorphism.
- Use a field when the class distinction no longer carries enough behavior to justify itself.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Type Code with Subclasses
- Martin Fowler — Remove Subclass
52. Q: How should a team decide between Collapse Hierarchy and Extract Superclass?
A: Show answer
Extract Superclass introduces a common parent when separate classes share data or behavior that should be unified. Collapse Hierarchy removes a parent-child distinction when the two classes have become too similar to justify separate levels. One increases shared structure across classes; the other reduces unnecessary structure within a hierarchy. Inspect the direction of the problem: duplicated common features in sibling classes suggest extraction, while a redundant parent and child suggest collapse. Test naming, constructors, and client expectations before deciding which class should survive.
Study instruction:
- Draw the before-and-after class relationships and identify whether the primary issue is duplication across siblings or redundancy between parent and child.
Key point:
- Extract a common parent to unify sibling classes.
- Collapse adjacent levels when their distinction no longer communicates useful variation.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
- Martin Fowler — Collapse Hierarchy
53. Q: What is the danger of using inheritance only to reuse implementation?
A: Show answer
Implementation reuse can tempt developers to make a class inherit from another class whose domain meaning is unrelated. The child then inherits operations it should not expose and may violate the parent’s invariants. This can create misleading APIs and subtle bugs even when the reused fields and methods initially appear convenient. A delegate or component is safer when the relationship is has-a rather than is-a. Evaluate the semantics of every inherited operation, not only the few methods the child originally wanted to reuse.
Study instruction:
- Take a class that inherits for code reuse and list inherited methods that have no valid meaning for the child.
Key point:
- Implementation reuse does not guarantee a valid subtype relationship.
- Composition prevents unrelated abstractions from leaking into the child’s public API.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
54. Q: How does a hierarchy of species delegates preserve specialization while freeing the host hierarchy?
A: Show answer
A host such as Bird can contain a species delegate selected from input type data. Each delegate implements the behavior specific to one species, while a common delegate superclass provides shared fields, back-references, and defaults. The host can then remain free to participate in another hierarchy, such as wild versus captive birds. Delegates may still use inheritance among themselves because their specialization axis is now narrowly scoped. This demonstrates that composition and inheritance can be combined rather than treated as mutually exclusive alternatives.
Study instruction:
- Design a host with two independent variation dimensions and place species-specific behavior in a focused delegate hierarchy.
Key point:
- Delegation frees the host’s inheritance slot for another dimension.
- Inheritance can still be useful within the delegate family itself.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
55. Q: What does the “favor composition over inheritance” principle actually imply?
A: Show answer
The principle warns against using inheritance indiscriminately, especially when developers use it merely to reuse code or create an invalid subtype. It does not mean inheritance is always harmful. A well-designed hierarchy can make a stable base case and its variations simpler than a set of delegates. Composition is preferable when behavior must vary across independent dimensions, change dynamically, or remain loosely coupled. A judicious mixture allows inheritance where substitutability and shared behavior are sound, and delegation where those conditions do not hold.
Study instruction:
- For one existing hierarchy, record the benefits of inheritance and the specific conditions that would justify replacing it with delegates.
Key point:
- Composition is an alternative when inheritance creates coupling or consumes a needed variation axis.
- Choose a judicious mixture rather than applying a blanket rule.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
- Martin Fowler — Replace Superclass with Delegate
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 12: Dealing with Inheritance
56. Q: What criteria help decide where a field or method belongs in a class hierarchy?
A: Show answer
Ask which classes need the feature, whether they use it with the same meaning, and whether callers can rely on it through the relevant static type. A feature used uniformly across siblings may move upward. A feature needed by only one variant may move downward. A feature whose behavior differs by type may be overridden or delegated. A feature that merely supports a subset of one class’s responsibilities may belong in a separate collaborator. These criteria expose the real ownership of behavior and help avoid both bloated superclasses and duplicated subclasses.
Study instruction:
- For each candidate member, record its users, semantic meaning, caller types, and dependence on other members.
Key point:
- Feature placement follows ownership, usage, and contract.
- The hierarchy should expose only capabilities valid for its types.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Pull Up Method
- Martin Fowler — Push Down Method
- Martin Fowler — Extract Superclass
57. Q: What should happen to subclass-specific data when replacing a subclass with a delegate?
A: Show answer
Data used only by the former subclass should move into the delegate, while the host retains state that is common to all instances. If the delegate needs host information, give it a back-reference or pass the necessary values through a narrow interface. During migration, constructors or factory functions initialize the delegate with its specialized data. The new boundary should avoid copying host state unnecessarily, while still making ownership and mutability explicit. Shared data may sometimes be moved into a delegate superclass if several specialized delegates use it.
Study instruction:
- Partition the original subclass fields into host-common state, delegate-specific state, and shared delegate state before moving methods.
Key point:
- Place variant-specific state with the delegate that owns its behavior.
- Keep host state common and expose only required host capabilities.
Source links:
- Martin Fowler — Replace Subclass with Delegate
58. Q: How can a safe incremental workflow reduce risk across all inheritance refactorings?
A: Show answer
Start with tests that characterize current behavior and a member-by-member inventory of the hierarchy. Introduce new classes, factories, fields, or delegate references before deleting existing structures. Move or copy one feature at a time, keep temporary forwarding methods where needed, and test after each stage. Only after all callers use the new abstraction should the old subclass, field, or inheritance link be removed. Then remove compatibility code and rerun the complete test suite. This approach makes the migration observable and localizes failures.
Study instruction:
- Write a sequence of small commits for an inheritance change, specifying what remains compatible and which tests run at each checkpoint.
Key point:
- Introduce the replacement structure before removing the old one.
- Incremental migration, forwarding interfaces, and tests make complex hierarchy changes manageable.
Source links:
- Refactoring, 2nd Edition — Chapter 12: Dealing with Inheritance
59. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 12, Dealing with Inheritance?
A: Show answer
Inheritance should express real commonality and substitutability, not merely provide a convenient source of implementation. Pull Up Method, Pull Up Field, and Pull Up Constructor Body centralize shared features; Push Down Method and Push Down Field localize specialized features. Replace Type Code with Subclasses introduces polymorphic variants, while Remove Subclass and Collapse Hierarchy remove class distinctions that no longer help. Extract Superclass unifies shared classes. Replace Subclass with Delegate frees a host from a subclass variation, and Replace Superclass with Delegate replaces a misleading is-a relationship with composition. The appropriate structure depends on behavior, lifecycle, coupling, and the axes of variation in the domain.
Study instruction:
- Summarize each of the eleven techniques with its motivating smell, direction of change, main trade-off, and regression checks.
Key point:
- Use inheritance where it gives a valid and useful subtype contract.
- Use delegation when independent variation or semantic correctness requires composition.
